new Lotus: Learning to Understand Time-series by Learning to Forecast

Authors: Van Dai Do, Huu Hiep Nguyen, Minh Hoang Nguyen, Manh Nguyen, Hung Le

Abstract: Time-series language models typically align numerical signals with language using labels, captions, or question-answer pairs. Although such supervision encourages linguistic representations, it does not explicitly require them to preserve information about how the underlying time-series signal evolves. We introduce forecast grounding, a pretraining principle that requires language-aligned time-series representations to remain predictive of the future evolution before they are adapted to downstream tasks. We instantiate this principle in LOTUS, which divides each time-series channel into non-overlapping patches and maps them into the LLM embedding space. It then predicts the next patch from the LLM hidden states of the time-series tokens, encouraging the learned representation to retain temporal information. To capture both temporal changes and periodic patterns, we also propose a temporal-spectral patch representation that combines each raw time-series patch with its spectral representation before mapping it into the LLM embedding space. Across three language model backbones and six downstream evaluation datasets, LOTUS achieves the best average performance with minimal trainable parameters, reaching 59.91% accuracy and outperforming the strongest matched baseline by 3.36%. Our representation analyses further show that LOTUS uses the numerical input and preserves temporal structure in its learned patch representations. These results establish forecast grounding as a promising pretraining principle, enabling compact time-series language models to outperform models 25 to 50 times their size on TSRBench.

new Inverse Reasoning Models for Efficient Post-Training and Verification from Answer-Only Data

Authors: Eric O. Korman

Abstract: Post-training reasoning LLMs often requires making a tradeoff between costly supervision and compute inefficiency: supervised finetuning typically requires reasoning traces, which is data inefficient since in many applications one only has prompt-answer tuples and lacks the groundtruth chain-of-thought (CoT) sequence. On the other hand, reinforcement learning (with verifiable rewards, for example) can operate without CoT data but it is much more compute-inefficient since it requires multiple autoregressive completions per optimization step, none of which are guaranteed to give a non-zero policy gradient. In this work we explore a middleground, a self-distillation technique that uses answer-only data, treating CoT as latent variables. This relies on training a partner ``inverse reasoning LLM" that generates the original's reasoning traces conditioned on the answer. The inverse model is trained once and reused across incoming batches of answer-only data, with all generation happening offline and up front, making the approach particularly suited to continual post-training. Using Qwen3-0.6B on the Countdown and GSM8K tasks, we show that after GRPO training has saturated, a single offline distillation round from the inverse model roughly doubles pass@1 on hard prompts and, on Countdown, outperforms rejection-sampling self-distillation on every hard-tail metric at every mix ratio (with smaller gains on GSM8K); rejection sampling by construction has no signal on problems the model never solves on its own. Continued GRPO on the fresh data retains an edge at hard pass@1 but trails distillation at pass@8 and pass@32 while regenerating rollouts at every step. As an added bonus we see that the inverse model has a second application as an accurate verifier of correctness. When used for best-of-$n$ selection at test time, it raises selection accuracy on Countdown from 0.753 (majority voting) to 0.969.

new Do Vision CIL Methods Transfer to Protein? A Diagnostic Benchmark of Frozen Protein Encoders

Authors: Ziyuan Gao

Abstract: Protein function annotation operates over an expanding label space, making class-incremental learning (CIL) a natural framework for incorporating newly discovered functions without full retraining. Yet existing CIL methods have been developed largely in vision and have not been systematically evaluated over frozen protein representations. We conduct a controlled benchmark of nine representative CIL methods across five sequence-, structure-, and sequence--structure-based protein encoders and two enzyme function datasets at different scales, while keeping the encoders frozen and evaluation settings consistent across representations. We find that no method transfers reliably across encoders, and that these failures persist across alternative phase constructions. Diagnostic analyses reveal that this instability arises from weak gradient-based stability signals, substantial heterogeneity in embedding geometry, and covariance assumptions whose appropriate capacity changes with both dataset scale and encoder. These findings suggest that the representation itself should be treated as a central component of continual-learning design rather than a passive feature extractor. More generally, reliable protein CIL will require encoder-aware methods that adapt their statistical assumptions and capacity to the geometry of the representation on which they operate. Code is available at https://github.com/zygao930/ProteinCIL.

URLs: https://github.com/zygao930/ProteinCIL.

new Do Perturbation Predictors Learn Gene Interactions? Diagnosing Extrapolation in Agentic Screening

Authors: Ziyuan Gao

Abstract: High-throughput perturbation screens increasingly use machine-learning models to predict the effects of gene combinations that were never measured. A difficult case arises when both genes in a test pair were seen individually during training, but their combination was not. In this setting, standard OOD methods often fail because neither gene appears unfamiliar on its own. We therefore compare each model prediction with a simple additive prediction obtained by summing the two observed single-gene effects. The difference between them, which we call additive disagreement (AD), measures how much extra interaction effect the model introduces for an unseen combination. Across eight prediction methods and three Perturb-seq datasets, specialised neural predictors do not consistently outperform this simple additive baseline. More importantly, predictions with larger deviations from the additive baseline tend to have larger errors. Conventional output-based and embedding-based confidence signals are inconsistent across datasets and models, whereas AD remains informative across all evaluated compositional settings. We further study how this signal can guide decisions in an agentic perturbation-screening workflow. The code is available at https://github.com/zygao930/OOD.

URLs: https://github.com/zygao930/OOD.

new Token Utility for On-Policy Distillation via Local Trust-Region Optimization

Authors: Narayanan Sadagopan, Yilan Chen, Vijay Lingam

Abstract: Token-selection methods for on-policy distillation (OPD) prioritize student-visited positions using diverse signals, including uncertainty, teacher-student disagreement, and mismatch direction. We instead derive token importance from the distillation objective itself: how much can each position's loss decrease under an equal local KL budget on the student's predictive distribution? The answer is the utility $s_t=g_t^\top F_t^\dagger g_t$, where $g_t$ is the logit gradient and $F_t$ is the categorical Fisher matrix. For reverse-KL it equals the student-weighted variance of the student-teacher log-probability ratio, and for forward-KL it equals the Pearson $\chi^2$ divergence, so the appropriate utility follows from the choice of objective. On shared training checkpoints, several independently motivated criteria, including student entropy, KL disagreement, and TIP, concentrate their importance on nearly the same positions as the derived utility at a 50\% selection fraction, while diverging at stricter fractions and for direction-specific criteria. We instantiate the utility as Fisher Utility Weighting (FUW), a continuous, mean-normalized token weighting. Across seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks and three Qwen3 student-teacher pairs, FUW improves over matched uniform OPD under both reverse-KL (1.35-4.0 macro points) and forward-KL (1.08-2.71 macro points), and is competitive with existing selective methods using a single closed-form score derived from the training objective.

new PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization

Authors: Gor Simonyan, Tatevik Abrahamyan, Narek Abelyan, Tigran Fahradyan, Hrant Khachatrian

Abstract: The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.

new Data Density Scaling Laws for Image Self-Distillation

Authors: Alvard Barseghyan, Ani Vanyan, Hakob Tamazyan, Hrant Khachatrian

Abstract: Current scaling law formulations suggest that increasing the number of unique samples in a dataset improves model performance, while repeated exposure to the same samples provides diminishing value. In this work, we examine this assumption for image self-distillation models. Through systematic experiments across diverse datasets, namely Maxar, SATLAS, Waymo, nuPlan, Ego4D, ImageNet, and YFCC15M, we demonstrate that the effects of sample repetition on downstream performance are non-trivial and dataset-dependent. We identify settings where repeatedly training on a smaller random subset can outperform training on a larger set of unique samples. This finding highlights a limitation of existing scaling laws. To address this gap, we propose a modified version of a Chinchilla-style scaling law that explicitly accounts for two factors: the number of unique samples and repetition per sample. This formulation provides a framework for understanding data-compute trade-offs and improves prediction in some regimes where repetition plays a central role, although its benefits are not consistent across datasets. Finally, we examine how subset selection and augmentation affect downstream performance.

new Scaling Cheap Synthetic Pretraining Improves Indoor Radio-Map Prediction

Authors: Khoren Petrosyan, Artashes Mkrtchyan, Rafayel Mkrtchyan, Hrant Khachatrian, Theofanis P. Raptis

Abstract: Real measurements are too limited to train an accurate indoor pathloss predictor that works across a wide range of buildings. Large-scale synthetic pretraining offers a way forward, but real building layouts are also scarce, and accurate ray-tracing simulators are slow, expensive, and often proprietary. We address both problems by introducing a generator that automatically creates diverse indoor layouts and a fast, open-source approximate ray tracer that computes radio maps for them. Using this pipeline, we generate 128M synthetic samples, pretrain ResNet-101 and Swin-T encoder-decoders, and fine-tune them on high-quality benchmark data. Despite simulator mismatch, our ResNet-101 model reduces error by 15% on average relative to the best known methods across five indoor radio-map prediction tasks and improves transfer on a small real-measurement dataset. Both architectures show predictable data-scaling trends: power laws fitted on 2M-16M samples predict the mean normalized downstream RMSE at 32M-128M with 3.33% mean absolute percentage error for ResNet-101 and 7.10% for Swin-T. These results show that pretraining on large amounts of cheap simulated data can improve indoor radio-map prediction, even when real measurements and high-quality simulations are limited.

new IR-PRM+ with Previous-Utility Prediction: Constant Regret and Best-Iterate Convergence

Authors: Michael Sullins, Ian A. Kash

Abstract: Regret matching (and its variants) and counterfactual regret minimization are central algorithms in practice for normal-form and extensive-form game solving. Yet these have historically lacked the stronger theoretical convergence guarantees available to optimistic and extra-gradient methods. Recent work introduced IREG-PRM+, a regret matching variant which does have strong theoretical convergence guarantees. However, it relies on a simultaneous, extra-gradient update. IREG-PRM+ is an instance of the more general IR-PRM framework, where empirically optimistic and alternating updates often have better performance. However, the prior extra-gradient analysis does not extend to these cases. We introduce a new approach that covers both settings. For simultaneous and alternating IR-PRM+ in finite two-player zero-sum matrix games, previous-utility prediction recovers the constant-regret and convergence-rate guarantees previously obtained with extra-gradient prediction: external regret bounded independently of T, an O(T^{-1}) average duality gap, and an O(T^{-1/2}) best-current-iterate gap. Moreover, the current duality gap vanishes and the current profile converges to the Nash equilibrium set. The key step bounds the component of consecutive strategy movement not controlled by the existing IR-PRM+ regret inequality using the relative growth of the nondecreasing regret-vector norm rho_t. Coupling this bound across the two players yields recurrences whose effective coefficient scales as 1/(rho_t^X rho_t^Y), preventing unbounded norm growth. We extend the analysis to finitely many simplex learners under a weighted Minty condition and, through the corresponding CFR variant IR-PCFR+, recover analogous guarantees for finite two-player zero-sum perfect-recall extensive-form games under the same full-reach condition as prior work.

new How Many Ranks Does a Token Need? A Noise-Floor Squelch for Rank-One LoRA Experts

Authors: Mei Okonkwo, Owen Castellanos, Ingrid Solberg, Yusuf Demirel

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) applies every rank-one component of its update to every token, and mixture-of-experts variants replace this with a learned router that activates a fixed number of components. We argue that the number of components a token needs is a detection question with a classical answer. A token's responses to the rank-one components of a trained LoRA consist of a few large values on a Gaussian noise floor whose width matches that of an untrained adapter, and the floor carries cross-talk rather than adaptation. We introduce SQUELCH, a router-free mixture of rank-one experts that (i) soft-thresholds each token's responses at a multiple of its own robust noise level, so the active rank varies per token; (ii) chooses that multiple for each module by Stein's unbiased risk estimate, so no rank budget is specified; and (iii) replaces the resulting marginal keep-or-drop decisions by one explaining-away step on the value Gram matrix, which suppresses redundant co-active components and recruits complementary ones. SQUELCH adds no trainable parameters to LoRA. We prove that the squelch is within a logarithmic factor of the oracle input-dependent allocation, that the selected threshold is risk-consistent, and that explaining away is a descent step toward the joint sparse code whose error is controlled by the coherence of co-active components. On commonsense reasoning, knowledge, math, code, and GLUE with LLaMA-3.1-8B, Qwen3, and DeBERTaV3, SQUELCH improves over LoRA by 2.2--3.2 points and over the strongest mixture baseline by 0.8--1.2 points while activating 8.6 of 32 components per token on average.

new Who Should We Listen to More? Welfare-Aware Preference Acquisition for Pluralistic Alignment

Authors: Ruomeng Ding, Tianwei Gao, Lianrui Geng, Mohit Bansal, Zhun Deng

Abstract: Pluralistic alignment needs to accommodate diverse preferences under limited human feedback. Using AI simulators to label preference comparisons reduces annotation costs, but variation in simulation accuracy across individuals can leave some people's preferences poorly represented in training. We study how to allocate human feedback across people to construct training data for pluralistic alignment, and how this allocation affects the trained policy. To make this allocation explicit and controllable, we define a social welfare objective over individual supervision utilities that measure label quality. Each query's marginal welfare gain depends on its expected supervision improvement, the recipient's current utility, and application-designated priority. Welfare-Aware Preference Acquisition (WAPA) uses auxiliary reference models to estimate query gains and individual utilities, then selects queries by estimated marginal welfare gain while updating recipient utilities. We prove exact optimality of the acquisition algorithm for the estimated welfare objective under a fixed query budget and model predictions. Experiments on six Roleplay collections and four OpinionQA regions demonstrate improved supervision and controllable allocation tradeoffs. Policy training on four Roleplay collections improves preference prediction for both seen and unseen individuals. When prioritizing a designated group and poorly represented individuals, acquiring human labels for 20% of comparisons recovers 75-86% of the average predictive utility gap between policies trained entirely on AI-generated labels and entirely on ground-truth labels.

new Lag: A Mean-Field Optimal Policy for Online Assignment with Reusable Resources

Authors: Keerthana Gurushankar

Abstract: We study online task assignment with reusable resources. This problem arises in peer reviewer assignment, skill-based call routing, rideshare matching, emergency dispatch, and crowdsourcing: Tasks arrive according to a geometric process and must be immediately assigned to servers that become temporarily busy, yielding rewards that depend on the task-server pair. The goal is to maximize long-run average reward. We cast the problem as a contextual restless multi-armed bandit (CRMAB). We derive an occupancy-measure LP (OCC-LP) that upper-bounds the reward of any work-conserving policy. From its dual we obtain Lag, a closed-form index policy. We introduce a scaling regime in which the number of copies of each server and the number of arrivals per step grow together. This regime yields the first deterministic fluid limit for a contextual bandit. In it, Lag is asymptotically optimal. Finally, we evaluate our algorithms on peer review datasets and call center routing. The Lagrangian index achieves 83-99% of the LP upper bound across all load levels. Moreover, the Lagrangian index policy significantly outperforms the natural greedy assignment scheme, improving reward by up to 12.3% on journal data and 36% on structured synthetic instances.

new Tracing Morphology, Semantics, and Divergence in Language Acquisition

Authors: Forough Shirin Abkenar, Haoshuo Fu, Liang Feng, Ning Yan, Masood S. Mortazavi

Abstract: Understanding the dynamics of language acquisition in Large Language Models (LLMs) is crucial for uncovering how statistical representations evolve into structured linguistic capabilities. However, most existing studies focus primarily on fully trained models, leaving the developmental trajectory across intermediate training stages largely underexplored. In this work, we leverage intermediate checkpoints from open-source models (e.g., Pythia and OLMo) under teacher-forcing evaluation to systematically investigate language acquisition across training steps and model scales. We focus on four core characteristics: morphological structure acquisition, embedding drift, repetition dominance, and co-occurrence effects. Our findings reveal that models acquire morphological structures well before semantic embeddings stabilize. Furthermore, we show that training dataset size has a positive impact on divergence recognition, while co-occurrence during pre-training and repetition during inference have a negative impact on divergence recognition and associative link generation.

new Loss Curvature as a Sampling-Free Proxy for Memorization in Diffusion Models

Authors: Shashank Sule, Xinyue Yu, Boonam Shin, Yuxuan Liu, Abolfazl Hashemi, Hayden Schaeffer

Abstract: Understanding the mechanism behind why diffusion models memorize their training data remains an open question in generative modeling. In this paper, we examine this question from two perspectives: (a) the model-level perspective concerning the onset of memorization in a model during training, and (b) the sample-level perspective concerning the proclivity of an individual sample to be memorized by a given model. We show that the so-called input loss curvature, or the Frobenius norm of the Hessian of the diffusion model's training loss, is an effective metric for quantifying both perspectives on memorization. In particular, we empirically demonstrate that input curvatures averaged across training and validation samples can identify when a model is likely to start regurgitating its training data when tracked over training time. However, this metric is not sufficient for measuring an individual sample's likelihood of being memorized. To address this perspective, we introduce a counterfactual definition of memorization in diffusion models, extending the analogous notion from supervised learning proposed by [Feldman, 2019]. We prove that this counterfactual memorization is controlled by the input curvature of the leave-one-out model, thus establishing a direct theoretical link between a sample's local loss landscape and its generation likelihood. We validate our derived bounds through several empirical experiments and show that input-dependent curvature provides a proxy for memorization.

new Graph Coarsening Enables Escaping Local Optima in Compute-Constrained Nonconvex MAX-CUT

Authors: M. Harper Langston, Pierre-David Letourneau, Dalton Jones, Harris Teague, Peter Scott, Mingu Lee, Richard Lethin, Chris Lott, Roland Memisevic, Fatih Porikli

Abstract: Because its binary optimization landscape contains exponentially many local optima, MAX-CUT is highly sensitive to initialization. We study Particle Polynomial Relaxation (PPR), a moment-based nonconvex continuous reformulation possessing an advantageous optimization landscape. We warm-start PPR with Combinatorial Multigrid (CMG), a spectral graph-coarsening method that produces structure-aware initial solutions in near-linear time without training data. On \texttt{t2g20} ($n=400$), benchmark instance with known exact optimum, the combined CMG+PPR pipeline reaches $97.5\%$ of optimum on average and $98.4\%$ on the best run. An extended variant with discrete local-search refinement reaches the published optimum on all sixty Biq Mac instances evaluated by a recent neural branch-and-bound method. Using a sparse CSR representation, the same approach scales to G-set instances with up to $20{,}000$ vertices reducing memory requirements by up to $3333\times$ while maintaining a consistent improvement over the CMG warm start. Gains are largest on sparse weighted graphs; on dense graphs we complement the pipeline with spectral sparsification.

new What Information Buys in Active Inference

Authors: Melih Kandemir, Mustafa Mert \c{C}elikok

Abstract: Information-theoretic exploration is of primary interest in methodological studies of reinforcement learning (RL) and computational cognitive neuroscience, while the established RL theory builds regret guarantees on confidence sets. This situation leaves open the conditions under which the information-driven exploration found in nature is more advantageous than the exploration of the established RL theory. We put the state-of-the-art methods of these two paradigms, Active Inference (AIF) and Bayes-UCBVI, on an equal footing: both act through the same state abstraction of a tabular MDP with $S$ states, and their greedy forms differ only in the exploration term. We find that AIF's information gain decays too fast to serve as a confidence width, locking greedy agents that explore by it out of the optimal action. Calibrating the information gain by a square root yields calibrated AIF, which attains regret of order $\sqrt T$ for an exact abstraction, but no better guarantee than Bayes-UCBVI for a fixed reward. When an adversary chooses rewards from the agent's data, Bayes-UCBVI's value-specific widths lock out, whereas calibrated AIF pays a factor $\sqrt S$, which no goal-memoryless agent avoids on some MDP whose start states the adversary also chooses. Calibrated information gain thus acts as a reward-agnostic $\ell_1$ confidence set, preferable to value-specific widths when rewards are chosen from the agent's data, not when they are fixed.

new Deep Generative Models for Phylogenetic Inference with Complex Evolutionary Processes

Authors: Ethan Baron, Alan N. Amin, Andrew Gordon Wilson

Abstract: Inference of phylogenetic trees from sequences plays a central role in understanding evolutionary relationships, with applications ranging from tracking pathogen spread to reconstructing the history of life. Conventionally, practitioners obtain posteriors over trees using likelihood-based methods like MCMC. However, likelihoods are only available for simple evolutionary models with restrictive assumptions. For realistic evolutionary models, inference using conventional methods is prohibitively expensive, inaccurate, or even impossible. In practice, practitioners settle for using as flexible a conventional method as possible, even though they expect it is misspecified. In principle, we could use simulation-based inference: simulate tree and sequence data from an evolutionary model and train a neural network to predict the posterior over trees given sequences. However it is a mathematical challenge to represent and train complex posterior distributions over trees. Here we present a flexible family of conditional generative models over trees. Our models iteratively build trees using three natural paradigms -- top-down, middle-out, or bottom-up -- and are trained using a discrete diffusion framework. For real and synthetic datasets of up to $65$ taxa and $>10^5$ tokens, our models recover MCMC posteriors much more accurately than the previous state-of-the-art simulation-based method Phyloformer 2. Finally, we compare to IQ-Tree, a popular flexible-but-misspecified conventional method. Our models match its tree estimates on simple data, but on more realistic data, our method is reliably more accurate.

new How Inner Optimizers Shape Replica Scaling

Authors: Eugene Belilovsky, Stefan Horoi, Edouard Oyallon, Benjamin Th\'erien

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) across geographically distributed and poorly interconnected compute pools or data centers has become a topic of significant interest. This has led to increased study of local optimization methods such as DiLoco for LLMs, especially those that use local (or inner) adaptive or preconditioned optimizers, which are critical for high-performance LLM training. However, a central challenge remains: it is well known that increasing the number of model replicas (replica scaling) while fixing the total training budget leads to monotonically decreasing performance. Existing work has empirically demonstrated that the choice of inner optimizer (Muon v.s. AdamW) can significantly improve this trend, but offers only limited explanation of the performance difference. In this work, we consider a range of inner-optimizers and identify two important factors that impact replica scaling: (1) the covariance structure of the pseudo-gradients and (2) the impact of batch size on the pseudogradient's noise scale. Based on these insights, we theoretically and empirically demonstrate that whitening-based pre-conditioning is a powerful inner-optimizer desideratum. Our empirical evaluation extends the results to reveal that whitening-based inner-optimizers achieve strong replica scaling (for 8-96 replicas), with performance relative to DP improving as parameters and data are scaled.

new Theoretical and Practical Advances in Hierarchical Bayesian Deep Learning Methodology

Authors: Neil K. Chada, Simon Cotter, Kody J. H. Law, Xinzhu Liang

Abstract: Reliable uncertainty quantification remains an important challenge in AI. Hierarchical Bayesian methods provide a principled framework for this by representing unknown quantities through posterior distributions. Joint inference of neural-network parameters and prior hyperparameters is challenging for conventional Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods due to strong dependencies. In this work, we introduce the multilevel particle marginal Metropolis-Hastings (MLPMMH) method, which combines pseudo-marginal inference for the hyperparameters with coupled sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) across hierarchy of network-width truncations for conditional parameter inference. The hierarchy is induced by a trace-class neural network (TNN) prior with width-dependent decay, motivated empirically by the compressible structure observed in trained neural-network parameters. Under standard multilevel coupling assumptions, we establish that MLPMMH achieves mean-square error (MSE) $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^2)$ at expected computational cost $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-2})$, and verify the predicted rate on a Bayesian regression problem. To address larger-scale applications, we further introduce S-MLPMMH, a practical approximation that replaces the exact sampling components with a PCA-based hierarchy and scalable Bayesian Monte Carlo. Experiments on MNIST demonstrate competitive predictive performance and uncertainty quantification while exploiting the parallel structure of the approximation. Software has been made publicly available at https://github.com/submission-code-anon/mlpmmh-anon.

URLs: https://github.com/submission-code-anon/mlpmmh-anon.

new Backpropagating Verifier Rewards Through Continuous Flow Language Models

Authors: Xu Han, Jiajing Hu, Li-Ping Liu

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has substantially improved the reasoning of autoregressive and discrete-diffusion language models. Language models that generate continuous token embeddings are emerging as a strong alternative, but RL post-training for them remains understudied. We propose \method{}, a post-training framework for continuous flow language models. Such a model denoises token embeddings over multiple steps and decodes them into discrete tokens only at the last step. To obtain pathwise gradients, which backpropagate through continuous actions and have much lower variance than REINFORCE, we introduce a critic that estimates the value of intermediate token embeddings, bypassing the non-differentiable decoding step. The critic must also keep track of the value function as the policy changes; to this end, we design a critic that predicts a value from every token representation and improves policy optimization. Across four reasoning tasks, \method{} reliably improves a continuous flow language model through RL post-training and outperforms baseline methods of the same type.

new Beyond Aggregate Accuracy: Item-Level Transitions in Prompt Optimization

Authors: Chinmoy Mitra, Tarek Mahmud

Abstract: Automatic prompt optimization (APO) uses feedback from model responses to improve prompts automatically. However, its effectiveness is usually measured through overall scores, which do not reveal which questions become correct, which previously correct answers become wrong, or whether improvements extend to unseen questions. We introduce Reflective Feedback-Driven Prompt Optimization (RFDPO), a tool that tracks recoveries (incorrect to correct), regressions (correct to incorrect), and churn (all changed outcomes). RFDPO uses this feedback to guide subsequent revisions and records prompt changes throughout optimization. We evaluate RFDPO across five benchmarks covering law, knowledge, instruction following, mathematics, and privacy, using GPT-5 as the optimizer and GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1 Mini as solvers. Our results show that overall accuracy can hide substantial changes: in five of nine configurations, churn was at least five times the absolute net change, and a single-item net change concealed up to 17 changed outcomes. Average validation gains were 7.9 percentage points, compared with 5.2 points on held-out tests. In a controlled ablation on LegalBench-Hearsay, item-level feedback reduced churn by 40% ($p=0.024$) while maintaining similar net gains. These findings motivate reporting recovery, regression, churn, and performance on unseen data alongside overall accuracy.

new Contextual Credit Policy Optimization

Authors: Huiwen Han, Ziyao Zeng, Zihao Zhu, Sisong Bei, Hao Wang, Hongyu Xu, Yan Han, Degui Zhi, Zhiwen Fan

Abstract: Group-based reinforcement learning enables large language model agents to learn from sparse outcome rewards in long-horizon tasks. Recent methods derive step-level credit by comparing observation-matched experiences across trajectories. However, identical observations can occur in different contexts, and matched peers are not equally informative for a given decision. Moreover, favorable terminal outcomes can obscure redundant actions or detours within successful trajectories. To address these two challenges, we propose Contextual Credit Policy Optimization (CCPO). For the first, CCPO weights observation-matched peers from other trajectories by contextual similarity, computed from frozen LLM representations and accumulated trajectory statistics, allowing peers from similar contexts to contribute more to a step's baseline. For the second, CCPO pairs historical credit, which compares the realized return against the weighted peer baseline, with future credit, which measures the change in contextual outcome value over a short realized continuation. A step is thus credited for the progress it makes, not only for its trajectory's eventual outcome. Both credits are computed in hindsight from rollouts already collected for training, enabling CCPO to address these challenges without an auxiliary value critic. Across two Qwen2.5-Instruct backbones (1.5B and 7B), CCPO achieves up to 97.4% success on ALFWorld and 79.4% on WebShop and outperforms GRPO by up to 21.6 and 18.5 points. Ablations show that contextual weighting outperforms uniform weighting, and that historical and future credit serve as complementary learning signals.

new Genesis Steering: Better Activation Steering Through Training Data

Authors: Tora Bodin, Eric Aubinais, Ilaria Torre, Chih-Hong Cheng

Abstract: Activation steering controls large language models at inference time, but its effectiveness depends on the choice of reference data. We introduce genesis steering, which derives a genesis vector from contrastive examples in the model's own fine-tuning data. When these data are unavailable, we draw on the membership inference literature to identify likely training examples and construct a proxy dataset. We establish two theoretical results on optimal displacement to the decision boundary and reduced proxy-estimation bias. We evaluate genesis steering and membership-based proxy construction on T\"ulu-3-8B, whose fine-tuning data are publicly available, and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct as a control. We find that safety data from T\"ulu-3-8B's training mixture yield stronger steering than external datasets on both models. On T\"ulu-3-8B, membership scores largely agree in identifying training examples, and vectors built from top-ranked examples achieve competitive performance against random subsets of the same size. In contrast, the results on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct hint that a MIA method will only have a positive effect if the mean direction shift with MIA-filtered in-distribution data is much less severe than the direction shift with OoD data.

new Unlocking Structured Pruning for Reasoning Language Models via Attention-Preserving MLP Trimming

Authors: Woosang Lim, Haneul Yoo, Vincent-Daniel Yun, Sungjoo Yoo

Abstract: Structured pruning reduces LLM inference costs by removing model components to produce smaller dense models. However, existing methods can cause reasoning performance to collapse on long-form reasoning tasks such as AIME. We propose \textit{LayerTrim}, a training-free structured pruning method that identifies redundant decoder layers and selectively trims their MLP features while preserving attention and decoder depth. LayerTrim is the first training-free structured pruning method designed to preserve long-form reasoning without gradients or recovery training. Across three reasoning language models and five benchmarks, LayerTrim outperforms existing structured pruning methods in most evaluated settings, improving average accuracy by up to 21.4 percentage points over RESP. On \texttt{Qwen3-14B}, LayerTrim achieves 1.24$\times$ faster prefill and 19.7\% lower peak GPU memory than the dense model, while better preserving reasoning ability. It also achieves the shortest average generation length among evaluated pruned models, while retaining the highest average accuracy. These results demonstrate that LayerTrim improves reasoning efficiency through both structural compression and reduced token generation.

new PairIT: Autoregressive Transformers for Low-Data Molecule Optimization

Authors: Hannah Lawrence, Bodhi P. Vani, Ji Won Park, Natasa Tagasovska, Samuel Don Stanton, Andrew Martin Watkins, Michael Maser, Ewa M. Nowara

Abstract: Generating novel molecules with desired properties is a central challenge in drug discovery. Although foundational work focused on de novo generation, practical use cases require a more complex setting: conditioning on an initial seed molecule, optimizing multiple properties, and operating in data-sparse regimes. We introduce PairIT, a flexible training framework for autoregressive transformers over SMILES sequences that enables seeded generation by learning from pairs of structurally similar molecules. By leveraging matched pairs, the model learns to generate local, chemically meaningful edits that improve a property of the seed molecule. Prompt tokens extend this framework by specifying the target property, desired direction, and approximate magnitude of change. Multiple prompt tokens can be concatenated to enable multi-property optimization at inference time. For properties where pair supervision alone is insufficient, reinforcement learning post-training can further improve optimization, providing a tunable trade-off between proximity to the seed and the optimization objective. PairIT naturally supports pretraining on a large dataset and fine-tuning on a smaller dataset, which is critical for performance in data-sparse regimes. Surprisingly, we observe diminishing returns to the scale of the pretraining dataset. We demonstrate empirically on MoleculeNet and Therapeutics Data Commons datasets that, with appropriate paired-data curation, a standard transformer model of modest size (68M parameters) can achieve robust seeded molecular optimization in low-data regimes.

new Imagining Goals with Geometric Horizon Models for Policy Generalization

Authors: Daniel Lawson, Dhruv Sreenivas, Adriana Hugessen, Khimya Khetarpal, Glen Berseth

Abstract: Training from offline data has allowed for substantial progress in domains such as robotics, leading to general-purpose policies that can be easily applied zero-shot or efficiently finetuned for downstream tasks. However, these policies can still have poor generalization, both due to the choice of modeling objective and from learning from a static dataset. In this work, we focus on the challenging task of zero-shot goal generalization, where a policy is evaluated on unseen tasks that require combining its existing knowledge (compositional generalization). An avenue for improving a policy's generalization is by generating new experience with world models; however, such generation has proven difficult for longer horizons. Thus, to alleviate this issue, we propose TD-Aug, which samples from a geometric horizon model and allows for directly imagining novel outcomes that can be achieved by composing existing knowledge. We demonstrate that training on these future outcomes as goals for goal-conditioned BC policies significantly improves generalization in stitching-based OGBench tasks.

new TRACER: Trace-Regularized Training Against Indirect Prompt Injection

Authors: Kshitij Mishra, Salem Lahlou, Nils Lukas

Abstract: Indirect prompt injection uses untrusted retrieved content, tool outputs, or agent messages to override an LLM's intended behavior. We show that defense robustness is channel-conditioned: the same attack succeeds 18% of the time as a follow-up message but 53% when placed in retrieved context. We propose TRACER (Trace-Regularized Adversarial Channel Exposure with Reward-shaping), a channel-aware adversarial training method that samples presentation wrappers and combines an answer-anchored, evidence-gated reward with ATRC, which aligns attacked reasoning with clean evidence when an injection is invalid. On GSM8K with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, TRACER reduces RAG-framed ASR from 65.5% to 6.1% while preserving 78-82% clean accuracy. Joint RAG+tool training achieves 13.9% average ASR across framings at 3B scale, while a single epoch at 7B reaches 9.4%. These results demonstrate that robust defenses must explicitly train and evaluate across deployment channels.

new MA-Bench: Measuring Operational Planning in Language Models

Authors: Rodney Lafuente-Mercado, Robert Sincero, Phil Sage, Yuan Xue

Abstract: How well can a language model turn a large, incomplete and partly contradictory body of documents into a plan when no single correct answer exists? We introduce Mission Analysis (MA)-Bench, built on mission analysis, the first step of military operational planning, in which a staff reads the full situation and states the problem before anyone proposes a solution. Its 10 expert-built scenarios pair synthetic packets of ten to twelve documents with 1,257 expert-written pass/fail requirements, each containing information on their source, question type, and importance level. A reasoning-model judge grades each brief against the requirements without seeing the documents; it grades like a fifth credentialed planner, leaving a four-planner panel's agreement unchanged (Fleiss $\kappa$ 0.42 to 0.41), and a judge from a second provider reproduces the model ranking (Spearman 0.94). Across twenty frontier and open-weights models the best gets 62% of the rubric items right. Models are strongest on facts stated in the documents (58%) and weakest on judgments that must be inferred (33%), and six failures in ten are omissions rather than errors. MA-Bench grades the determinations, not the prose. We release the packets, rubrics, prompts, scorer and every graded brief with item-level verdicts.

new Towards Training and Inference of Graph Neural Networks in Graph Databases

Authors: Victor Pekkari, Harry Wik, Alfred Clemedtson, Borun Shi, Xuan-Son Vu

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks on large graphs commonly rely on in-memory graph storage with ad hoc data processing pipelines for both training and inference. Graph databases provide scalable storage and efficient traversal for graphs that can evolve over time, but their applicability in GNN training and inference are rarely studied. We show that graph DBs can be used in GNN training and inference directly, executing sampling, feature retrieval, and even the full forward pass. We show different GNN training pipelines can perform on-the-fly sampling and retrieval in graph DBs using Cypher. We further provide optimisations that speed up the training process by \~2.5x while maintaining the same accuracy. We illustrate that fully in-DB inference is possible, independent of how the GNNs are trained. Our inference setup can be >10x faster than cold loading the graph into RAM with no extra memory overhead on real-world graphs. Our work lays the foundation for in-graph-DB GNN training and inference.

new Demystifying On-Policy Distillation: Where Does the Teacher's Advantage Live?

Authors: Yiping Li, Fan Zhao, Zhibo Hou, Zhiyu An, Wan Du

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is increasingly studied as a cheaper alternative to reinforcement learning, yet it remains a form of knowledge distillation (KD) that relies on teacher supervision. We revisit OPD from the KD perspective by asking where the teacher's advantage lies along student-generated trajectories. Controlled token flips at teacher-student disagreements directly measure the accuracy gained from teacher corrections. Single-token flips yield a modest average gain on selected failed trajectories, whereas repeated flips raise accuracy from 28.4% to 74.5%, close to the teacher's 76.9%. Gains continue to accumulate with more flips, but mass- and KL-based selection outperform random flips by 15-18 percentage points at counts of similar magnitude. Selective intervention remains useful after OPD, but greater argmax agreement does not consistently improve accuracy across model pairs. We advocate training schedules that learn more valuable teacher corrections while maintaining distributional stability. Controlled flips and fork-resolution analysis evaluate OPD beyond accuracy by measuring which teacher preferences the student acquires and what further correction can recover.

new Diagnosing Low-Confidence Patient Profiles via Localized Harmful Covariate Shift Detection

Authors: Olivier Lefebvre (Department of Computer Science, Universit\'e de Sherbrooke, Sherbrooke, Canada), Hakima Laribi (Department of Computer Science, Universit\'e de Sherbrooke, Sherbrooke, Canada), Dan Poenaru (Department of Pediatric Surgery, Montreal Children's Hospital, Montreal, Canada, Centre for Outcomes Research and Evaluation), Martin Valli\`eres (Medical Physics Unit, Department of Oncology, McGill University, Montreal, Canada)

Abstract: Before deploying clinical prediction models, their reliability must be assessed to ensure safe use. Errors may arise from distribution shifts between training and deployment or from heterogeneous performance where the model systematically underperforms for certain patient subgroups. Yet most post-hoc diagnostic methods evaluate reliability only at the population level, checking whether the deployment data resemble the training data and whether the model performs well on average, without accounting for heterogeneity that may cause some patients to be disproportionately affected. We propose a unified diagnostic pipeline that combines both perspectives. MED3pa first discovers interpretable low-confidence patient profiles, and Detectron is then applied within each profile to test whether its degradation is associated with a detectable harmful covariate shift. Across three clinical prediction tasks and three injected subgroup shifts in MIMIC-IV, we find that shift detectability itself is informative. An injected age shift, undetectable at the population level, was nonetheless localized to specific degraded profiles, whereas sex- and race-related shifts were detected broadly and therefore offered little guidance for identifying the most affected patients.

new Adaptive Computation with Conformal Certification

Authors: Shehtab Zaman, Aneesh Rangnekar, Nancy Lee, Harini Veeraraghavan

Abstract: Scientific imaging data involving medical (e.g., 3D radiographic, microscopic images) and astronomical sensors pose different challenges from natural images. One key challenge is that these datasets are often orders of magnitude larger than natural images. Efficient training and inference on such data is challenging due to the increased hardware requirements, often needing bespoke solutions. Moreover, the relevant objects such as tumors and astronomical phenomena are sparsely distributed with respect to the background information. Standard efficiency techniques like quantization and pruning apply uniform scaling of all regions, potentially compromising the precision/fidelity of predictions. Thus, efficient methods that reduce computational requirements without losing resolution for relevant parts of the data and with uncertainty estimates on predictions are essential. We present \textit{Conformal Adaptation} with risk guarantees for uncertainty-aware sparse computations. Our formalism is general, spanning dense pixel-level classification and regression tasks where hierarchical models predict at low resolution, certify the signal using conformal risk control, and refine the certified predictions. We benchmark our method on multiple tasks such as 3D medical image segmentation, 2D galaxy flux prediction, and 1D calcium spike rate prediction, achieving $2.5$--$72\times$ wall-clock speedup across the various tasks, including on commodity CPUs.

new FireGraphNet: Sub-daily wildfire spread prediction with directed graph neural networks

Authors: Aiyshwariya Paulvannan Kanmani, Miguel Esparza, Ali Mostafavi

Abstract: Wildfire decisions are made in hours, yet most learned spread models predict daily on targets including burned area. We propose FireGraphNet which formulates 12-hour growth as node classification on directed H3 graphs, with candidates ahead of the front and wind alignment, terrain, fuel continuity and barriers on edges. On satellite perimeters it achieves average precision 1.7-1.9x the strongest distance baseline on test California fires and 1.62x out of state. We show this skill comes from message passing rather than cell features or the aggregator, is highest on young fires in state, is carried deep along the wind by its edges, and is hidden by pooling. A convolutional network matches this ranking once trained on new growth, at worse calibration, so candidate construction, target definition, and aggregation shape conclusions about model skill. Editing fuel and barrier attributes reduces predicted spread more under guided than random placement, extending FireGraphNet to operational intervention analysis.

new Narrowing the Collaboration Gap, Probably

Authors: Mirah Shi, Marcel Hussing, Ira Globus-Harris, Natalie Collina, Aaron Roth, Surbhi Goel

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as teams of agents that hold different private information about a shared task. How should such agents concisely communicate in a way that promotes aggregation of decision-relevant information? Communicating only proposed actions can discard decision-relevant uncertainty: two agents may prefer the same action for different reasons. We study an alternative interface in which agents communicate calibrated beliefs over actions. We first analyze a simple synthetic setting that captures the structure of multi-round reasoning under partial information. In this setting, we show theoretically and empirically that communicating unbiased probabilities can be strictly more powerful than communicating actions. We then test the same principle in a collaborative maze-solving task with trained transformers and pretrained LLM agents. Our results demonstrate that probability communication outperforms action communication when the exchanged predictions preserve calibrated uncertainty. When communicated probabilities are biased, we show that the benefits of probability communication can disappear. We give a post-hoc conversational calibration intervention that consistently improves decision-making by correcting these biases both in a toy transformer setting and when pretrained LLMs use the corrections in context. These results suggest that probabilistic communication is useful for multi-agent collaboration not merely because it transmits more information, but because calibrated uncertainty gives other agents a reliable object to update on.

new Riding the Fixed-Point Curve: Image Classification in the Continuous Thought Machine

Authors: Thomas Hannagan

Abstract: The Continuous Thought Machine (CTM) is a recurrent network that attends to inputs and makes predictions based on the synchronization between its neurons. On tasks spanning many domains, it displays emergent thought processes that have not been explained. We report a number of findings about how the CTM classifies images, focusing on ImageNet. Each thinking step is an attracting but strongly non-normal map exhibiting balanced amplification (Murphy and Miller, 2009); while attraction is already present at CTM initialization, training highly promotes non-normality. By Banach's theorem, the neural state has a locally unique attracting fixed point, which moves at every step, and the thought trajectory is the curve these fixed points trace, followed one tick behind. Most of that curve's motion is shared across images, and visible as a previously reported traveling wave of activity across neurons. The attention schedule along the thought is largely generic: shuffling queries across images at every tick costs only 7 percent of classification accuracy, while image-independent increments predict the next query with R^2 = 0.98 (held out). Our results suggest that the CTM has found a single strategy to process all ImageNet images.

new Spectral-MoE: Heterogeneous Per-Layer Expert Utilization in Dense-to-MoE Conversion

Authors: Zachary Broveak, Christian Kubicka, Thierry Tambe

Abstract: Large language models impose substantial memory demands and arithmetic costs during inference. Dense-to-mixture-of-experts (MoE) conversion is a model optimization technique that aims to reduce these arithmetic costs. Dense-to-MoE conversion partitions pretrained feed-forward network (FFN) neurons into experts and selects a subset for each token. Leading methods fall into two categories: analytic methods produce a converted model quickly using heuristics, while learning methods produce a model much more slowly by optimizing via gradients. Both approaches, however, fix the same active expert count, or "utilization," at every layer. We introduce Spectral-MoE, which learns to allocate a utilization budget heterogeneously across layers. Its name draws a metaphor between separating a dense FFN into experts and separating a light beam into its spectrum. Spectral-MoE consists of two distinct approaches: a static approach that uses a single per-layer scalar which is shared across tokens, and a dynamic approach which emits a per-token schedule. We demonstrate that both allocators improve performance over uniform on both the analytic-based and learning-based SOTA dense-to-MoE methods. At 25% FFN utilization, dynamic allocation reduces WikiText-2 perplexity relative to uniform by 1.04, 2.96, and 2.71 points on the learned base for Llama-2-7B, Llama-3-8B, and Qwen2.5-7B, respectively. The corresponding reductions on the analytic base are 2.45, 6.26, and 2.17 points. On the learned base, dynamic allocation improves six-task mean accuracy over uniform by an average of 1.5, 4.2, and 3.6 percentage points across the three models at FFN utilizations of 12.5%, 25%, and 50%, respectively. These results establish per-layer expert utilization as a novel optimization axis in dense-to-MoE conversion.

new LLMs program using Reusable and Composable Abstract Representations

Authors: Casper L. Christensen, Kyle J. Ray, Dmitry Manning-Coe, Adam Shai, Paul Riechers

Abstract: When writing code, the same computation can be expressed in different programming languages and through different implementation strategies. We show that the activations of LLMs capture this shared structure in reusable and composable abstract representations when they generate code. These representations emerge in the middle layers, are reusable across programming languages, and afford the model an impressive amount of generalization ability when generating code under various interventions. Using activation steering, we show that function, programming language, and implementation strategy can be independently manipulated. The independent interventions on language and function can be flexibly composed to control code generation, even in cases when the prompt contains no information about language and function. We additionally find that finetuning on examples of arbitrarily defined functions in one programming language transfers to other languages, consistent with the models reusing abstract features of code generation that are shared across different examples. Finally, to study how such abstract representations come to be, we implement a minimal model of shared, reusable, and composable structure in a synthetic setting, and show that transformers trained on such data learn representations that reuse these parts flexibly across contexts.

new FinGREAT: Evaluating LLMs on Financial Graph-guided Retrieval And Typing

Authors: Ashman Mehra, Bryan Routledge, Jane JaeYeon Pyo, Pierre Jinghong Liang

Abstract: Financial disclosures express the underlying relationships of double-entry accounting through unstructured text. While financial LLM benchmarks predominantly emphasize question answering and classification, schema-guided evidence discovery remains relatively under-explored. We investigate whether open-weight LLMs can extract and classify evidence corresponding to predefined double-entry accounting relationships underlying the text. Using a financial accounting graph comprising 11 nodes (accounts) and 11 edges (transaction types), we evaluate mid-sized models from 5 open-weight families across 1,300 SEC 10-K Item 7 filings spanning 26 years, comparing their extraction performance against three proprietary models. Results reveal substantial differences in extraction volume, edge distributions, and cross-model agreement. Building on this study, we introduce a human-annotated relation classification benchmark comprising 483 passages across three financial accounting categories, evaluating 34 open-weight and 10 proprietary models. The strongest open-weight and proprietary models achieve 84.1\% and 92.8\% accuracy, respectively, with considerable variation across accounting categories. Our findings highlight the importance of evaluating evidence discovery and relation understanding separately, while investigating locally deployable models for scalable, privacy-sensitive financial analysis.

new Refined Topology-Pass Scheduling: Damped Trust-Region Compensation for Dynamic k-NN Graphs in SE(3)-Equivariant Structure Prediction

Authors: Jiankai Huang, Hanwen Shen

Abstract: Equivariant structure-prediction pipelines alternate coordinate updates with the construction of a k-nearest-neighbour (kNN) graph whose membership is a discrete, piecewise-constant function of the coordinates. Rebuilding that graph at every block boundary (PB) is the expensive part of an epoch, whereas reusing one frozen graph (F) is fast but leaves a topology gap. We study a two-pass design in which the cheap pass records a small amount of state (margins, kNN radii, measured per-boundary displacements) and a second pass compensates conservatively. We formulate the choice of the graph-construction basis as a damped fixed-point iteration whose unique fixed point is exactly PB, and derive (i) exact non-flip certificates with the two-sided constant $m_i/4$ and one-sided constant $m_i/2$, (ii) the minimum-variance (BLUE) replay gain $\gamma^*$ and the resulting feasibility crossover, (iii) an $\ell_\infty$ trust-region projection that preserves convergence, and (iv) an analytic near-tie spacing law $E[m] \approx r_k/(3k)$ that matches measurements to 1\%. Three compensation levels are implemented in C++ (L1: frozen set with live geometry; L2: margin-gated local repair; L3: damped, projected basis) and validated under a matched-conditions protocol (identical seed, MSA depth and trajectory family, only the topology mode differs). L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly ($D = 0.0000$ at every boundary), L1 stays within 0.6% and L3 interpolates monotonically: L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly when the trust region is removed, and L3 interpolates between the frozen and the PB topology. The recorded trajectory itself yields a measured $1.5\times$ Pass-1 and $1.76\times$ epoch speed-up with no measurable loss gap. An open-source implementation of the runtime and of all the experiments reported here is available at https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

URLs: https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

new Beyond Time Induction: Structured Conditional Neural Solvers for Parametric Stochastic-Volatility Pricing

Authors: Jose Miguel Lara Rangel, Mixtli Vazquez Mendoza

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks provide a flexible framework for learning parametric PDE solution families without labeled data, yet preserving accurate derivatives across heterogeneous regimes remains challenging. Structured neural solvers offer compact representations, but restrictive conditional parameterizations can limit adaptation across the solution family. We introduce BeTINN, a structured conditional architecture extending time-induced modulation through multi-directional time-parameter conditioning and a joint residual pathway. We evaluate BeTINN on the Heston stochastic-volatility PDE, a demanding parametric problem with mixed derivatives and economically relevant first- and second-order sensitivities. BeTINN improves joint accuracy across normalized option value and key sensitivity metrics relative to our baselines. Controlled ablations characterize the roles of its architectural components. We further find that validation physics loss is informative but does not fully determine differential fidelity, and that accurate solution values can coexist with localized derivative errors. These results support structured conditional adaptation and motivate derivative-aware evaluation when sensitivities are downstream quantities of interest.

new Exploring Interpretability in Deep Learning-Based Forest Classification - Separating Similar Forest Classes using Sentinel-2 Time Series

Authors: Maximilian Kirsch, Christine Preisach

Abstract: The ecological insurance theory posits that diverse forests are more resilient and better able to adapt to changing environmental conditions. Increasing drought and heat stress in Central Europe promotes mixed forests for maintaining ecosystem services. However, a diverse species distribution complicates remote sensing-based species mapping, as distinguishing pure from mixed species compositions at the pixel level remains challenging. We evaluated the potential of recurrent neural networks (RNNs) for tree-species classification from Sentinel-2 time series and investigated the spectral-temporal features underlying class discrimination, with particular emphasis on separating pure from mixed Norway spruce pixels. We compared RNN classification models with Random Forest (RF) and Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) and used Integrated Gradients (IG) to quantify feature attributions across spectral bands and time. The best-performing RNN achieved a Macro-F1 score of 0.851 on the test data, outperforming XGBoost by 0.012 and Random Forest by 0.103. Beyond classification performance, the attribution analysis revealed distinct spectral-temporal patterns associated with the separation of pure and mixed Norway spruce pixels. Attribution values increased particularly after approximately day of year (DOY) 125, while B11 showed the strongest contribution during periods of increasing spectral divergence between the two classes. These findings show that recurrent models can provide competitive classification performance while also revealing the phenological periods and spectral regions that are most relevant for distinguishing closely related forest compositions. Combining time-series classification with explainability therefore provides additional insight into the spectral-temporal mechanisms underlying tree-species discrimination and may support more detailed mapping of mixed forest structures.

new Outcome-Routed Distillation and Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Language Models

Authors: Wentao Lu, Tianyu Zhu, Jesse Clark

Abstract: On-policy distillation provides dense supervision on the student's own samples, but its targets still come from the teacher. Verifier reinforcement learning (RL) instead uses an outcome signal, but it provides no direct constraint on capabilities outside the verifier. Combining the two is difficult for diffusion language models (dLLMs), because bidirectional denoising makes the policy likelihoods required by ratio-based RL unreliable. We develop a likelihood-free pipeline for a mixture-of-experts dLLM. Each rollout group is routed by verifier outcome. Mixed groups use ratio-free, group-centred REINFORCE. If every student rollout fails but a teacher succeeds, the model receives dense KL supervision on the student's denoising canvases. All other groups are skipped. A KL term anchored to the distilled checkpoint limits changes outside the verifier reward. The tool-calling teacher succeeds on only 18.6% of the training prompts. At 24 denoising steps, distillation exceeds the teacher by 6.77 percentage points, and routing improves the distilled checkpoint by a further 18.31 points. Routing lowers reasoning-trace accuracy by 0.71 points but raises direct-answer accuracy on the same items by 13.21 points. The anchored arm restores trace accuracy. Its tool-calling score differs by -0.16 points, with a confidence interval that includes zero, although this comparison also removes a small fallback distillation route. A domain-conditional recipe retains a 16.37-point tool-calling gain; a second seed gives 18.23 points and reproduces both preservation results, but the math result is seed-dependent. We pre-registered every arm and evaluated each comparison on paired items in a single sweep on one machine.

new Dimension-Free Scaling Laws for Size Generalization: An Exponential Separation

Authors: Behrooz Tahmasebi, Melanie Weber

Abstract: Modern machine learning models increasingly operate on variable-size structured data, such as sets and graphs. A central requirement is \emph{size generalization}: the ability of a model trained on inputs of one size to generalize to inputs of a different, potentially much larger, size. Classical statistical learning theory, however, primarily considers fixed input domains, and its guarantees often deteriorate with input dimension. Although extensive work has established when models on sets and graphs can be transferred across input sizes, such results do not characterize the statistical cost of doing so: the sample complexity of size generalization, and its relationship to classical in-domain learning, remain largely unknown. We develop a statistical learning theory of size generalization and establish a sharp exponential separation in sample complexity. Letting $n$ denote the number of training samples, we show that polynomial source decay is insufficient for efficient size generalization: while it yields error $n^{-\Theta(1)}$ and hence polynomial sample complexity for in-domain learning, size-generalization error decays only as $(\log n)^{-\Theta(1)}$, yielding an exponential separation. Thus, polynomial in-domain sample complexity can become exponential across sizes. We then identify an exponential source condition under which size generalization achieves error $n^{-\Theta(1)}$, recovering polynomial sample complexity. These guarantees are independent of input dimension and depend only on the number of training samples, yielding dimension-free scaling laws. We also provide matching minimax lower bounds, establishing optimality. To our knowledge, this work provides the first dimension-free minimax theory of size generalization and a rigorous characterization of when, and at what statistical cost, learning can transfer across input sizes.

new Exact Scaling Laws for a Random-Features Bigram Model

Authors: Julian Enoch, Elvis Dohmatob

Abstract: Understanding neural scaling laws is increasingly important for theory and practice, yet first-principles explanations remain limited even in simple settings. In this work, we study capacity-limited scaling in a bottlenecked bigram model with power-law (Zipf) token statistics, viewed as an analytically tractable toy model of a large language model. Fixed random embeddings serve as a proxy for representational capacity and induce an explicit bottleneck of width, allowing us to derive scaling laws for prediction regret as a function of model width, vocabulary size, and the Zipf exponent. Our results complement recent theoretical accounts based on optimization dynamics by isolating scaling due purely to representational constraints. In the appropriate regime, the theory recovers the functional form of classical neural scaling laws and suggests that heavy-tailed language statistics may underlie both optimization- and capacity-driven scaling. Experiments natural language datasets (WikiText2, OpenWebText) closely match the predicted curves.

new Verifier-Grounded Agentic RL for Provably Correct Code

Authors: George Ma, Yuhao Zhang, Bing He, Zeren Shui, Ferhat Erata, Murali Krishna Ramanathan, Somayeh Sojoudi, Hao Zhou

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for code usually rewards passing tests, which a model can do while remaining wrong on the inputs the tests do not cover. A program verifier instead decides whether a program satisfies a formal specification for all inputs. Language models are almost always paired with verifiers at inference time only, and turning a verifier into a reward raises two difficulties. It reports only acceptance or rejection, which gives no gradient when every attempt fails, and a policy can cheat it, most simply by telling it to assume what the proof was supposed to establish. We build an agentic environment and reward that address both. The reward reads the verifier's diagnostics rather than its verdict, scoring a candidate by the weighted fraction of proof obligations it discharges, extended with hidden probes from a reference proof's annotations. An integrity audit outranks the verifier, rejecting any candidate that alters the specification or assumes what it was meant to prove. The model works as an agent against a live verifier over many turns, with a separately trained proof specialist as a frozen collaborator. Across Qwen3 models from 1.7B to 14B and a 35B Qwen3.6 mixture-of-experts model, held-out verification on code generation rises from 66.1% to 93.5% on DafnyBench and from 56.3% to 100% on SWE-Proof. This reward leads every other training signal we compare, including a binary pass-or-fail reward trained under otherwise identical settings, with the widest margin on code generation. When the agent must also deliver the repository patch, graded by each project's withheld tests, the fraction of held-out issues it resolves rises from 59.6% to 72.5%. Evaluated unchanged on three verification suites withheld from training, the checkpoints improve on all of them, including one whose language and verifier are absent from training.

new DTDD: Divergence-Triggered Dynamic Distillation for Reliable On-Policy Supervision

Authors: Qijia He, Zequn Li, Spencer Luo, Tianle Chen, Pradyumna Narayana, Zixian Ma, Ranjay Krishna, Lan Nie, Sugato Basu

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense token-level supervision on student rollouts, but this supervision can become unreliable when the student and teacher diverge, either because of a large initial policy gap or compounding errors in long-horizon reasoning and tool use. We introduce Divergence-Triggered Dynamic Distillation (DTDD), an adaptive OPD framework that uses segment-level student-teacher divergence to trigger teacher takeover during training. When divergence triggers an intervention during reasoning, the student prefix continues to receive standard OPD supervision. The teacher then generates a recovery suffix, which the student learns through behavior cloning, using cross-entropy on the teacher-generated tokens. We evaluate DTDD in three regimes: long-horizon reasoning, distillation from a base initialization without offline supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and multi-turn agents. We also compare alternative trigger designs and teacher-intervention rates. DTDD reduces the post-warmup peak gradient norm by $8.6\times$ and, from a base initialization, reaches $21.3\%$ held-out math accuracy in $2.6\times$ fewer optimizer updates than OPD; both methods finish near $22\%$. It improves best-observed performance by $4.8$ and $4.1$ percentage points on ALFWorld with LoRA and agentic code, respectively. Ablations show that segment-averaged triggers outperform token-spike triggers. Behavior cloning is also more effective than the tested OPD surrogate for training teacher-generated recoveries. Across 23 experimental arms, takeover tends to help when the teacher substantially outperforms the OPD-trained student, provided that the teacher can recover from student-visited states, the student can learn those recoveries, and teacher intervention is neither excessive nor insufficient.

new Did the LLM Answer the Question? A Semantic Reconstruction Game for Jailbreak Evaluation

Authors: Liam Davis, Romain Emil-Popescu, Suman Jana

Abstract: Jailbreak attacks against large language models (LLMs) are evaluated using attack success rate (ASR), which depends on correctly identifying responses that fulfill prohibited requests. Existing evaluators can mistake refusals or topically related responses for successful attacks. We show that changing only the judge LLM can alter reported ASR by up to $82$ percentage points. We introduce the semantic reconstruction game, a training-free evaluator that decomposes compliance judgments into constrained semantic comparisons. Given only a response, an LLM reconstructs the request answered by its substantive content, excluding information merely repeated or refused. The game compares this reconstruction against similar decoy requests to determine whether it matches the original request, allowing abstention when neither candidate matches. Across three off-the-shelf LLM judges, the game achieves the lowest mean error against human labels among evaluated methods and reduces variation in reported ASR. It prioritizes avoiding false positives, even at the cost of missing some successful jailbreaks.

new Historical-Feature Residual Adaptation for Sequential Screening

Authors: Yixiang Hong, Yuchen Zhang

Abstract: Sequential screening policies must use acquired feedback effectively under a fixed experiment budget. We study acquisition-time residual adaptation: a small ranking correction built from fixed historical features and anchored to base scores recorded before feedback is revealed. The interface accepts a history-conditioned scorer and a candidate feature table; its primary instantiation augments AssayFormer's ten-dimensional representation with 64 historical directions while retaining the pretrained model. In retrospective evaluation on 20 official-test screens from 15 recorded sources, a comparison fixed before target-outcome access increased source-macro fraction of hits recovered from 22.58% to 28.61%, exceeding all six prespecified comparators. A same-feature ordinary ranking head achieved 25.73%. Supplementary strong logistic, rank-fusion, and unprojected controls achieved 26.42%, 26.02%, and 26.78%, respectively. A separate model-independent pilot on 13 reused development screens reconstructs the features without AssayFormer assets. Across three seeds, residual adaptation improves random-forest hit recovery from 28.87% to 31.27%, while logistic recovery changes from 30.22% to 29.78%. The results show effective pretrained-policy adaptation and useful transfer to an independent nonlinear base, with gains that depend on the base model.

new Why don't Graph Transformers attend to the same token?

Authors: Alexandre Bismuth

Abstract: In large language models (LLMs), attention concentrates heavily on the first token. This 'attention sink' is believed to protect deep models against over-mixing. Even though graph transformers face a related pressure, we find that a SoTA graph transformer, GRIT, rarely forms sinks on molecules, and that it sinks less when trained on larger graphs, the opposite of the trend in LLMs. In principle, a graph can prevent sinks: we prove and show empirically that its symmetry and locality limit the attention any node can receive. On molecules, however, with distinguishable atoms and global attention, neither limit prevents sinks; what stops them lies in the model. We find that the attention scoring function is the main cause. Replacing GRIT's gated logit with a dot product raises its sink rate from 0.09 to 0.68 on identical graphs. In Graph Transformers using dot-product attention, removing the sink gives about the same error as removing a random node, while GRIT's most-attended nodes matter to its predictions. Attention sinks are therefore a property of dot-product attention rather than of Transformers. In our experiments, they form when dot-product scoring has a distinguishable node to concentrate on.

new Counterfactual Gate-Support Decomposition: Separating Selection from Redistribution in Training-Data Filtering

Authors: Hyojung Han, Jongmin Kim, Seung-Hun Jeon

Abstract: A training-data quality gate changes both which examples survive within regions of the input space and how training mass is distributed across those regions, so a gate-versus-random comparison does not isolate these mechanisms. We introduce Counterfactual Gate-Support Decomposition (CGSD), a four-arm, budget-matched design that crosses gate eligibility with support distribution and decomposes the observed gate effect into within-support selection, between-support redistribution, and their interaction. The practical constraint is finite-pool realizability: we characterize mass and token-assembly conditions under which the required arms can be constructed, together with a support-extinction case in which the ordinary design is undefined. On Korean-English and Korean-Japanese retrieval, the mean within-support selection component is the only one that exceeds its calibrated null band (+29.7 and +5.6 holdout items; 30/30 positive replicates), decisively in Korean-English and marginally in Korean-Japanese; redistribution and interaction remain unresolved at the available power.
