In a fixed, version-controlled testbed with three model families, accuracy-matched proxy reward models and two gold reward models, RL turns 41% to 66% of its proxy gain into rent while gold reward plateaus. Rent follows each regulator's prices. Neither proxy verifies facts, but one pays for confident specifics and the other for displays of diligence, so RL policies learn to fabricate details about nonexistent entities (97% to 100% of probes) or to deflect factual questions (67% to 98%). Kinship between policy and regulator adds a fixed premium of about 0.1 standard deviations but gives the policy no extra leverage under optimization. At a fixed expected fine, frequent mild audits beat rare severe ones, and random audits are as efficient per audit as targeted ones. Bringing the bunching estimator of tax economics to RL training, we also find a first sign that policies learn to stay just below a targeted audit's threshold.

new Memory-Efficient Subspace Optimization with Residual Correction: From Momentum SGD to Muon

Authors: Sifan Yang, Hongyi Tao, Dingzhi Yu, Haomin Bai, Wei Jiang, Silin Gao, Pi Bu, Lijun Zhang

Abstract: Memory-efficient subspace optimization has emerged as a practical approach to reducing the memory cost of LLM training while preserving optimization performance. Previous algorithms typically rely on the leading singular subspace of stochastic gradients, yet the gradient noise may dominate the subspace in later training stages and can prevent convergence. To overcome this limitation, we adopt a residual correction framework, which maintains the optimizer states in the subspace and adds the orthogonal residual gradient to the parameter update. We first integrate this scheme into momentum SGD to develop SMRC, which attains an $O(T^{-1/4})$ convergence rate for a fixed orthonormal basis, matching that of full-space momentum SGD. Our analysis introduces a subspace virtual iterate to resolve the coupling between the momentum and the residual correction. Furthermore, we extend SMRC to a multi-stage variant which periodically recomputes bases to track the evolving dominant subspace and retains the same rate. Since each basis refresh breaks the consistency of the virtual iterates, we devise a boundary correction approach that aligns them across stages. Beyond momentum SGD, we incorporate our framework into Muon and propose PMRC, which applies the matrix sign to the projected momentum. Through a weighted momentum tracking analysis that accounts for the residual gradient, we establish an $O(T^{-1/4})$ convergence rate for PMRC, which matches the rate of full-space Muon. We also design a multi-stage PMRC with periodically refreshed bases, which maintains a convergence rate of the same order. Extensive experimental results confirm the effectiveness and efficiency of our algorithms.

new Energy Consumption Analysis of Discrete Diffusion and Autoregressive Language Models

Authors: Boris Ruf, Marcin Detyniecki

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) continue to scale, their energy consumption during inference has become a critical concern. While Autoregressive (AR) models are the industry standard, Diffusion Language Models (DLLMs) have emerged as a promising alternative capable of parallel token generation. In this paper, we perform a comparative energy benchmark of diffusion models (LLaDA-8B, Dream-7B) against AR counterparts (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen-2.5-7B). We analyze the trade-offs between model architecture, KV-cache optimization, and Joules-per-token efficiency. Our results show that at longer sequences (200--600 tokens), diffusion models achieve 2.5--2.7 J/token, approximately 2$\times$ more efficient than AR models with KV-cache (5.3--5.7 J/token). KV-cache provides substantial benefits for AR models at longer sequences (50--70\% energy reduction).

new SPARS: A Stability-Plasticity-Aware Region Selection Framework for Continual Learning

Authors: Teif Aldaajani, Huda Hakami, Maha A. Thafar

Abstract: Continual learning aims to acquire new knowledge while retaining previously learned knowledge. However, updating shared parameters when learning new tasks often overwrites existing knowledge, causing catastrophic forgetting. Memory replay methods mitigate catastrophic forgetting but rely on previous samples, raising privacy, memory, and scalability concerns. On the other hand, parameter isolation-based methods preserve learned knowledge but may limit knowledge transfer, whereas regularization promotes adaptability but remains susceptible to interference between old and new tasks. To maintain the stability-plasticity trade-off, this paper proposes Stability-Plasticity-Aware Region Selection (SPARS), a replay-free hybrid framework combining global parameter isolation with localized parameter regularization. SPARS estimates parameter importance using either the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) or a novel Confidence Score (CS) estimator that leverages logit sensitivity and achieves comparable performance to FIM without requiring labelled data. Based on parameter importance, SPARS partitions parameters into three importance-aware regions namely, winning, neighboring, and non-important parameter regions. Each region receives a different adaptation strategy: selective freezing, soft regularization, or unrestricted learning based on its role in the stability-plasticity trade-off. To mitigate classifier bias in class-incremental learning, we employ Quadratic Discriminant Analysis to improve performance on previously learned classes .Experiments on CIFAR-10 and TinyImageNet demonstrate consistent improvement in average incremental accuracy over existing isolation- and regularization-based methods across task- and class-incremental learning. On TinyImageNet, SPARS achieves the best performance with 6% improvement for class-incremental learning while retaining over 60% of parameters for future tasks.

new Neural Wasserstein Splines: Representation-Invariant Second-Order Priors for Trajectory Inference

Authors: Amartya Banerjee, Harlin Lee, Caroline Moosm\"uller

Abstract: Population dynamics are often observed only as unpaired snapshots, which leave the evolution between measurements underdetermined. Second-order regularization can constrain it, but a penalty on the accelerations of sampled particles is a prior on joint particle paths, and different particle representations of the same marginal curve can have different acceleration costs. We therefore introduce Neural Wasserstein Splines (NWSP), a sample-based method that learns a continuous population curve under a Wasserstein smoothness prior. A neural network maps latent variables to the control points of a cubic spline in time, so samples at any time come from the spline basis without numerical integration. We project the generator's velocity onto gradients of a finite dictionary of scalar potentials and differentiate this measure-dependent projection in time, obtaining a finite-dimensional surrogate for the intrinsic Wasserstein acceleration, trained jointly with distributional fit losses. For a fixed dictionary and ridge parameter, we prove that the population penalty depends only on the marginal curve and bound its sensitivity to moment-estimation errors. Learned potentials (NWSP-N) sharpen acceleration estimates. On various synthetic and single-cell benchmarks, NWSP is highly competitive with transport and flow-based methods, ranking in the top two in 11 of 16 settings. The penalty improves on an unregularized spline in 13 of 16 settings and, while competitive with a particle-acceleration penalty, adds provable representation invariance.

new Occupancy Modeling via Drifting: One-Step Generation Enables Direct Policy Extraction

Authors: Robin Uhrich, Marco Bagatella, N\'uria Armengol Urp\'i, Pavel Kolev, Georg Martius

Abstract: The growing interest in behavior foundation models, combined with recent advancements in generative modeling, has led to an appealing recipe for reinforcement learning from unlabeled data: pre-train a generative model to model occupancies, i.e., discounted state visitations, on reward-free trajectories, such that a policy can be easily extracted once a small reward-labeled dataset becomes available. We decompose this recipe along three axes: (i) the degree to which the multi-modal structure of occupancies must be captured, (ii) the class of generative model, and (iii) the method used for policy extraction. Along each axis, we consider existing choices, propose simpler alternatives, and validate each decision experimentally. This analysis culminates in a novel, streamlined method that leverages drifting, a modern one-step generative sampler, to model the occupancy. We show that this enables policy extraction via direct differentiation through the occupancy model, and bypasses the need for critic distillation. Overall, we observe that this nimble setup leads to improvements in performance on standard benchmarks.

new On the Limits of Representation-Based Test-Time Adaptation for Multimodal LLMs

Authors: Miko{\l}aj Pi\'orczy\'nski, Wojciech Masarczyk, Damian S\'ojka, Sebastian Cygert, Bart{\l}omiej Twardowski

Abstract: Vision-language models suffer significant accuracy drops under common corruptions. While test-time adaptation (TTA) successfully mitigates these shifts in CLIP-like contrastive models, its application to generative multimodal large language models (MLLMs) remains underexplored and uniquely challenging due to their sequential token generation. In this work, we demonstrate that representation-based TTA fails to recover performance in MLLMs. Our experiments reveal that even when violating standard TTA assumptions by leveraging target data offline, sample-blind feature alignment recovers only marginal accuracy. We attribute this failure to the nature of corruption shifts: unlike CLIP-like architectures, which benefit from a shared domain-shift direction within a single pooled classification embedding, MLLM visual token sequence shifts are nearly orthogonal and span the full embedding space.These findings bound what any sample-blind, position-shared correction at a single encoder site can achieve, establishing that representation-space interventions alone might be insufficient to ensure corruption robustness in MLLMs.

new Universal Sample-Based Embeddings of Higher-Order Objects

Authors: Radovan Halu\v{s}ka, Christopher Solinas, Nathan R. Sturtevant, Martin Schmid, Michael Bowling

Abstract: Neural networks operate naturally on finite-dimensional vectors, yet many problems require reasoning about higher-order objects such as distributions, functions, and graphs. Such objects capture relationships within a ground set and rarely admit an explicit compact representation. Moreover, we typically want a compact representation not of a single higher-order object but of an entire family of such objects, including members unseen during training. While a few approaches exist for particular object types, none is designed to work across object types. We propose a unified framework that represents any higher-order object as a fixed-size embedding vector learned over a family of such objects. An encoder maps an object, seen only through a finite set of samples, to an embedding, and a domain-specific decoder supplies the training signal by modeling the object conditioned on that embedding. Since the encoder works only from samples, one architecture serves different object types. Because the decoder is a model already suited to single instances of the object type, the framework inherits that model's native operations and inductive biases. We instantiate the framework on distributions, functions, and graphs. In each case we show that a single trained model represents an entire family and generalizes to members unseen during training. Furthermore, we demonstrate the downstream utility of these embeddings: interpolating distributions in embedding space, solving differential equations in embedding space, and predicting unseen edges from a graph's embedding.

new When Non-Adversarial Noise Hurts Learning: Benchmarking Skill Self-Evolution in Agents

Authors: Tao Yu, Zhongtian Luo, Yuchen Long, Yiheng Lu, Yuxuan Zhou, Yanpei Gong, Siyi Cao, Yuqing Wu, Haopeng Jin, Zhenghao Zhang, Yujia Yang, Jingmin Zhu, Yunyun Hou, Xinming Wang, Hongzhu Yi, Jiachen Liu, Sirui Wang, Nan Duan, Yan Huang, Liang Wang, Tianjin Huang, Yanyan Zou

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly improve by distilling experience into reusable skills, but errors in that experience can persist in learned rules and affect later tasks. Existing evaluations largely examine clean learning or perturbations encountered after skills are frozen, leaving robustness during self-evolution insufficiently understood. We introduce RSE-Bench, to our knowledge the first benchmark for evaluating skill self-evolution under ordinary, non-adversarial noise. It spans four domains and six benchmarks, with controlled perturbations to instructions, environmental evidence, execution records, and outcome--record pairings that preserve the underlying tasks and native scoring rules. The protocol separates noise during learning from input-side noise at evaluation, testing frozen skill stores on held-out tasks. We also introduce \textsc{RSEClaw}, a method-agnostic framework supporting consistent noise injection and process diagnostics of actual exposure and skill updates. Across three methods and two backbones, noisy learning retains only $25.5\%$--$42.1\%$ of the aggregate gain achieved by clean self-evolution, depending on the noise type. Perturbations confined to execution records and outcome--record pairings cause average losses comparable to input-side noise, despite leaving the original executions and evaluations intact. Diagnostics further identify differences in candidate skill text that coexist with small changes in mean held-out performance.

new CARD: Closing the Teacher-Student Loop for Adversarial Robustness Distillation

Authors: Zhi Li, Haowei Liu, Hongchen Yang, Xiaoxuan Wang, Song Gao, Zhenli He, Wei Zhou, Shaowen Yao

Abstract: Adversarial robustness distillation typically treats a robust teacher as a fixed knowledge source and transfers robustness to a compact student in a one-way manner. This paradigm terminates teacher-student interaction once the student is distilled, thereby discarding potentially useful feedback from the distilled student. However, exploiting such feedback is nontrivial because indiscriminate weak-to-strong transfer may propagate student errors to the teacher. To address this issue, we propose Cyclic Adversarial Robustness Distillation (CARD), a closed-loop framework that turns the distilled student into a feedback source for refining the robust teacher and subsequently transfers the teacher gains back to the same student. CARD first performs strong-to-weak adversarial distillation and then uses reliability-enhanced student feedback to refine the robust teacher. The refinement combines inverse adversarial student feedback with teacher-student discrepancy maximization, after which the refined teacher is used to continue training the same student under the unchanged base distillation objective. We further establish a sample-level sufficient condition for reliable weak-to-strong feedback transfer and derive a distribution-level upper bound on the refined teacher's risk. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet-10 across multiple teacher-student architectures and base distillation methods show that CARD improves adversarial robustness in most evaluated settings while generally preserving clean accuracy. These results show that the distilled student can provide useful feedback to the teacher, enabling robustness transfer to proceed beyond the conventional one-way teacher-to-student paradigm.

new Beyond DFT electronic band structures via dual-space orbital learning

Authors: Minhyuk Kang, Beom Seok Kang, Nikolai Argatoff, Miguel Nouman, Danish Khan, William A. Goddard III, Anima Anandkumar

Abstract: Accurate electronic band structures are essential for predicting material properties, but high-fidelity calculations are computationally demanding. We introduce O-kRystal, a dual-space orbital-learning framework that uses a lower-cost Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) calculation as both an energy baseline and a source of structured orbital information. Combining PBE band energies with real- and reciprocal-space orbital matrices, O-kRystal predicts higher-level corrections for individual electronic states as a function of crystal momentum and spin. For states within 5 eV of the band-edge midpoint, it predicts Heyd-Scuseria-Ernzerhof (HSE06) band energies with a mean absolute error of $0.171 \pm 0.009$ eV across nearly 6,000 three-dimensional materials spanning insulating, metallic, magnetic, and nonmagnetic systems. On a separate benchmark of six strongly correlated materials, it reduces the corresponding PBE band-energy MAE by 68.7% and resolves orbital-dependent changes in the relative placement of transition-metal d and ligand p states. Despite training only at high-symmetry points, it maintains comparable accuracy at unseen points along band paths and reconstructs HSE06 densities of states from dense Brillouin-zone predictions. Finally, training O-kRystal on $G_0W_0$ quasiparticle energies for two-dimensional materials demonstrates its applicability across levels of theory and the potential of orbital learning for higher-fidelity, momentum-resolved electronic-structure prediction beyond density functional theory.

new MolHop: Scaffold-Hopping Molecular Design with Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Yanyi Su, Chenyan Wang, Yulei Gong, Yeyun Chen, Xiaohui Yang, Jun Cheng

Abstract: Molecular design is often formulated as an optimization problem over existing molecular spaces. However, molecular optimization and the discovery of structurally distinct replacement molecules represent two fundamentally different search problems. LLM-based molecular design agents typically perform the former, conducting local refinement within the neighborhood of existing structures to improve target properties through structural modifications. In contrast, the latter requires exploration of a broader chemical space to discover molecules with entirely different scaffolds while preserving the functional characteristics of reference molecules. We propose MolHop, an agentic framework for cross-scaffold molecular discovery that enables models to navigate and edit within a pre-organized chemical space. MolHop organizes approximately 1.6 million molecular scaffolds from commercial molecules into a coarse-to-fine hierarchical action space. The agent selects new exploration regions through scaffold navigation actions and adjusts candidate properties through molecular editing. The framework maintains historical trajectory memory to support long-horizon search processes. On drug molecule and commercial compound tasks, connecting Grok-4.6 and DeepSeek-Flash-V4.1 to the MolHop framework substantially improves their cross-scaffold joint success rates. We develop MolHop-9B through agentic reinforcement learning, and MolHop-9B outperforms generative baselines including GPT-5.6-sol, Claude-5-Opus, and Gemini-3.1-Pro, while generating candidate molecules with larger scaffold differences from the reference molecules. These results demonstrate that effective molecular discovery beyond the traditional optimization paradigm requires structured chemical and action spaces, enabling agents to explore new structural regions rather than merely refine existing molecules.

new MorphingNet: Symbolic Regression Where the Network Is the Formula

Authors: Jincheng Gao, Jiahe Yi, Shuo Sun

Abstract: We introduce MorphingNet, a network for symbolic regression (SR) whose units change their function continuously. Instead of searching over expressions built from a fixed operator library, MorphingNet trains the form of each function as a parameter, so SR becomes one continuous optimization problem. Its units are either pole-free rational functions or complex exponential--logarithm functions, which move between exponentials, sinusoids, damped oscillations, power laws and logarithms as their parameters change. The network is a readable formula at every step of training, and no operator library is needed to train it; a structure-recovery step afterwards rewrites the trained formula into short elementary expressions. Training is dense linear algebra, so it is GPU-friendly and scales to large data. MorphingNet achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on SRBench, recovers most generating formulas on synthetic benchmarks, and on large data is up to two orders of magnitude faster than the strongest search method. By turning the search for a formula into the training of a network, MorphingNet offers a new paradigm for SR and for scientific discovery from data.

new Learning Graph Energies for Finite-Time Generation

Authors: Amartya Banerjee, Xingyu Xu, Caroline Moosm\"uller, Harlin Lee

Abstract: Energy-based models can represent distributions over discrete structures such as molecular graphs, but an energy that is accurate at equilibrium need not yield accurate samples when the sampler stops after a fixed number of steps. We introduce finite-horizon energy learning, which adapts the acceptance energy of a Metropolis--Hastings sampler to the distribution it returns after a prescribed number of steps, with initialization, proposals, and annealing schedule held fixed. For graph generation, a frozen pretrained model supplies initialization and proposals, and a learned energy governs acceptance. A training-only discriminator scores completed samples, and coupled continuations of the accepted and rejected branches of a proposal give an acceptance-gradient estimator that is unbiased for a fixed discriminator under stated conditions, without equilibrated negative samples or differentiation through discrete states. Generation uses the learned energy in the original single-trajectory sampler, without discriminator evaluations or reranking. On twelve pairwise binary targets with exactly evaluated terminal laws, learned energies have lower median terminal total variation than the equilibrium-correct energy in every tested short-horizon setting, and than its numerically tuned scalar multiple in most settings. On MOSES, a learned two-coefficient combination of pretrained Graph Energy Matching (GEM) potentials lowers FCD by 3.7--10.5\% relative to scale-calibrated GEM at matched sampling steps, with validity gains at the shortest budget and a sub-point validity trade-off at higher budgets.

new Safe-FCL: Continual Risk Awareness and Fine-Grained Safety Aggregation for Federated Continual Learning of LLMs

Authors: Shuaijie Ma, Yongkang Hu, Yu Cheng, Jiuan Zhou, Yi Xie, Yushuo Zhang, Zhaoxia Yin

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning enables large language models (LLMs) to learn collaboratively from distributed client data, but malicious client updates can compromise global model safety. As federated tasks evolve, changing malicious update patterns challenge the ability of existing defenses to maintain reliable risk detection over time. To address this challenge, we propose Safe-FCL, a framework integrating continual risk awareness with fine-grained safety aggregation for federated continual learning of LLMs. Specifically, the Continual Risk Awareness (CRA) mechanism learns emerging risk patterns across sequential tasks and constrains detector updates using a protective subspace derived from historical features, mitigating forgetting of previously learned malicious patterns while adapting to new risks. To translate risk awareness into aggregation-level safety control, the Fine-Grained Safety Aggregation (FGSA) mechanism combines client-level risk assessments from CRA with module-level deviations from historical update behavior to adaptively regulate aggregation contributions, suppressing potentially malicious updates while minimizing unnecessary restrictions on benign updates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Safe-FCL outperforms current SOTA methods, improving global-model Rule and MD-Judge scores in federated continual learning by 0.41% and 0.64%, respectively, while increasing average risk recognition accuracy by 2.01%.

new LatentLoop: Scaling Looped MoE Models with Asymmetric Compute Allocation

Authors: Zefeng He, Yunkai Chai, Xiaoyang Chen, Tong Zhu, Zongliang Wu, Yun Luo, Yafu Li, Yu Cheng

Abstract: Looped Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models combine parameter reuse with sparse computation to improve performance under matched budgets. Prior work demonstrates that different loop visits can play different roles even when they share parameters. To probe the contributions of different loop visits, we learn separate residual coefficients in a model that applies a shared block twice, observing a smaller scale in the first visit. Fixed coefficient experiments show that reducing the first visit's scale preserves or improves performance, whereas the same reductions to the second hurt it. This asymmetry motivates us to explore asymmetric compute allocation. We propose \textbf{LatentLoop}, which compresses the first visit's sequence, retains full resolution in the second, and reallocates saved computation to increase the hidden size. We further learn how strongly the first visit updates the full sequence, and share MoE weights across visits while using separate attention parameters to accommodate their different sequence resolutions. Across 15 configurations spanning 1.3B--7B parameters, with the largest budget exceeding $10^{21}$ FLOPs, LatentLoop consistently lowers validation NLL. At a fitted NLL of 1.988, predicted compute savings are 19.0\% and 7.9\% relative to vanilla MoE and the looped baseline. It also improves average scores on nine benchmarks at all sizes in 0-shot and 5-shot settings. These results suggest asymmetric compute allocation is a promising direction for looped MoE models.

new Empirical Boundaries of Reservoir Computing for Few-Shot Sequence Pattern Tasks

Authors: Mario Mallea, Angela Nebot, Franscisco Mugica

Abstract: Efficient sequence modeling remains a fundamental challenge in machine learning. Despite the success of Transformers, recurrent neural networks (RNNs) have recently re-emerged as efficient alternatives. Particularly, Echo State Networks (ESNs) offer a compelling framework of randomized RNNs that have been extensively studied for time-series forecasting and dynamical systems. This paper presents an empirical analysis of distributional, architectural, and optimization variants of ESNs evaluated on few-shot sequence-to-sequence (seq2seq) pattern tasks. These synthetic tasks utilize discrete symbol sequences, characterized by delays, inactive phases, and triggered markers to isolate and evaluate memory and pattern recognition capabilities. The minimality of these tasks is crucial to understand the strengths and limitations of machine learning models. Furthermore, when the input signal utilizes a one-hot encoding, ESN dynamics are governed strictly by their weight distributions, offering an ideal lens through which to study reservoir computing. Our findings map the performance boundaries of ESNs relative to state-of-the-art (SOTA) models. We show that preserving a linear state regime is vital for memory tasks, such as pattern completion or simple copy. We propose a data-dependent modulation mechanism for nonlinear input injection into the reservoir (G-ESN). G-ESN functions as a statistical filter capable of solving tasks that conventional ESNs cannot (the adding problem and bracket matching). Finally, for Deep ESNs, we evaluate a shared readout mechanism across layers that outperforms conventional concatenated readouts on structural tasks, including selective copy, sorting problem, and bracket matching.

new Provable Transformer Context Compression for In-Context Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Yajie Zhao, Yingjun Mo, Zhiyong Wang, Bo Tang, Chao Xu, Fang Kong

Abstract: Transformers can perform in-context reinforcement learning without parameter updates. However, existing theoretical constructions often require context sizes that grow with the number of sampled transitions, leading to increasing memory costs in large-scale problems. This raises a fundamental scalability question: how much context is intrinsically necessary to carry out such learning? We answer this question for fixed-policy TD evaluation. We show that, with a shared linear decoder, the minimum context dimension required to exactly reproduce the first $L$ TD iterates is the Krylov dimension induced by the sampled transitions. When the available context dimension $m$ falls below this threshold, exact reproduction is no longer possible. In this regime, we characterize the optimal approximation error and show that it decays as $\Theta(\gamma^m\max\{1,1/(m(1-\gamma))\})$ where $\gamma$ is the discounting factor. Moving from representation complexity to actual memory cost, we characterize the optimal retained memory for exact continuous representations as $\Theta(nk_L)$ and show experimentally that on a sampled task whose exact rank is full but whose numerical rank is small, a context accurate to $10^{-2}$ is $88.7\%$ smaller than the sparse sufficient statistics of TD. Finally, we establish the learnability of the shared executor through pretraining by proving global convergence of an auxiliary objective over effective attention parameters under suitable task diversity. Experiments across diverse TD evaluation problems validate parameter recovery and the predicted context--accuracy tradeoff.

new Outperforming 1000 Euclidean Layers With Hyperbolic Goal-Conditioned RL

Authors: Timo Klein, Thomas Lang, Andrii Shkabrii, Lukas Miklautz, Yllka Velaj, Sebastian Tschiatschek

Abstract: Scaling self-supervised contrastive reinforcement learning (CRL) to 1000-layer Euclidean neural networks has enabled state-of-the-art goal-reaching performance, yet wall-clock time scales linearly with depth. We find that changing the geometry achieves stronger performance with far shallower networks. This matters in complex navigation environments, where walls can make route distance deviate substantially from Euclidean distance. In a U-shaped maze, we show that the longer the route, the more it deviates from Euclidean distance. Hyperbolic CRL addresses this mismatch by replacing Euclidean distances in CRL's contrastive objective with geodesic distances in hyperbolic space. On hard navigation tasks, inductive bias beats scale: depth 64 hyperbolic agents outperform 1024-layer-deep Euclidean networks while training more than $12 \times$ faster and using about $13 \times$ fewer parameters. We observe that hyperbolic agents behave qualitatively better in hard humanoid mazes, where they follow corridors to reach goals, whereas Euclidean agents try straight-line shortcuts over walls. Our results show that matching the representation geometry to the task can substitute for substantial network depth. Code is at https://github.com/Probabilistic-and-Interactive-ML/HypGCRL .

URLs: https://github.com/Probabilistic-and-Interactive-ML/HypGCRL

new ChemArgus: Automated Scientific Diagnostics for Lab-Grade Multimodal Chemistry Reasoning: A Frontier Benchmark

Authors: Hanyao Guo, Mo Cui, Zihan Tan, Leixin Sun, Yichun Wang, Yinan Shen, Guojun Zhu, Jia Jiang, Erhao Chen, Zui Chen, Wenzhe Gao, Zhihao Yu, Qijun Wang, Yetian Zhu, Jiande Chen, Chuhang Pei, Yao Jiang, Yichen Fan, Pinhan Wang, Xiaorui Pan, Qianyi Zhu, Yang Liu, Mang Ye, Guancheng Wan

Abstract: Chemistry evaluation has moved past multiple-choice probes toward open-ended expert problems, yet the benchmarks that define the current frontier still score answer endpoints alone. Such scoring conflates memorized facts with stepwise derivation, treats visual and textual inputs as interchangeable, and confounds long-horizon planning with stepwise execution. These blind spots prevent any diagnosis of where the reasoning chain breaks in a model. We present ChemArgus, a competition-level chemistry benchmark drawn from chemistry competition material, textbooks, and research papers, whose scoring replaces the answer endpoint with three measurement properties. Fine-grained scoring grades every subquestion point by point with partial credit, so a score lands at the level of the step rather than the outcome. Multimodal capability is priced on every problem containing a real image, paired with a controlled text description, so visual understanding is measured against text on identical tasks. Diagnostic attribution reads paired metrics that share one scoring unit and differ only in context construction, so a gap between two scores isolates a single cause, namely integration, propagation, or local reasoning. GPT-5.6-Sol scores 23.2% on unassisted whole-problem solving against 28.8% and 30.4% under the two sequential metrics, a gap measuring the integration burden. Through rubric partial credit and these cause-isolating score gaps, ChemArgus shows not only whether a model answers chemistry correctly but where its reasoning chain fails, so that every score it reports doubles as a diagnosis, a readout of the chain rather than a verdict, aiming to guide language models step by step on their path toward chemistry expertise, one corrected step at a time, each step priced by the rubric, point by point.

new On the Convergence of Semi-Gradient TD: A Mean Field Perspective

Authors: Haruki Settai, Tadashi Kozuno, Shinji Ito

Abstract: Semi-gradient temporal-difference learning is a core algorithmic component of reinforcement learning, but bootstrapping with function approximation generally induces a non-conservative vector field and can lead to instability. We analyze this effect for two-layer neural networks in the mean-field regime and identify a simple stability mechanism in which entropy-induced relaxation dominates the sensitivity of the moving bootstrap target. Under this condition, we prove exponential convergence to a unique fixed point and quantify its bias relative to the unregularized Bellman solution. We also show that the same mechanism extends to entropy-regularized off-policy control through soft Q-learning.

new Sequential Learning-Based Physics-Informed Neural Network Modeling for Parameter Estimation on a Power Converter

Authors: Samuela Rokocakau, Panagiotis Kakosimos, Giulia Tresca, Pericle Zanchetta

Abstract: A staged physics-informed neural network (PINN) is proposed for identifying the inductance, capacitance and parasitic resistances of a DC buck converter from time-domain data. Gradients are released in the order of L to C to rL to rC, a sequence derived from sensitivity analysis. The five-stage schedule recovers L and C to within 5% and the series ESR to 10%, while a three-stage variant attains the same accuracy 11% faster. new GBTSVR: Twin Support Vector Regression via Granular Ball Computing

Authors: A. Quadir, M. Tanveer

Abstract: Regression models used in real-world applications are often challenged by noise, outliers, data heterogeneity, and asymmetric error characteristics, which severely limit the effectiveness of conventional point-wise learning models. While twin support vector regression (TSVR) partially addresses these issues through the use of nonparallel regression bounds, it still relies on individual samples as basic learning units. As a result, TSVR remains highly sensitive to noisy and contaminated observations and fails to leverage the underlying structural and regional information inherent in complex datasets, leading to suboptimal robustness and generalization performance. To address these limitations, we introduce granular ball twin support vector regression (GBTSVR) and its quantile extension, granular ball twin support vector quantile regression (GB-TSVQR). The proposed models integrate granular ball computing with TSVR, replacing individual data points with compact, structure-preserving granular balls as fundamental learning units. This region-level representation effectively suppresses noise and outliers while preserving intrinsic data geometry, thereby enhancing robustness, stability, and generalization. GBTSVR constructs nonparallel lower- and upper-bound regression functions over granular balls, significantly reducing sensitivity to sample-level perturbations. Building upon this framework, GB-TSVQR further incorporates quantile regression principles to simultaneously capture heterogeneous trends and asymmetric error distributions across different quantile levels. Extensive experiments on synthetic datasets, time-series benchmarks, and outlier-contaminated data demonstrate that the proposed GBTSVR and GB-TSVQR consistently outperform state-of-the-art regression models.

new Trainable at Depth, Solvable at Small Size: What the Loss Surface of a Polynomial Activation Dictates for Designing, Initializing and Training Networks

Authors: Xiuyi Yang

Abstract: What makes a deep network train, and what shape is its loss surface? We answer both with one construction at two scales: a polynomial activation f (z) = z + {\lambda}z 3 , clamped and projected onto the unit sphere, plus an unnormalized identity path. As a deep stack it trains to one hundred layers on MNIST with no normalization machinery; as a small model its square loss is a polynomial system we solve exactly. The small scale explains the large one: it prescribes how to design, initialize and train the stack.

new Rethinking Prediction Horizons: Decoupling Model Queries from State Updates in Few-Step MeanFlow

Authors: Tinglan Wang, Haixu Liu, Shaobo Chen, Nuo Qian, Qi Gao, Kai Wen, Yu Yao

Abstract: MeanFlow predicts interval-average velocities, but conventional few-step sampling ties the interval queried from the model to the duration of the state update. We separate these two quantities at inference, fixing the model, update grid, and number of queries while varying the prediction horizon. Our central finding is that the horizon response depends on both query position and the states entering that query. Across seven independently trained CIFAR-10 checkpoints at classifier-free guidance (CFG) scale 2, a longer horizon worsens FID at early steps but improves it at the midpoint. To probe whether query time alone determines this response, we fix the midpoint query and current/subsequent guidance while changing only earlier CFG. In held-out factorial outcomes on five previously studied checkpoints, the mean midpoint horizon benefit increases from 0.109 to 1.308 FID, indicating sensitivity to the incoming-state distribution. A frozen four-query rule reduces mean CIFAR-10 FID by 2.023 without retraining or extra queries, including gains on five checkpoints trained after rule selection. A milder rule also improves mean FID by 1.041 on a stronger frozen grid, with fixed-rule FID gains on the evaluated public ImageNet-256 and AFHQ checkpoints. However, matched-budget calibration favors query-start shifting in historical CIFAR tests, and the tested Horizon allocation adds no FID benefit beyond Grid+Start on two public ImageNet models. Thus, prediction horizon is a distinct, position- and state-sensitive inference control, but not a universally preferred one.

new A Large-Dimensional Analysis of Least-Squares Federated Learning via Generalized Total Variation Minimization

Authors: Ekkehard Schnoor, Malik Tiomoko, Alexander Jung

Abstract: Federated learning over decentralized networks inherently trades off the suppression of local finite-sample noise through collaboration against the bias that collaboration introduces between heterogeneous nodes. We study this tension by performing a large-dimensional analysis of Generalized Total Variation Minimization with least-squares models. Using tools from random matrix theory, we show that Lipschitz observations of the global GTVMin solution concentrate in large dimensions, universally for any underlying network structure. We provide exact analytic expressions for the asymptotically precise performance under both i.i.d. and non-i.i.d. Gaussian mixture distributions on a fixed network. For the latter, these expressions quantify how regularization balances local adaptation against collaboration. Our theoretical results are confirmed by experiments on synthetic and real-world data.

new What Do Foundation Models for Market Microstructure Learn? A Controlled Study with Synthetic Limit Order Books

Authors: Fernando Acero

Abstract: Autoregressive transformers trained on limit order book (LOB) message streams are increasingly proposed as foundation models for market microstructure. Because the generative process of real markets is unknown, these models can be judged only by the realism of their output, leaving open what they actually learn. We make the question answerable by inverting the setup: we train GPT-style models on order flow from simulators whose latent parameters are known, so that the Bayes-optimal predictor, the generating state, and the exact data entropy serve as references. Measured against them, next-token pretraining on order flow implements amortized Bayesian inference over market regimes. The learned predictive matches the exact Bayes optimum to within a millinat per token and approaches the oracle floor even on a nonstationary family whose latent state drifts within an episode, a regime that requires filtering. The latent regime is linearly decodable from frozen activations, well above baselines, so a frozen-model read-out calibrates simulator parameters more accurately than windowed maximum likelihood at a fraction of the cost. In our experiments these capabilities are not produced by scale: capacity and token volume saturate early, and it is the diversity of training regimes, not the token count, that determines generalization to unseen markets. On real NASDAQ data, zero-shot transfer is set by how well the simulator prior covers real flow: an enriched mixture nearly closes the gap to an in-domain baseline, and pretraining beats from-scratch training at every budget across held-out tickers. Known ground truth turns average next-token loss, which is uninformative near the entropy floor, into an exact comparison against the Bayes-optimal and oracle predictors.

new Exhaustive Canonical Skeletons Generation: A Formal Dataset for Decomposable Symbolic Regression

Authors: Giorgio Morales, Anik\'o Ek\'art

Abstract: Neural symbolic regression (SR) models require diverse datasets of mathematical expressions for robust training. However, existing datasets rely predominantly on stochastic sampling methods, which suffer from high structural redundancy and incomplete functional coverage. To address this, we introduce an exhaustive dataset of univariate canonical skeletons generated via a deterministic constrained context-free grammar. A canonical skeleton acts as a generic structural representation that subsumes more specific functional forms, eliminating redundant expressions and avoiding repeated computational efforts. Although our dataset consists of univariate skeletons, it is designed to support the decomposable SR paradigm to solve multivariate SR problems without assuming linear separability. Furthermore, we pair this dataset with a sampling strategy that generates clean numerical data without triggering numerical instabilities or relying on artificial protected operators. Canonicalizing two existing neural SR datasets reveals that our dataset is substantially more compact, eliminates structural repetitions, and achieves a uniform operator distribution free of simplicity bias. new From Spectral to Frobenius: When Muon Loses Its Spectral Implicit Bias

Authors: Yongtao Wu, Mahdi Mansouri, Zhenyu Zhu

Abstract: Prior work on implicit bias shows that gradient descent selects the Frobenius max-margin direction on linearly separable problems, whereas Fan et al. (2025) established spectral max-margin behavior for spectral-norm steepest descent methods, including idealized Muon with exact orthogonalization. In practice, however, Muon approximates orthogonalization using finite Newton-Schulz iterations and a positive stabilization constant. We prove that these implementation choices cause Muon to select the Frobenius rather than the spectral max-margin direction in linear classification with separable data. We also establish this behavior for both exponential moving average and Nesterov-style momentum. The key technical ingredient is a summable perturbation argument showing that the resulting parameter trajectories remain at bounded distance from those of gradient descent. We then disentangle the roles of Newton-Schulz iteration count, the stabilization constant, and the Newton-Schulz polynomial, revealing distinct asymptotic behaviors, including directions that achieve neither Frobenius nor spectral max-margin. Finally, we show that Muon can recover its spectral max-margin bias with Newton-Schulz iterations growing logarithmically over training.

new KANLab: TensorFlow Playground for Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks

new A Bregman Proximal Viewpoint on Neural Operators

Authors: Abdel-Rahim Mezidi, Jordan Patracone, Saverio Salzo, Amaury Habrard, Massimiliano Pontil, Remi Emonet, Marc Sebban

Abstract: We present several advances on neural operators by viewing the action of operator layers as the minimizers of Bregman regularized optimization problems over Banach function spaces. The proposed framework allows interpreting the activation operators as Bregman proximity operators from dual to primal space. This novel viewpoint is general enough to recover classical neural operators as well as a new variant, coined Bregman neural operators, which includes the inverse activation operator and features the same expressivity of standard neural operators. new Seeing at the Edge: Energy Characterization of Vision-Language Model Inference on Edge Platforms

Authors: Junlin Liao, Harshit Kondle, Di Liu, Zonghua Gu

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are moving onto edge devices, but little is known about how much energy they draw on single-board computers with NPU or GPU accelerators, or where that energy goes. We measure total-system energy, latency, and thermal behavior of Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct and its text-only sibling on a Raspberry Pi 5 with a Hailo-10H NPU and on a Jetson Orin Nano Super, on both CPU and accelerator, and separate the fixed energy of each request from the energy that grows with output length. Because the accelerators use different quantization and serving software, we compare deployment stacks rather than silicon. Three findings stand out. First, NPU offloading makes the Pi a much more efficient VLM platform: it cuts energy per generated token by about two-thirds, leaves the host CPU largely free, and avoids the thermal throttling that the CPU backend reaches in sustained use. Second, an image adds both fixed and per-token energy, but at typical response lengths generating the answer costs far more than processing the image. Third, the Jetson GPU favors short responses through its lower fixed cost per request, whereas the Pi NPU favors longer ones through its lower cost per output token; their energy curves cross near 40 output tokens.

new In-Context Batch Black-box Optimization via Flow Matching

Authors: Quoc-Anh Hoang Nguyen, Xinyu Zhang, Daolang Huang, Julien Martinelli, Samuel Kaski

Abstract: Batch black-box optimization is important for expensive systems where many candidates can be evaluated in parallel, yet only a few rounds of evaluation are affordable. In this regime, effective batch construction must identify multiple promising regions while avoiding redundant evaluations, and conventional Bayesian optimization can require increasingly difficult batch-selection procedures as the batch size grows. We introduce Flow Matching for Thompson-Sampling Refinement (FMTR), an amortized approach that learns offline a continuous, history-conditioned proposal distribution from which variable-size batches can be sampled. FMTR is trained offline by conditional flow matching on Thompson-sampling argmaxes refined toward high-value regions via a horizon-dependent Langevin pushforward map, adapting the exploration-exploitation tradeoff over the optimization horizon. At test time, the model parameters remain fixed and batch generation requires neither task-specific surrogate refitting nor numerical acquisition optimization. FMTR achieves strong performance against batch BO and in-context baselines on synthetic and real-world benchmarks, while substantially reducing the computational cost of batch selection.

new Optimizing Sparse Boolean Rule Networks using Mixed Integer Linear Programming

Authors: Thomas Pammer, Johannes F\"urnkranz, Markus Sinnl

Abstract: We study the problem of learning accurate and cognitively simple binary classifiers that are directly interpretable as Boolean rules. We propose mixed integer linear programming (MILP) formulations for training sparse disjunctive normal form (DNF) networks, viewed as small networks with a conjunctive hidden layer and a disjunctive output layer. Contrary to previous work that uses MILP for inductive rule learning, we start with a fixed network structure, for which we then find optimal weights. A regularized objective explicitly trades off misclassification costs against sparsity, yielding globally optimized rule sets. Our main technical contribution is an enhanced formulation that substantially reduces the number of integer decision variables and eliminates redundant constraints, while preserving exact Boolean semantics for all feasible solutions. Furthermore, we develop a combined approach based on initialization and dropout to break symmetries in the weights between rules, mitigating redundancy, and encouraging more diverse and stable rule representations. Experiments on synthetic benchmarks and UCI datasets show that the advanced formulation improves computational efficiency and achieves competitive or superior accuracy compared to established interpretable baselines, including SAT-based DNF learning and well-known rule- and tree-based learners. Overall, our approach provides a principled optimization framework for learning compact Boolean rule sets with explicit control over accuracy and sparsity trade-offs.

new RM-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Generative Reward Models

Authors: Qinglin Su, Licheng Pan, Zhen Fang, Jiakang Wang, Qingnan Ren, Yunsheng Lu, Yan Gao, Zhixuan Chu

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning on Mixed-domain preference data (Mix-RL) has become the mainstream approach for training general-purpose Generative Reward Models (GRMs). However, Mix-RL suffers from two critical bottlenecks: the reward sparsity induced by outcome rewards, and the cross-domain interference arising from jointly optimizing heterogeneous capabilities, which together give rise to imbalanced capability acquisition and pervasive reward hacking. Inspired by the success of On-Policy Distillation (OPD) in multi-domain capability integration, we introduce RM-OPD (Reward Modeling via On-Policy Distillation), a two-stage framework that separates specialist acquisition from capability integration. First, we cultivate domain-specialized teacher models via single-domain GRPO fine-tuning, allowing each expert to reach its performance ceiling in isolation; then we consolidate these teachers into one student model through (i) routed on-policy distillation, which provides dense signed token-level supervision on student-generated critiques; (ii) domain contribution reweighting, which reweights token guidance toward target domain shares, preventing specific domains from dominating optimization; and (iii) optional criteria context distillation, which conditions teachers on domain-specific evaluation criteria to sharpen token-level supervision. Across multiple RM benchmarks and two base models, RM-OPD consistently outperforms vanilla Mix-RL and other strong baselines, achieving overall superior performance and exhibiting an emergent "teacher-surpassing" effect. These results establish RM-OPD as a promising paradigm for building generalist GRMs.

new Dimension-Optimal Dynamic Regret in Exp-Concave Online Learning

Authors: Yanyi Li, Wendao Wu, Haihan Zhang, Qinshi Wang, Chenheng Zhang, Chunyuan Zheng, Cong Fang, Haoxuan Li, Zhouchen Lin

Abstract: Tracking changing comparators is a central problem in online learning. For exp-concave losses, previous work established the $T^{1/3}P_T^{2/3}$ dependence on the horizon $T$ and Euclidean path length $P_T$ for fixed dimension, while the optimal cost of dimension remained unresolved. We resolve this question by establishing a sharp $d^{1/3}$ curved dimension factor, improving the $d^{2/3}$ factor of recent switching and interval reductions [1, 2]. To achieve this improvement, we develop a proper, full-information learner on compact convex subsets of $\mathbb{R}^d$ that adapts to the unknown path length and, under fixed dimension-independent diameter, gradient, and curvature parameters, guarantees $$\operatorname{Reg}_T(u_{1:T}) \le \widetilde{O}\left(d+\min\left\{d^{1/3}T^{1/3}P_T^{2/3},\sqrt{TP_T},T\right\}\right).$$ A matching lower bound on a diameter-one Euclidean ball establishes the minimax frontier up to logarithmic factors when $T\gtrsim d^2$, including the transition from the curved rate to larger path budgets. The lower bound already holds for losses that depend on only one coordinate per round, isolating an unavoidable cost of learning across directions. Our upper bound follows from square-root Gaussian KL control of comparator movement and a cancellation argument that pays for history restarts. The curved learner maintains $O(\log^2 T)$ Gaussian states, uses one gradient per round, and runs in time near-linear in $T$ under a Mahalanobis-projection oracle.

new HGRP: Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network for Routability Prediction

Authors: Yule Sheng, Yue Yuan, Shan Shen, Daying Sun

Abstract: Routability prediction enables rapid evaluation of placement quality without routing, providing effective feedback for routability-driven placement optimization. Recent machine learning methods have achieved efficient routability prediction through data-driven approach. However, their prediction performance highly depends on the capability of input representations to capture essential circuit information. To address the limitations of existing circuit representations in simultaneously preserving logical topology, physical layout and routing resource information, this paper proposes HGRP, a heterogeneous graph neural network for routability prediction. HGRP models the cells, nets, and routing grids as distinct types of nodes and explicitly represents topological connectivity, spatial mapping, and grid adjacency, thereby constructing a heterogeneous graph representation that preserves multi-level circuit semantics. Based on the representation, a heterogeneous message passing mechanism is developed to enable information interaction and feature fusion among distinct types of nodes, effectively learning the complex correlations between circuit structures and routing behaviors. Experimental results on the CircuitNet dataset show that HGRP improves F1-score by 18.69\% relative improvements compared with the state-of-the-art in the congestion prediction task. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/ShenShan123/HGPR.

URLs: https://github.com/ShenShan123/HGPR.

new MAEM: Mitigating Recency Bias via a Replay-Free, Capacity-Controlled Dynamic Mixture of Experts for Class-Incremental Graph Learning

Authors: Junlin Chen, Wenhao Wang, Junchao Xu, Qingsong Yao, Fei Xue, Li Sun, Xiao Wang, Ruijie Wang

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) excel on graph-structured data but suffer catastrophic forgetting when classes arrive sequentially. In class-incremental graph learning, predictions are often dominated by recently learned classes, causing severe recency bias. Dynamic expansion can isolate knowledge across sessions, but many methods assume task-incremental settings and require task identities at test time to select branches or heads---information unavailable in class-incremental learning. Learnable routers offer an alternative, yet continual fine-tuning the router on new data can bias routing toward recent components. We propose MAEM, a replay-free, capacity-controlled dynamic mixture-of-experts framework for class-incremental graph learning. MAEM builds representations with Simplified Graph Convolution (SGC) and trains a dedicated expert for each new session, then freezes it to protect prior knowledge. For routing without task IDs or a shared trainable router, each expert uses a masked autoencoder (MAE); test nodes are assigned to the expert with the lowest reconstruction error, reducing routing-level recency bias. When the expert pool reaches a fixed capacity, MAEM merges the least-used experts via Git Re-Basin alignment and further distills them into a fused expert using topology-aware pseudo data synthesized from MAE distributions (DeepInversion-style), without storing or revisiting historical graph data. On four class-incremental graph benchmarks, MAEM mitigates recency bias, outperforms strong continual graph learning baselines, and keeps model capacity bounded under strict replay-free learning.

new Retrieval Over Training: Similarity search-based Model Selection for Time Series Anomaly Detection

Authors: Christos Panourgias, Roberto Stanzione, Adrien Petralia, Themis Palpanas, Paul Boniol

Abstract: Anomaly detection is a fundamental task for time-series analytics, with important implications for the downstream performance of many applications. Despite the large number of anomaly detection methods proposed in the literature, recent benchmark studies have shown that no single detector performs best across highly heterogeneous time series. Therefore, a practical and scalable solution is to develop a model-selection method that, for a given time series, selects the anomaly detector most likely to perform well. Nevertheless, the model selection approaches proposed in the literature suffer from a significant drop in accuracy when applied in Out-of-Distribution (OOD) settings. In this paper, we tackle the aforementioned limitation and propose RAMSAD, a retrieval-based framework for model selection in time-series anomaly detection. Given a new time series, our method queries a knowledge base of previously observed instances, retrieves the top-k most similar series, and transfers detector recommendations from their performance profiles. The framework can operate with standard similarity measures as well as embedding-based representations. Our results show that RAMSAD outperforms existing baselines in In-Distribution (ID) settings and is the most robust selector in OOD, where, when selecting a small subset of detectors, it matches or exceeds ensembles that run the entire detector pool, at a fraction of their cost. Overall, we demonstrate that similarity-based retrieval constitutes a strong and efficient foundation for model selection in time-series anomaly detection.

new Adversarial Reinforcement Learning for Robust Planetary Landing

Authors: Miguel Leiva-V\'elez, Nicolas Gaston-Rozado, Andrea Scorsoglio, Hodei Urrutxua, V\'ictor Rodr\'iguez-Fern\'andez

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) policies for planetary landing and other aerospace guidance, navigation, and control tasks are commonly trained under randomized disturbances to improve robustness. However, typical stochastic perturbation sampling assumes independent variations, whereas real mission disturbances often exhibit spatial and temporal correlation and cannot be reproduced through independent random draws. This work investigates adversarial reinforcement learning to discover challenging off-nominal conditions. A secondary agent generates physically constrained perturbations that degrade the performance of a landing controller, forming an asymmetric sequential zero-sum game. Training proceeds in three stages: baseline controller learning, robustness enhancement with stochastic perturbations, and an iterative adversarial phase in which the controller and disturbance generator are alternately optimized. new Long-Horizon RL Needs Muon

Authors: Kai Ruan, Jinghao Lin, Tao Chen, Ziqi Zhou, Qianshan Wei, Zihe Huang

Abstract: Long-horizon agents must coordinate reasoning, tool use, and environment feedback over extended interactions. Learning from sparse outcome rewards requires weighting trajectories of different lengths and updating the policy while rollouts are still being collected. new Harness Policy Distillation: Aligning Agent Policies with Runtime Semantics

Authors: Junze Zhu, Weihao Chen, Zhiyang Zhou, Zhen Wu, Xinyu Dai

Abstract: Deploying a general-purpose model as an agent means running it inside a product-specific runtime harness that decides which actions are executable, how state advances, and when the loop may terminate. Ordinary trajectory supervision shows which action was taken but leaves the runtime semantics behind that action implicit. We introduce Harness Policy Distillation (HPD), which makes these runtime control semantics an explicit training target alongside actions to align the harness policy. HPD replays reference trajectories through the harness to recover the runtime semantics that are implicit in ordinary trajectory data, and represents them as control documents at each model-call boundary. These documents provide privileged information for a teacher to generate actions, while harness verification ensures that the resulting targets are executable under the runtime contract. We jointly train a shared model on the runtime semantics recovered from control-state privileged information and native history, thereby internalizing these semantics into the model policy. At deployment, the trained model uses the original harness interface without requiring the control document or any additional supplement. On TRAJECT-Bench with the Pi harness, Qwen3-8B trained with HPD reaches 22.98% autonomous task success and 49.23% runtime compliance, against 3.09% and 19.90% for the unadapted model. Harness-verified teacher actions substantially improve the native deployment policy, while joint control-state supervision provides further gains in task completion and runtime compliance. HPD turns executable runtime semantics into dense supervision for learning harness-compatible actions at each decision boundary.

new FUSE: Fairness-aware Update Screening against Byzantine Exploitation in $q$-Fair Federated Learning

Authors: Tiantong Sun, Jinlin Liu, Xingru Wei, Zequan Fang, Wei Chen, Qiang Yang, Xiaojin Zhang

Abstract: Client-level fair federated learning, in the form of $q$-fair federated learning ($q$-FFL), agnostic federated learning, or tilted empirical risk minimization, has emerged as an attractive solution to equalize utility across heterogeneous clients. This paradigm grants high-loss clients greater relative influence, while a central server aggregates their loss-weighted updates into a single shared model. Yet, the Byzantine robustness of $q$-fair federated learning has been largely neglected, leaving the server unable to tell legitimate fairness-induced amplification from malicious update scaling. To address this critical gap, we formalize the problem of Byzantine-robust $q$-fair federated learning under an \emph{update-only interface}, in which the server observes nothing but the fairness-scaled client updates. Our analysis under shared class-conditional label skew shows that fairness scaling preserves the nonnegative class-mixture structure of honest updates, while replacing their unit-sum constraint by a client-dependent radial scale. Moving beyond magnitude-based screening, we show that this warped geometry supplies two complementary tests, one radial and one structural, that a single norm cannot provide. We then introduce FUSE, a structure-aware robust aggregator that uses a small trusted labeled dataset for coefficient recovery, scale and cone-consistency screening, and post-screening step-size control. Across four benchmarks and seven Byzantine attacks, FUSE improves seed-wise worst-attack accuracy over the strongest baseline on each benchmark by 3.9--30.1 percentage points, while largely preserving the influence of the high-loss honest clients that fairness is meant to protect.

new Resolution Allocation with Learnable Resizing and Spectral Filter Parameterization

Authors: Wei Wu, Eric C. Larson

Abstract: Learnable downsampling and spectral-domain filter parameterizations have each made convolutional networks more flexible, but their interaction has not been systematically examined. We bridge this gap by examining convolutional kernels learned in the spatial and spectral domains, together with learned downsampling ratios under a cropped resizing implementation. We find that spectral and spatial kernels respond differently to learned resolution allocation, with larger gains for the spectral kernel. We observe this consistently on three datasets (EuroSAT, CIFAR-10 and STL-10) and at two model sizes (differences in accuracy gain of +0.013 to +0.028 on held-out test sets). Part of this difference reflects the greater gain of the spectral kernel from retaining more spatial area, but the difference remains positive when total retained area is matched. This pattern is observed across datasets and model sizes, although its magnitude and dependence on the complexity budget vary with model size and the resizing implementation.

new TANGO: RNA Topology and Geometry Co-Design

Authors: Tianmeng Hu, Biao Luo, Ke Li

Abstract: Designing RNA sequences that satisfy target tertiary-structure objectives is an important problem in RNA engineering, with broad applications in synthetic biology and therapeutics. Existing 3D RNA design methods have made substantial progress, but they largely treat the target fold as a geometry-only objective. This is incomplete for RNA, whose functional folds are specified not only by tertiary geometry but also by secondary base-pairing topology. We therefore formulate RNA inverse design as a topology-and-geometry co-design problem and propose TANGO, a multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for this task. TANGO adopts a staged curriculum: it first learns a topology-aware policy from secondary-structure targets, then transfers this policy to tertiary-structure targets to train a general co-design model, and finally applies frontier-guided fine-tuning to refine target-specific trade-offs among secondary-structure fidelity, tertiary-structure fidelity, and sequence diversity. new From Plato to Aristotle: Toward a General Theory of Representation Hypotheses

Authors: Dai Shi, Andi Han, Xiaoyu Li, Luke Thompson, Jos\'e Miguel Hern\'andez-Lobato

Abstract: The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) holds that increasingly capable models converge toward a common representation. However, recent studies have shown different views on aligning representations via their neighborhoods, spanning trees, and distances. We show that these views can differ because the alignment criteria behind them sit at different points of one lattice, and we formalize this explanation as the Lattice Representation Hypothesis (LRH). Under the LRH, the convergence claimed by the PRH is agreement at the top of the lattice, whereas its evidence comes from neighbor overlap, a criterion near the bottom. The Aristotelian Representation Hypothesis (ARH) restricts convergence to local neighborhoods because calibration keeps the growth of neighbor overlap with model size but largely removes that of centered kernel alignment. We show that this removal can come from calibrating a maximum over layers, so global convergence remains open. Back into Plato's Cave (BPC) infers coarse shared structure from the collapse of neighbor overlap on larger evaluation sets, whereas we trace it at least partly to a fixed number of neighbors covering shorter distances. We show that the spanning-tree overlap used by Structure over Geometry (SOG) mostly repeats one-nearest-neighbor overlap. The LRH also extends to independently trained models, whose training procedure and objective decide the criteria met reliably across runs. We prove this claim for the training procedure in a linear autoencoder and for the objective in a finite contrastive Gibbs model. Experiments on dozens of pretrained vision and language models and thousands of runs of both theoretical models support these conclusions. We hope this work paves the way toward a general theory of representation hypotheses, with a framework to analyze them and compare their evidence. Project page: https://eethanshi.github.io/LRH

URLs: https://eethanshi.github.io/LRH

new Predicting Unseen Scientific Interactions: Conditional Flow Models Hallucinate Non-Additive Effects

Authors: Sam Gijsen, Moritz Seiler, Kerstin Ritter

Abstract: Conditional generative models are increasingly used for scientific prediction beyond the direct training support. Yet scientific questions often concern whether one intervention changes the effect of another. Such interactions can be of primary interest while explaining little of the total variation, allowing models to appear accurate overall without recovering the relevant scientific effect. It remains unclear how these models learn interactions and when these successfully extrapolate to unseen conditions. We therefore study interaction recovery in flow-matching models using a physiological time-series simulator, drug dose-response data, combinatorial cell perturbations, and molecular conformer generations, where we separate recovery from hallucinated interaction structure. In the controlled setting, individual effects are learned much earlier than interactions and models hallucinate interactions where none exist. Across settings, we find qualitatively different generalization problems, with successful prediction depending on whether condition structure continues from observed to held-out targets. When it does, interactions are recoverable. If not, models can generate plausible responses while missing the interaction. We furthermore find that hallucinations increase as targets become less identifiable, and that successful interaction recovery in output space can fail under nonlinear readouts. Finally, we show that aggregate fidelity metrics can conceal whether models learn and extrapolate interactions.

new SpectrumQuant: Spectrum Alignment for LLM Quantization

Authors: Dwaipayan Haldar, Indranil Patra, Azhar Yousuf, Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula

Abstract: Orthogonal transforms have emerged as an effective tool for low-bit quantization of large language models (LLMs), as they reshape activation distributions without changing the underlying model function. Existing approaches typically rely either on fixed data-agnostic transforms, such as Hadamard rotations, or on calibration-time optimization of learned rotations. We study rotation selection from first principles and show that maximizing covariance leads to spectral alignment and provides a principled criterion for constructing quantization-friendly orthogonal transforms. Based on this insight, we propose SpectrumQuant, a closed-form rotation method that estimates activation covariance from calibration data and uses it to construct a covariance-aware orthogonal transform for post-training quantization. Our formulation characterizes quantization-friendly rotations as compositions of alignment and mixing components, while recovering Hadamard matrices as a special case. Interestingly, a class of butterfly matrices belongs to this category. In the realm of LLM quantization, SpectrumQuant bridges the gap between data-agnostic structured rotations and learned rotations, providing an effective and efficient data-aware alternative for post-training quantization. Experiments on Llama and Mistral models show that SpectrumQuant is competitive with state-of-the-art rotation strategies while requiring less than one-third of the GPU memory and significantly less time than learned rotations approaches like SpinQuant and DartQuant.

new Comparative Evaluation of Deep-Learning based DoA Estimators for Real-Time ISAC Beamforming

Authors: Anjali K. S., Manoj Kumar Panda, Chandramani Singh

Abstract: Integrated Sensing and Communication (ISAC) systems rely on accurate Direction-of-Arrival (DoA) estimation for effective beamforming and spectral-efficiency enhancement. While numerous classical and deep-learning-based DoA estimation methods have been reported in the literature, their suitability for real-time ISAC operation remains insufficiently understood because practical deployment requires consideration of both estimation accuracy and computational latency. This paper presents a unified evaluation framework for systematically comparing classical and learning-based DoA estimators within an ISAC beamforming pipeline. Representative classical and learning-based DoA estimators, together with an ensemble architecture, are integrated with Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)-based beamforming and evaluated under identical operating conditions. The impact of DoA estimation accuracy, inference latency, beamforming-weight generation time, and resulting effective spectral efficiency is investigated. Results show that different estimators occupy distinct operating points in the accuracy-latency-performance design space. Although learning-based approaches substantially reduce estimation time compared with MUSIC, the overall processing latency increasingly becomes dominated by the computational cost of SVD-based beamforming. Among the evaluated methods, the Regression Convolutional Neural Network (RCNN) exhibits a favorable balance between estimation accuracy, computational efficiency, and beamforming performance, making it a promising candidate for real-time ISAC deployment. The findings further suggest that future low-latency ISAC systems may benefit from beamforming strategies that reduce or avoid computationally intensive SVD-based beamforming-weight generation.

new Are We Overthinking One-Step Generation?

Authors: Woobin Park, Jaeyeon Kim, Yilun Du, Sitan Chen, Bohyung Han

Abstract: Diffusion and flow matching are now dominant frameworks for generative modeling in continuous domains, but they typically require multiple network evaluations at inference. This has motivated extensive research on one-step generative modeling primarily built on diffusion and flow, though existing approaches often rely on complex training machinery. In this work, we introduce Priming Models, a simple two-stage framework for training one-step generators. Motivated by the difficulty of distributional training from scratch, Priming Models are first trained on a reconstruction objective, e.g., pixel-space denoising at a fixed noise level, before undergoing feature-space distributional post-training. Our central scientific finding is that this simple reconstruction stage is already enough to entirely alleviate the difficulty of distributional training. Empirically, across class-conditional image generation, text-to-image generation, and world modeling, Priming Models significantly advance the Pareto frontier of training compute vs. generation quality. On ImageNet at $256 \times 256$ resolution, we achieve a state-of-the-art FID of $1.19$ among one-step generators using approximately one-tenth the training compute of prior methods with comparable FID. We open-source our codebase at https://github.com/JaeyeonKim01/priming.

URLs: https://github.com/JaeyeonKim01/priming.

new Training Dynamics of Transformers on Unbounded Inputs: Attention Learning Can Lag Output Layer Learning

Authors: Yaomengxi Han, Debarghya Ghoshdastidar

Abstract: The success of transformers is attributed to several factors, including their capacity to express formal languages over variable-length inputs, and ability of the attention mechanism to focus on relevant input features. However, there is limited understanding of the training dynamics of shallow transformers, specifically how to decouple the learning of the attention and the output (feedforward) layer. A natural extension of the theory of one-step gradient feature learning in feedforward networks suggests that one could first learn the attention weights with a single gradient step, and then train the output layer. We show that this fails for a shallow transformer trained to recognize a star-free language from unbounded-length input. We prove that the transformer can be trained to recognize the language with a three-phase gradient descent: the output layer is updated first, followed by an attention update, and the output layer is fine-tuned in the third phase. In contrast, the language cannot be learned by training only the attention or output layer, or by a single attention-layer update followed by finetuning the output layer. We empirically show that ``attention learning lagging output layer learning" is beneficial in other language recognition, regression, and in-context learning tasks as well.

new When Does Task-Aware Imputation Matter? An Analysis of Robustness Under Train-Test Mismatch

Authors: Maria V. Salvador, Ana F. Sequeira, Jaime S. Cardoso

Abstract: Missing data is a pervasive challenge in machine learning, often degrading predictive performance and complicating model deployment. Most existing approaches treat imputation as a preprocessing step independent of the downstream task, a separation that becomes problematic under train-test mismatches in missingness mechanisms or rates. We introduce a unified framework that treats missing entries as variables in a task-conditioned optimization problem. We instantiate this framework in two settings: an amortized approach for scenarios with missing data at both training and test time, and a latent optimization approach for settings where only training data is incomplete. The evaluation is conducted on 98 tabular datasets, and robustness under train-test mismatches is assessed on 36 datasets. Results reveal that reconstruction error constitutes a poor proxy for predictive utility (Spearman rho approximately 0.16 to 0.32), and that task-aware imputation becomes critical in scenarios where classical methods fail to recover complete-data performance, achieving win rates of up to 80% in challenging train-test mismatch scenarios.

new Non-asymptotic Convergence of Average-reward Q-learning with Options

Authors: Kintan Saha, Ahana Deb, Anders Jonsson, Debabrota Basu

Abstract: We study the finite-sample convergence of Q-learning algorithms for the average-reward formulation of Reinforcement Learning (RL) with options. While options allow temporarily extended and hierarchical actions, the average-reward formulation incorporates continual tasks beyond discounted and episodic RL. We establish the first $\tilde{O}(1/\text{iterations})$ convergence guarantees, in the mean-square sense, for average-reward option Q-learning algorithms in both the inter- and intra-option settings. First, we prove that both inter- and intra-option Bellman operators satisfy multi-step span contractions under unichain assumptions. Then, we propose synchronous estimators of these multi-step option Bellman operators leading to two variants of option Q-learning.
