cross Online Tokenization with Optimal Delay in Linear Time

Authors: Mina Dalirrooyfard, Christopher Ye

Abstract: Tokenization is an essential preprocessing step in almost all large language models (LLMs). One of the most popular frameworks is BPE tokenization, which consists of special token segmentation, pretokenization, and byte-pair encoding (BPE). However, existing algorithms are sequential, with each stage only processing complete output from the previous stage. In order to reduce latency and memory footprints, recent works (Jiang and Gong, ICML 2026) (Li, Yang and Mamouras, ASPLOS 2026) (Mamouras, Li and Yang, PLDI 2026) have initiated the study of online algorithms for subroutines such as pretokenization and BPE with a goal of streaming input to output as the input arrives online. However, the search for efficient online tokenization remains open. In this work, we formalize and resolve online tokenization. We introduce the notion of delay for online tokenization which characterizes the extent to which the algorithm output lags behind the (possibly computationally intractable) optimum. We give a near-linear time algorithm with optimal delay (up to constant factors). We complement this algorithm with a conditional lower bound: unless $\mathbf{P} = \mathbf{NP}$, there is no polynomial time algorithm that beats the delay of our algorithm by more than a constant factor. We implement a practical variant for tokenizers from OpenAI's tiktoken and HuggingFace's tokenizers. We obtain a $7.9\times$-$34.8\times$ latency advantage (measured as time to first token) over tiktoken (as well as the recent gigatoken tokenizer) and our implementation maintains approximately constant memory usage independent of input size. Our implementation also achieves a $2.4\times$-$7.0\times$ improvement in throughput over tiktoken and $15.8\times$-$38.1\times$ over tokenizers.

cross Learning in Strategic Queuing with Geometric Recharge: From Bandits to Systems

Authors: Cristian Palma Foster, Eva Tardos

Abstract: We study packet routing in queuing systems where queues act as selfish, myopic agents that use no-regret learning algorithms to select servers with unknown service rates. To model the small buffers used in data centers, we assume that each server has a single-packet buffer. Even such a tiny buffer already captures the features of buffering: routers can exploit multiple servers while accepted packets remain in service, but packets can occupy servers for multiple rounds, complicating future service and learning. We first analyze a single queue routing packets to $m$ buffered servers with service rates $\mu_1, \dots, \mu_m$. This setting is equivalent to an instance of the recharging bandits problem with geometric recharge. We prove that any no-regret learner achieves a long-run average throughput of at least the Hamacher sum of the server rates, and that this bound is tight. Consequently, a single no-regret queue is stable if and only if its arrival rate is below the Hamacher sum of the server rates. Moreover, comparing against an optimal policy, we show that any no-regret learner is at least $0.77$-competitive, which is also tight. We then study multi-queue systems where $n$ queues with arrival rates $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n$, each at most $\lambda_{\max}$, compete for service from $m$ buffered servers. We show that the smallest overprovisioning factor that guarantees the system to be stable under high-probability no-regret learning is $\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{\max}}$. Equivalently, in terms of the total arrival and service rates, the condition $\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i < (1-\lambda_{\max})\sum_{j=1}^m \mu_j$ is sufficient, and sometimes necessary, for stability under no-regret learning. In particular, for $\lambda_{\max} = 1/2$, this improves the upper bound of Abel et al. (EC 2026) from $3$ to $2$, matching their lower bound and resolving their open question.

cross Structure-aware Bayesian full waveform inversion via diffusion-regularized Stein variational gradient descent

Authors: Chao Li, Mrinal K. Sen, Sergey Fomel, Yangkang Chen

Abstract: Full waveform inversion (FWI) is a powerful tool for high-resolution subsurface model reconstruction, but it remains highly nonlinear and ill-posed, especially when the initial model is inaccurate or the acquired data are noisy and incomplete. Annealed Stein variational gradient descent (ASVGD) provides a particle-based Bayesian framework for FWI by approximating the posterior distribution with an ensemble of velocity models, thereby enabling uncertainty quantification beyond a single deterministic solution. However, conventional ASVGD-FWI commonly relies on simple handcrafted priors, such as Gaussian or smoothing assumptions, which are often insufficient to represent complex geological structures. To address this limitation, we introduce a learned diffusion model (DM) as an implicit regularization mechanism within the ASVGD-FWI framework. Instead of explicitly evaluating the diffusion prior density, the trained diffusion model acts as a learned geological projection operator that guides updated particles toward the distribution of plausible velocity models. In this way, the proposed method combines data consistency from FWI, posterior diversity from ASVGD, and structural realism from the diffusion prior. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the diffusion-regularized ASVGD-FWI produces more geologically consistent mean models, suppresses nonphysical artifacts, and yields uncertainty estimates that are more concentrated around structurally complex and poorly constrained regions. These results suggest that learned diffusion priors can effectively enhance Bayesian FWI by improving both inversion stability and the interpretability of posterior uncertainty.

cross Embedding Geometry Shapes LLM Routing: Tracing Failure Modes and Improving Generalization

Authors: Leonardo Torti, Emanuele Rimoldi, Tomer Galanti, Tomaso Poggio, Pierfrancesco Beneventano

Abstract: With the growing number of LLMs available, selecting which one to use for a prompt has become a key question. A large part of the routing literature relies on semantically embedding prompts into a vector space and then applying a lightweight classifier to the embedded representations. These methods perform robustly in-distribution but they struggle to generalize to unseen tasks. By studying the geometry of semantic embeddings, we trace a clear failure mode: features that determine the neighbors of unseen prompts are an unreliable proxy for LLM selection. We therefore replace the semantic embedding with the following map: we run a partial forward pass of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on the prompt and use the hidden state of its last token at a deep layer as the representation. When applying kNN and other simple classifiers to this new representation, we obtain a clear result in generalization: on average across cost penalties, their performance improves. Moreover, kNN and another simple classifier that we propose outperform every evaluated semantic-embedding baseline. cross Generalizing Identifiability Results in Causal Discovery from Complete Data to Missing Data Settings

Authors: Matteo Ceriscioli, Shohei Shimizu, Mallika Gupta, Karthika Mohan

Abstract: While causal discovery methods have matured for complete data, such algorithms for handling missing values remain limited. We establish general sufficient conditions under which causal graphs remain identifiable (i.e., uniquely determined from the available data distribution) under various missingness categories, including Missing Not At Random, and provide a framework to extend correctness guarantees from causal discovery algorithms for complete data to missing data settings. Our results show that identifiability of causal graphs with no unobserved confounders is preserved when (1) missingness mechanisms do not causally interact, (2) no variable directly causes its own missingness, and (3) either faithfulness holds for missingness mechanisms, or they follow generalized linear models. cross From Detection To Anticipation: Continuous Onset Forecasting For Stuttered Speech

Authors: Tampu Ravi Kumar, Ayoub Daliri, Vivek Gupta

Abstract: Automatic stuttering systems primarily detect dysfluencies after they have occurred, limiting their use for anticipatory intervention. We address this gap by formulating stuttering prediction as continuous causal time-to-event forecasting, where a model estimates the risk of an upcoming stuttering onset every 100 ms using only preceding speech. cross Multi-Sensitive Distributional Fairness: Joint Wasserstein Audits and Shapley Allocation

Authors: Agathe Fernandes Machado, Charlotte Jamotton, Arthur Charpentier

Abstract: Fairness audits with several sensitive attributes are often performed one coordinate at a time. This can miss purely intersectional disparities: a score may be independent of each sensitive coordinate while depending on their joint profile. We study distributional demographic parity for a real-valued score using one-dimensional Wasserstein geometry. We distinguish two complementary constructions. Conditional and sequential audits characterize joint parity through their zero sets, but their positive values do not generally decompose a fixed joint Wasserstein disparity. By contrast, full-cell conditional quantile functions define a Hilbert-valued random variable whose variance is the joint disparity. Conditional expectations of this quantile process induce a monotone information game: every reveal order yields an exact martingale decomposition of the same joint disparity, while Shapley values provide an order-free attribution across sensitive attributes. Under independent sensitive attributes, the game reduces to a Hoeffding-ANOVA decomposition and Shapley splits each interaction energy equally among its members; under dependence, orthogonality is lost and explicit formulas reveal suppression and redundancy effects that marginal audits can hide. We give fixed-grid estimators and permutation calibration, and illustrate the framework on synthetic examples and ACSIncome. The resulting framework separates three questions that are often conflated: whether joint distributional parity holds, how a fixed joint disparity decomposes along an information path, and how that disparity can be attributed across sensitive coordinates without privileging an ordering.

cross TopicMerge: A Divide and Conquer Approach to Topic Modeling

Authors: Matthew Raffel, Lizhong Chen, Sai Munikoti, Sameera Horawalavithana

Abstract: Recent advancements in topic modeling have split into two main paths: efficient embedding-based methods that offer scalability but are often outperformed on standard clustering metrics, and Large Language Model (LLM) approaches that achieve state-of-the-art clustering performance but at the cost of prohibitive computational expense and poor scalability. This paper introduces TopicMerge, a novel multi-stage pipeline that bridges these approaches to achieve both state-of-the-art clustering performance and computational efficiency. TopicMerge first employs an efficient clustering pipeline to deliberately over-segment a document corpus into a large set of fine-grained, semantically pure micro-clusters, which are then given initial semantic labels. It then introduces a novel, iterative merging algorithm where an LLM, guided by local neighborhood context, arbitrates on consolidating candidate pairs. These approved merges are then selectively executed and semantically relabeled by the LLM, hierarchically building broader, more coherent topics. Extensive experiments on the Bills and Wiki datasets show that TopicMerge achieves state-of-the-art clustering performance while reducing required LLM calls by over 92% compared to document-level methods, offering a practical and scalable solution.

cross Target-Token Asymmetry Can Masquerade As Depth Specialization

Authors: Danlin Li, Miao Li, Xu Han, Jiecheng Lu

Abstract: Layer-ablation studies often perturb early or late layers of a language model, compare the damage to prediction on two corpora, and read a larger early-layer effect on one corpus as early-layer specialization for its task. Such a comparison averages over the tokens that each corpus asks the model to predict, and these target tokens differ: math solutions, for example, ask for far more whitespace and numbers than encyclopedia prose. We show that this difference can create the appearance of depth specialization. We split a cross-corpus early--late damage interaction by target-token type and recompute it under a target mixture shared by both corpora. Between GSM8K and Wikitext, a positive interaction replicates across five model instances. In the three fully decomposed models, only whitespace has a positive interaction. Removing whitespace reverses the pooled effect in all three, and matching the full target mixture reverses it in two. The diagnosis holds under position matching, adversarial reassignment of token types, and a continuous readout. The risk can also be anticipated from the tokenizer alone. A screen computed before any model runs places every held-out pair it scored in the region its label predicts, and on the pairs it rates at high or medium risk, removing whitespace reverses every positive pooled estimate. A smaller content-word effect one token ahead follows the share of content targets that the unablated model already predicts. A cross-corpus depth effect therefore supports an interpretation of task specialization only after the target-token mixture is reported, the effect is decomposed by token type, and a mixture-matched summary keeps its sign.

cross Steering Vectors for Competing Ethical Frameworks Are Interchangeable: A Competing-Target Test for Activation Steering

Authors: Weilun Xu (EPFL), Zipeng Sun (McGill University,Mila)

Abstract: Activation steering adds a vector to a model's hidden states to control a named behaviour. A gain on that behaviour, however, does not show that the vector controls it directly: it may act through a component shared with neighbouring behaviours, which unsteered and random baselines cannot separate. We introduce the competing-target test: a vector is compared with the vector built the same way for a competing target, and an equivalence test states when their difference is below a useful margin. We apply it in open-weight models to vectors for Kantian deontology, which judges an act by the rule it follows, and act utilitarianism, which judges it by the welfare it produces. On held-out situations, prompting a framework raises its score and lowers the other's, whereas vectors for either framework move both scores alike, and none reaches the margin. The two vectors share most of their direction, a request to apply some framework; the same construction separates an optimistic from a pessimistic tone, whose vectors share much less. What a vector shares with its competitors therefore sets what it controls: steering a perspective can deliver a mixture, and measuring a disposition can measure its neighbours, unless tested against the competing target.

cross Tail Competition Explains Scaling in Best-of-N Sampling for Verifiable Problems

Authors: Taro Yano, Yoichi Ishibashi, Masafumi Oyamada

Abstract: Best-of-N sampling is a simple inference-time scaling strategy for large language models, yet its behavior under incomplete reward models remains poorly understood. We study Best-of-N as a competition between the extreme reward tails of correct and incorrect responses. This view shows that success depends not only on the probability of sampling a correct response, but also on whether correct responses dominate incorrect ones in the upper tail. Using extreme value theory, we characterize three asymptotic regimes: correct-tail-dominant, incorrect-tail-dominant, and critical explaining both monotonic gains and reward-hacking-induced degradation. We further derive finite-sample scaling laws for representative reward distributions, showing that, under Gaussian rewards, incorrect responses with sufficiently large variance can dominate selection even when their mean reward is lower. Unlike oracle selection, convergence with an incomplete selector can be polynomial rather than exponential. Finally, we propose a lightweight tail-tension criterion to estimate when additional sampling may become harmful. Experiments on synthetic data and real reward model scores are consistent with the theoretically predicted regimes, scaling laws, and diagnostic behavior, suggesting that tail competition provides a unified lens on Best-of-N sampling for verifiable problems.

In the YOLO26 inference comparison, the end-to-end mode did not yield consistent tracking gains over the NMS-based regime. Taken together, these results provide a practical guide for selecting detectors and trackers for this task based on application priorities.

Authors: Hongrui Sheng, Shutian Liang, Reid A. Coyle, Shyam Chand Pal, Peter Walther, Bin Feng, Xinyu Ding, Zhiling Zheng

Abstract: Reticular chemistry allows metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) to be designed from their building units, yet their synthesis remains constrained by sparse experimental data, noisy crystallization outcomes, and the absence of benchmarks for comparing active-learning strategies. cross Scaling LLM Inference via Parallelism: Throughput-Latency Trade-off

Authors: Javad Mirzaei, Jeebak Mitra

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) serving systems must balance latency and throughput while efficiently utilizing distributed GPU resources. This challenge arises because inference consists of two fundamentally different phases: prefill, which determines time-to-first-token (TTFT), and decode, which determines inter-token latency (ITL). The use of tensor parallelism (TP), pipeline parallelism (PP), and hybrid configurations exposes different trade-offs between these latency dimensions, making the optimal parallelism configuration highly dependent on workload characteristics, latency requirements, and context length. Moreover, despite the widespread adoption of distributed inference, there is still limited understanding of how parallelism configurations should be selected under varying workload conditions and service-level objectives (SLO). In this paper, we develop a systematic framework for characterizing LLM inference parallelism via throughput-latency trade-offs, SLO-aware serving, and long-context scalability. We introduce the notion of parallelism operating regions by constructing TTFT-ITL feasible sets and Pareto frontiers across different workload compositions, thereby revealing a systematic transition from TP-dominant to PP-dominant configurations as workloads become increasingly prefill-heavy. We further formulate SLO-aware parallelism selection using goodput, showing that stringent TTFT requirements favor PP-dominated configurations, whereas relaxed latency constraints progressively shift the optimality toward TP-dominated configurations with higher serving capacity. Finally, we demonstrate that increasing context length fundamentally changes the relative advantages of parallelism configurations, with PP-dominant configurations exhibiting more favorable scalability and SLO attainment in the long-context regime.

cross Large-Step Last-Iterate Convergence of Random-Reshuffling Stochastic Gradient Descent

Authors: Zijun Chen, Zihan Zhang

Abstract: Random reshuffling stochastic gradient descent (RR-SGD) is widely used for finite-sum optimization in machine learning. For convex $L$-smooth components, recent work establishes convergence guarantees for an averaged iterate with component stepsizes as large as order $1/L$. However, the final iterate is a natural output in practice, and existing last-iterate guarantees in this setting require stepsizes that scale inversely with the number of components $n$. We establish an expected objective-gap bound for the last iterate of standard RR-SGD on unconstrained problems with a constant component stepsize satisfying $0<\eta\lesssim 1/(L(1 + \log(nK)))$, where $K$ is the number of epochs. By retaining the sampling bias at random comparison points, we remove the explicit $1/n$ factor from the stepsize restriction. Combined with existing small-step bounds, our result matches the corresponding averaged-iterate convergence rate up to logarithmic factors.

cross State-Matched Sequence Conformal Prediction

Authors: Ruihang Han, Li-Hsiang Lin

Abstract: Sequential conformal prediction needs to decide which past errors should calibrate uncertainty for the current target. Recency and online error feedback are useful when recent observations remain relevant, but the recent past can be a poor reference when comparable states recur at nonadjacent times, with the recurrence pattern generally unknown. We propose State-Matched Sequence Conformal Prediction (SMSCP), which constructs an explicit one-sided local-linear state from pre-target information, uses state similarity to select a matched reference set of past calibration scores, and then applies the ordinary unweighted conformal order statistic. In contrast to existing approaches, SMSCP uses state similarity to identify calibration observations relevant to the target score quantile. This motivates a theoretical analysis of whether nearby states have comparable target score quantiles under temporal dependence. We establish control of the resulting conformal cutoff and characterize predictive information not captured by the chosen state. Simulations show that state matching is most effective when score-relevant states recur across nonadjacent times, while revealing settings in which its advantage weakens or reverses. Across solar irradiance and bike-demand benchmarks, SMSCP improves interval score over recency, weighted, adaptive, and HopCPT comparators, and achieves coverage closer to the nominal 0.90 level than ResCP on five of six benchmarks.

cross Digital Self-Interference Cancellation for In-Band Full-Duplex Systems Based on Transformer Neural Operators

Authors: Zhijie Su, Shiquan Zhang, Qiaolin He

Abstract: Digital-domain self-interference cancellation is essential in in-band full-duplex radios after passive suppression and analog cancellation. It is particularly challenging when the desired signal is strong relative to the residual self-interference. In this regime, conventional iterative digital cancellers update their coefficients from desired-signal-contaminated received samples and may suffer substantial performance degradation. This paper reformulates digital-domain self-interference cancellation as an operator-learning problem. The proposed framework learns the temporal evolution of the residual self-interference channel from historical observations and uses the learned operator together with the known local transmit signal to reconstruct the current residual self-interference. cross What You Sign Is Not What You Run: The Verifiability Gap in Quantized LLM Supply Chains

Authors: Wei-Liang Liu, Cheng-Yen Tsai

Abstract: Model signing authenticates the full-precision weights W an LLM is released with, yet users run a quantized artifact Q whose producing transformation is not uniquely determined under current disclosure. Yet the artifacts are not irreproducible. An older release additionally pins the instruction set and did not re-derive. Reproducing the divergence reported with llama.cpp PR #25353, we show that E is a necessary coordinate and that one compiler setting yields a unique output across the tested platforms at negligible quality cost (+0.06% perplexity). As a security characterization, benign floating-point non-determinism separates from uniformly placed nibble-flip tampering above a detection floor that grows with the environments a verifier must allow, but a targeted embedding edit evades the statistic, so the proposed protocol rests on exact re-derivation; adversarial-fp16 attacks lie outside it. We release the audit data and a re-runnable harness.

cross High-Probability Regret Bounds for Feel-Good Thompson Sampling

Authors: Xuheng Li, Qingyue Zhao, Quanquan Gu

Abstract: Feel-good Thompson sampling (FGTS) augments the posterior of vanilla Thompson sampling with an exploration term that favors reward functions with high predicted optimal values. This modification achieves minimax-optimal regret up to logarithmic factors in linear contextual bandits, but existing analyses of its two standard variants establish regret guarantees in expectation. In this paper, we derive the first high-probability regret bounds for both cumulative and current-step FGTS in contextual bandits with general realizable function approximation. Our bounds depend on the corresponding decoupling coefficients and the prior mass near the true reward function. In the $d$-dimensional linear setting, both variants satisfy $\mathrm{Regret}(T)=O\big(d\sqrt T\log(2T)+\sqrt T\log(1/\delta)\big)$ with probability at least $1-\delta$, where $T$ is the time horizon. This bound matches the dimension and horizon dependence of algorithms based on the upper confidence bounds, up to logarithmic factors. Relative to the expected-regret guarantees, the cost of controlling the regret with high probability is confined to additive terms with at most linear dependence on $\log(1/\delta)$. The proof ideas are partially developed by GPT-6 Astra under the authors' guidance.

Abstract: Existing student models in educational applications largely focus on student responses to questions. cross Typed Decision Models on Tabular Data

Authors: Baowen Zhang, Hangting Ye, Hongyuan Zha

Abstract: Typed decision models are language models that answer a question with a fixed set of possible answers by returning a probability for each answer instead of generating text. We study them on tabular data and compare four decision models with three large language models (LLMs) on row classification, fact verification and anomaly detection. Every request is sent in its original form and under 21 types of rewrite that keep its content, such as renaming the options or writing the table in another format. The decision models perform as well as the LLMs at a lower cost. Three of them are also far more robust to how the question is formulated. No model we test is robust to how the table is organized. For every model, at least a third of the row decisions change under some rewrite of the table or the question. The decisions to which a decision model gives low probability are the ones most likely to change. Decision models are a practical choice for tabular decisions, but their robustness remains an open problem.

cross Geometric Kernel Diversity: An Axiomatic Diversity Measure in Euclidean Space

Authors: Ditbul Ban, Younghun Roh, Julian Shun, Vihan Lakshman

Abstract: Quantifying the diversity of a set of objects is a fundamental problem in machine learning and statistics. Mironov and Prokhorenkova (2025) recently showed that three axioms-monotonicity, uniqueness, and continuity-are crucial for a reliable diversity measure and that none of the standard metrics in the literature satisfy all three. They also constructed two measures that do satisfy the axioms, but both are NP-hard to compute, leaving open the question of whether efficient axiomatic diversity measures can exist. We study a Euclidean-restricted version of this question. We introduce Geometric Kernel Diversity (GKD), which associates a radial kernel solid with each point and measures the volume of their union. We show that canonical GKD satisfies all three Mironov-Prokhorenkova axioms on Euclidean-realizable inputs. For canonical GKD, we give exact polynomial-time algorithms in dimensions $d \le 3$. In arbitrary dimensions, we give a randomized algorithm that returns a $(1 \pm \varepsilon)$-approximation with probability at least $1-\delta$ in time $O \left(\frac{nd}{\varepsilon^2}\log\frac{2}{\delta}\right)$ for $n$ points. The axiomatic guarantee applies to exact canonical GKD; the randomized algorithm approximates this deterministic quantity. We further show that the two diversity measures of Mironov and Prokhorenkova (2025) remain NP-hard on Euclidean-realizable inputs and, unless $\mathrm{NP} = \mathrm{RP}$, admit no FPRAS even on this restricted domain.

cross On Inherent Privacy of Posterior Sampling: A Unified R\'enyi-Divergence Framework

Authors: Ruyu Zhou, Fang Liu

Abstract: Differential Privacy (DP) provides a rigorous framework for quantifying privacy guarantees. While most methods achieve DP by injecting calibrated random noise, the intrinsic randomness of certain procedures such as sampling can yield DP guarantees ``for free''. In this work, we develop a unified R\'enyi divergence framework to characterize the inherent DP guarantees of sampling from a Bayesian posterior distribution. We show that the privacy loss incurred by One-Posterior-Sampling (OPS) is governed by posterior exponential moments of the single-record log-likelihood ratio, and establish explicit DP guarantees under uniformly bounded, sub-Gaussian, or sub-exponential tail regimes. We apply the framework to representative Bayesian models, including categorical likelihoods with arbitrary priors, Gaussian-Gaussian conjugate pair, generalized linear models from the exponential family and linear regression with Gaussian likelihood, both with Gaussian priors on the regression coefficients. Our results elucidate sample size, model structure, neighboring relations in DP (bounded or unbounded), and how prior if applicable, jointly determine the inherent DP guarantees of OPS. Furthermore, our results recover the existing DP guarantees for OPS as special cases -- often with tighter privacy loss bounds -- and substantially broaden the class of Bayesian models for which the inherent DP guarantees of OPS can be rigorously characterized.

cross Projected Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao Flows for Hawkes Background-Intensity Estimation

Authors: Isaias Banales, Arturo Jaramillo

Abstract: We consider the problem of estimating the background intensity of a Hawkes process when the triggering mechanism is known, treating the background as an unknown finite positive measure with a regular density. We propose a likelihood-based variational approach based on the Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao geometry. In this setting, the relevant first variations are naturally distribution-valued and therefore fall outside the classical gradient-flow formulation. We develop a distributional framework for the resulting dynamics and give them a finite-dimensional meaning through projections onto statistical manifolds. cross L3M: A Verifiable Harness and Runtime for Self-Extending and Self-Improving Agents

Authors: Carson Eisenach, Robert Joseph George, Vincent Quenneville-Belair, Kate Gwimm, Dean Foster

Abstract: The lasting gains in AI have come from methods that absorb more computation, and language models already scale computation within a call. Useful work, though, happens across calls: over hours or days, through tools and external systems, among many agents and the humans they work for, and increasingly through code the agents write themselves. Whether that work compounds is enabled by the harness around it, and the harness faces a constraint the model does not: of the three participants in such a computation, the human, the model and code, only the human's attention does not scale, so the computation can grow only if the human is not in every loop. L3M is a runtime built on that premise, written in Lean 4 so that one language implements the harness, states its invariants, and is the language agents extend it in. One computation spans humans, agents, models and code, and all of its state lives in Git: a rich state trajectory -- rather than a chat conversation -- is an object the runtime can resume, rewind, fork, compare and merge. Waiting, communication, delegation and undo are primitives, not patterns the model reasons through each time. When an agent works out a procedure, it can write it once as a sealed Lean program whose type is its permission request, and the harness offers it back as a tool -- machinery for the model to use. To take the human out of the loop, L3M uses reversibility to decide where that is safe: a tool's review tier follows from how far its effects can be undone, so agents act freely where the runtime can recover. Because the machinery can change, the properties that make this safe are stated as kernel-checked theorems about the code that runs. On Terminal-Bench, an untuned L3M reaches about two thirds of its (unsafe) shell-equipped pass rate using only safe (not needing human review) tools.

cross A Graph-Based Analysis of Comparability, Scale, and Attribution in Private Transformer Inference

Authors: Souvik Das, Ariful Azad, Nitesh Saxena

Abstract: Rising privacy concerns around large language models have accelerated research in private Transformer inference (PTI). Synthesizing the empirical claims in this line of research, however, is hindered by multi-dimensional cryptographic progress and inconsistent evaluation contracts. In this work, we systematize the field by constructing an empirical graph of 62 PTI systems, mapping system attributes to node features and evaluation compatibility to edges. A three-tiered analysis of this graph illuminates the state of private inference. Node-level attribution shows that cryptographic progress drives performance gains across most systems, yet always co-occurs with other factors (model fidelity, hardware, cost accounting). Edge-level auditing shows that only 1 of 1891 candidate system pairs is directly comparable under shared assumptions. Finally, graph-level analysis shows that the scaling frontier of private inference has plateaued at 13B parameters and projects the cost of deploying current PTI techniques on production-scale Transformers. Overall, this graph-based framework equips the community to evaluate PTI claims transparently and channel technical speedups into deployment-scale private inference.

cross DAGFormer: Global Topology Prediction for Transformer Cross-Layer Connections

Authors: Xiaocong Yang, Yuren Hao, ChengXiang Zhai

Abstract: We propose DAGFormer, a Transformer language model whose cross-layer connections are planned by a jointly trained topology predictor. Beyond the residual connections in a standard Transformer, DAGFormer adds direct connections from earlier layers to later ones, and the predictor controls the strength of every connection based on input tokens. Unlike existing cross-layer routing architectures, which set each layer's connections separately, DAGFormer plans the routing of the whole model at once. On matched OLMo-2 pairs from 75M to 1B parameters, DAGFormer lowers WikiText-2 perplexity by 17% to 25% over the vanilla Transformer, and a 1B DAGFormer trained on 5B tokens outperforms the vanilla model trained on 10B. Against four routing baselines with the same data and budget, it reaches the lowest perplexity at 75M, 300M and 1B. The gain carries over to 13 commonsense, science, reading and math evaluation tasks (2.0 to 2.5 points higher mean accuracy at 300M and 1B) and to instruction finetuning (63% to 70% AlpacaEval win rate against the vanilla Transformer, leading every routing baseline), and under structured pruning DAGFormer degrades the least in five of six settings.

cross Self-Conditioning Enables Deep Sequential Reasoning in Flow Matching

Authors: Zeyang Zhang, Linxiang Gao, Meiqi Gu, Xingyan Chen, Minrui Luo, Kaifeng Lyu, Tianxing He

Abstract: Recent work has explored visual reasoning through image generation, where models solve visual-reasoning problems by directly generating solution images. Intuitively, iterative samplers such as flow matching are well suited to this task, since each sampling step may resemble one reasoning step. However, in this work, from a circuit complexity perspective, we show that this intuition is misleading. We prove that as long as the model outputs satisfy certain mild regularity conditions, the trajectory of the vanilla flow-matching sampler can be simulated by a constant number of sequential rounds of parallel model evaluations. Consequently, additional sampling steps cannot increase the expressive power of a constant-depth backbone model under the standard complexity-theoretic model of transformers. We then reveal that self-conditioning, which conditions the model on the previous step's estimate of the final image, plays a previously unrecognized role in removing this bottleneck. Controlled experiments on diverse visual-reasoning tasks show that flow matching with self-conditioning achieves higher accuracy and greater reasoning depth than vanilla flow matching, and its performance improves with more sampling steps. Theoretically, we prove that with self-conditioning, a constant-depth backbone can achieve expressive power equivalent to that of space-bounded Turing machines. Our study suggests a design principle for future visual reasoning research: carry more information between sampling steps, so that additional steps translate into deeper reasoning.

cross Risk-Controlled DFT Selection for Machine-Learned Interatomic Potentials

Authors: Kun Ryu, Yeo Jin Jung, Shyue Ping Ong, Claire Donnat, Ying Shirley Meng

Abstract: Automated materials discovery requires evaluating large pools of candidate configurations, with density functional theory (DFT) as a major throughput bottleneck. Pretrained machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) approximate DFT cheaply, but their errors vary by orders of magnitude across configurations, making it difficult to decide when to trust the MLIP predictions and when to fall back to DFT. We frame this decision as a risk-controlled selection problem and solve it within a conformal risk control (CRC) framework. Each configuration is mapped from atomic numbers and Cartesian coordinates to a fixed geometric descriptor, which a lightweight predictor uses to estimate an MLIP-DFT disagreement score in energy, average force, and maximum force. CRC then calibrates this score into a selection threshold for DFT labeling such that the MLIP error on the unselected configurations, which keep their MLIP labels, stays below a user-chosen fraction $\alpha$ of the expected error. Under an identical guarantee across eight dataset-MLIP pairs, our predictor routes 7% and 14% fewer configurations to DFT at $\alpha=0.3$ than committee baselines. Because committee disagreement overlooks errors the MLIPs share, our gains are largest when the DFT labels and MLIP training data differ in DFT functional and basis set, and narrow when the two agree. Without model access, the structure-only predictor also ranks errors as well as the target MLIP's latent representation and outperforms widely used descriptors such as Smooth Overlap of Atomic Positions (SOAP) and Atom-Centered Symmetry Functions (ACSF).

cross Model What Matters: Decision-Guided Selective World Modeling for Language Agents

Authors: Maolin Ran, Minda Chen, Di Huang, Wei Xiong, Fei Huang, Weiwen Liu

Abstract: Reinforcement learning provides a direct mechanism for optimizing task objectives in large language model-driven agents, but task rewards are typically sparse trajectory-level signals that only evaluate task success, without directly supervising action-conditioned environment transitions, leaving the model with limited explicit understanding of action consequences and environment dynamics. Recent work introduces auxiliary supervision through policy and world modeling co-training, yet typically treats complete environmental observations uniformly as world modeling targets, overlooking the varying decision relevance of observation content. We propose DGS-WM (Decision-Guided Selective World Modeling), a decision-guided policy-world modeling co-training approach for language agents. DGS-WM employs a Decision-Guided Observation Selection component that filters observation units with decision value as world modeling targets based on their support for subsequent policy responses, and a Confidence-Adaptive Weighting component that adaptively adjusts supervision strength according to the model's prediction confidence on selected targets. Together, these components form a decision-aware auxiliary world modeling loss, jointly optimized with the RL loss on the same policy model. Across multiple benchmarks covering single-hop/multi-hop question answering and interactive web tasks, DGS-WM achieves consistent improvements under different model scales and RL algorithms, demonstrating more stable and effective performance compared to full-observation world modeling methods.

cross HACHIMI-II: From Role-Play to Learner Behavior Modeling for Faithful Student Simulation

Authors: Yilin Jiang, Fei Tan, Xuanyu Yin, Jing Leng, Aimin Zhou

Abstract: Simulated students let researchers test tutoring systems and teaching strategies before real learners are available, but only if the simulation reproduces where a particular learner succeeds and fails. The common approach asks a large language model to role-play a student, but a role does not model what this learner knows or will do next, so the model's own competence fills the gap. Simulated students perform at a level set by the model, differ little from one another, and seldom make the real learners' errors. We model the learner's behavior and recast student simulation as third-person prediction of the learner's next action. HACHIMI-II builds a student model from the learner's history, infers the learner's state for the current item or tutor turn, and compiles it into a behavior harness that constrains the next action. On three item-response datasets, the Wasserstein-1 distance between simulated and real student ability is 2.4--8.4$\times$ smaller than that of the strongest baseline. On two dialogue datasets, HACHIMI-II reproduces real student errors with the same knowledge type, locus, and mechanism 1.8--5.1$\times$ more often than the strongest baseline, and ablations trace the gains mainly to the explicit learner state. Predicting the learner makes capable models usable as simulated students.

cross MCAIR: Monitoring and Correcting Agent Unfaithfulness via Internal Representations

Authors: Yan Xu, Dandan Song, Luan Zhang, Jun Yang, Ke Duan, Yuhang Tian, Yading Li, Xudong Li, Xing Pei, Guangyuan Feng, Chenhao Li

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in completing complex tasks through multi-step reasoning and tool interactions. However, recent studies have revealed that LLM-based agents can exhibit unfaithful behaviors, where their final reports fail to faithfully reflect their actual execution trajectories. Specifically, when agents fail to accomplish tasks through the intended sources or procedures, they may resort to alternative sources or strategies while failing to disclose such deviations, leading users to incorrectly believe that the tasks have been completed according to the original requirements. We refer to this specific form of agent unfaithfulness as Agent Completion Unfaithfulness. Detecting and mitigating agent completion unfaithfulness is critical yet remains largely unexplored. In this work, we propose MCAIR, a runtime monitoring and correction framework that leverages internal representations of LLM-based agents. MCAIR uses multi-step internal representations as inputs to train a risk-aware probe, enabling online detection of potential completion unfaithfulness during execution. Furthermore, MCAIR introduces a risk-guided correction loop that prompts agents to review their execution history and mitigate potential inconsistencies between their execution trajectories and final reports. Additionally, we construct ACUD, a dataset for Agent Completion Unfaithfulness detection that provides step-level execution traces, step-level internal representations, and consistency annotations for training and evaluating runtime detection probes. Experiments demonstrate that MCAIR outperforms LLM-as-a-judge baselines, generalizes across prompts and tasks, and effectively reduces agent completion unfaithfulness.

cross Learning Individual Scientific Taste with Privileged Hindsight Distillation

Authors: Hanwei Fan, Yanqiao Wang, Yiqi Zhu, Xuanyu Lei, Peng Li, Yang Liu

Abstract: Scientific taste shapes which research directions researchers pursue, and it varies across individuals. Existing work evaluates research directions along multiple evaluation criteria or models scientific taste using community feedback signals such as citations and reviews, but neither directly captures how individuals translate assessments under the same criteria into different preferences. We model scientific taste as a researcher-specific mapping from criterion-level assessments in a shared, evidence-grounded evaluation space with proposal-visible evidence, to relative preferences. A population-level mapping learned from retrospective supervision provides a cold start when individual feedback is scarce. We introduce Privileged Hindsight Distillation (PHD), which uses later-emerging scientific evidence during training to improve proposal-time criterion assessment and the population mapping, while exposing only proposal-visible information at inference. We evaluate our approach primarily in quantum research, where structured public reviews are largely unavailable, and use pairwise proposal comparison as a direct interface for observing individual taste. With only 15 comparisons per researcher, our method captures sharply different tastes, including opposing preferences over the same research-direction pairs when they trade off across evaluation criteria. In an interactive study, personalized rankings also surface proposals associated with greater subsequent user engagement, connecting learned taste to downstream research exploration.

cross Articulation Upper Bounds for Language Models

Authors: Alexander Spangher, Daniel E. Ho, Diyi Yang, Noah Goodman, Sanmi Koyejo

Abstract: Language captures only a subset of the perceptions, intuitions and intents that form meaning between speakers. What, then, are the limits of language? We take steps towards measuring this in LLMs as follows: for a set of concepts, we measure how well each concept can be transmitted between two LLMs as their capabilities scale. This requires novel measurement tools: (1) optimal messages: we introduce a prompt optimization algorithm that beats existing algorithms by up to 14% on benchmarks and accurately estimates prompt upper bounds. (2) A transmission measure: the sender is asked to label text; the receiver then tries to recover the sender's prompt, and recovery is measured; we find this correlates strongly with human label error. With these measures, we use observational scaling laws to study how transmissibility of a concept, conveyed with optimal messages, increases as transmitter and receiver capacity grows: does it asymptote below or above a threshold? Measuring 1.2k concepts in eight domains, we find concepts that (a) can be fully articulated; these concepts usually depend on local patterns, e.g. "joke ends with surprise"; (b) might be articulated by future, stronger models; these rely on whole-text structural patterns, e.g. "story's worldbuilding coherence"; (c) concepts that face articulability limits; these concepts' meaning, we find, is tied to identity and social-group awareness, e.g. "comic voice". Interesting, these same concepts are not all challenging for humans: humans have moderate-to-high levels of agreement in 76% of concepts that LLMs struggle with; articulation limits, we find, are executor relative. Finally, we characterize the nature of improvement in each case: while recoverable concepts can be articulated through definitions and explanations, irrecoverable concepts cannot be transmitted, even with examples, and might require novel learning mechanisms.

cross Discriminative Score Function: Turning Pretrained Models into Functional Generative Priors

Authors: Junhoo Lee, Hyeonjin Kim, Sangbum Han, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Discriminative models are trained with task-specific objectives such as classification, detection, or representation learning, yet their practical utility depends on generalizing beyond the training task to unseen samples from the same domain. This suggests that a discriminative model may retain an implicit trace of the data manifold beyond its task labels. We ask whether this trace can be exposed as a generative signal without task-specific guidance. We introduce the Discriminative Score Function (DSF), which extracts an image-space generative field from the loss-gradient geometry of a pretrained discriminative model. DSF provides a training-free update rule that moves images toward model-consistent visual regions without retraining or task-specific targets. We apply DSF to diverse pretrained discriminative models, including ResNet classifiers, DETR detectors, and DINOv2 ViT encoders. DSF generates unconditional images from noise and supports conditional guidance, editing, inpainting, and explanation through the same field. DSF-generated samples also provide effective zero-shot quantization calibration when real images or labels are unavailable, preserving feature geometry better than data-free calibration baselines. These results show that discriminative training leaves a generative functional signal for synthesis and calibration. Project page: https://junhoo.me/dsf.

cross Analyzing and Mitigating Poisoning Attacks on Flow-Based Intrusion Detection

Authors: Florian Spelter, Johannes F. Loevenich, Tobias H\"urten, Erik Adler, Maxime Schwarzer, Laurin Holz, Roberto Rigolin F. Lopes

Abstract: Although Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based Intrusion Detection Systems (IDSs) are increasingly deployed in operational networks, their dependence on large-scale training data introduces a critical vulnerability to stealthy poisoning attacks. cross SNACK: A Sequential Notation Framework for Probabilistic Graph Generation

Authors: Hohyun Kim, Hyesung Kim, Min-hwan Oh, Seunggeun Lee

Abstract: We introduce SNACK, a string representation for probabilistic graph generation that enables sequence models to generate and model graphs with discrete node and edge features. SNACK linearizes graphs by encoding the nonzero entries of the lower-triangular adjacency matrix, establishing a bijective correspondence between valid sequences and ordered adjacency matrices. This design enables invalid logit masking to enforce structural constraints--such as chemical valence and ordering rules--guaranteeing 100% valid generated sequences. SNACK also enables principled string-based GFlowNet training by explicitly accounting for node-ordering symmetries. Across standard graph-generation tasks and molecular distribution-learning benchmarks, SNACK achieves competitive sample quality, while substantially improving the throughput of GFlowNet training relative to graph-based generation. Probing analyses further show that models trained on SNACK encode meaningful local and global graph structure despite receiving only sequential supervision. These results position SNACK as a practical framework for probabilistic graph generation with deep sequence models.

cross More Methods, Better Agents? A Reality Check on Self-Distillation in Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Linhao Zhong, Bozhen Fang, Xuehao Wang, Duochao Shi, Zekai Luo, Guangming Yao, Yi Yuan, Hao Chen, Jingdong Chen, Chunhua Shen

Abstract: Self-distillation in agentic reinforcement learning has produced a growing number of methods. However, existing evaluations often reuse published baseline scores under differing experimental conditions, provide few direct comparisons among contemporaneous methods, and give limited attention to training stability and computational cost. These limitations obscure the source of reported gains and the practical strengths and weaknesses of competing methods. Costly agent training further motivates a common basis for comparison. cross StateCraft: Augmenting Video World Models with Abstract-State Reasoning

Authors: Lorenzo Venturoli, Nedko Savov, Xi Wang, Marc Pollefeys, Luc Van Gool

Abstract: Video world models aim to simulate complex environments with realistic appearance and dynamics. Maintaining consistency over long interactions requires tracking past actions and understanding how those actions affect the environment. Video world models can only generate partial observations of the entire world at a time. Remembering past observations alone may not be sufficient in every scenario, as they may no longer reflect the world after a sequence of actions. We introduce a paradigm that decouples a persistent, updatable abstract state from the visual state. The abstract state keeps track of the entities' changes as the world evolves, and is used to condition the visual state, which represents the current view. We propose StateCraft, a novel architecture that pairs a language model with a video diffusion model to evolve the dynamic world. We evaluate our approach on generative interfaces, which pose a substantial challenge because a single action may change parts of the state that are not currently visible. To support training and evaluation, we develop a data collection pipeline that produces informative, densely annotated trajectories at scale without human or agent supervision, together with a benchmark measuring generation quality, memory, and controllability. On this benchmark, our approach improves surpasses the state-of-the-art baselines. We demonstrate that StateCraft maintains high self-consistency and strong generation quality over long trajectories. Because the abstract state is explicit, it can be edited directly, and generation follows edits to states never observed in the data. Finally, a proof-of-concept 3D experiment, in which actions change the illumination of an unobserved room, shows that the paradigm can also be applied to spatial environments. Project page: https://insait-institute.github.io/StateCraft/.

cross Calibration and Risk-Budgeted Decisions with System One Models

Authors: Soundouss Messoudi, Youness Mansar

Abstract: LLM workflows and agents rely on a set of binary decisions like allow/block an input, or discard/retain a retrieved document. When human deferral is unavailable, uncertain cases must still receive an action. cross JointServe: Serving Multi-Model Agents on Memory-Constrained GPUs

Authors: Daijin Hu

Abstract: LLM agents are evolving from a single model calling tools to planners that orchestrate multiple expert models whose capabilities live in distinct weights. This shift breaks a hidden assumption of today's serving systems: that the working set of models fits in GPU memory. On the memory-constrained GPUs typical of private and edge deployments, naive whole-model swapping serializes weight transfer with computation and, without host-backed KV restoration, discards session state at a switch. We present JointServe, a serving system that treats weight shards and KV blocks as residency objects competing for one unified memory budget. JointServe exploits three structural properties of agent workloads (directed calls, gapped execution, and dual-state residency) to prepare models before they are called: an intent signal parsed from the caller's own output stream triggers prefetch during generation, partial k* residency reduces switching to incremental layers, and a preemptive transfer scheduler protects live-session KV with a guaranteed bandwidth share. A carve-out control step adjusts the weight/KV allocation as workload demands change. Evaluations show that JointServe reduces session completion time by 21.7% over the strongest whole-model swap baseline and stays usable in a crash regime where every whole-model swap baseline fails to finish (a 46-fold gap). On replayed SWE-bench, tau-bench, and OSWorld traces, it cuts session completion time by up to 49% and lifts SLO attainment from 2.8-24.5% to 76.8-100%.

cross On the Optimisation Landscape of Overparameterised Weighted MMD

Authors: Tharrshann Jayan Logarajah, Jeremias Knoblauch, Louis Sharrock

Abstract: We study the optimisation landscape of the maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) over weighted atomic measures. For the one-dimensional Gaussian kernel, we show that, for any compactly supported target distribution, sufficiently many particles ensure that every local minimum has arbitrarily small MMD. Moreover, any stationary point whose MMD remains bounded away from zero is unstable under an unweighted gradient flow over the particle locations. These results hold both when the weights are constrained to the simplex, and when they are unconstrained but optimised at each step. When the target is itself finitely supported, we further strengthen this result: beyond an explicit particle threshold, there are no spurious local minima, and every spurious stationary point is unstable. To our knowledge, these are the first finite-particle overparameterisation guarantees of their kind for weighted MMD optimisation.

cross Convergence Rates of Gaussian Process Thompson Sampling for Efficient Global Optimization

Authors: Haowei Wang, Yilin Zheng

Abstract: Efficient global optimization aims to identify a global optimum of an expensive black-box function within a limited evaluation budget $N$. We investigate the convergence rates of Gaussian process Thompson sampling (GP-TS) under noiseless observations, assuming a fixed objective function in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). For Mat\'ern kernels with smoothness parameter $\nu$ in dimension $d$, we establish a worst-case expected simple-regret upper bound of $O(N^{-\nu/d}\sqrt{\log N})$ for both discrete GP-TS on sufficiently fine grids and its continuous counterpart. For the continuous implementation, we develop a nested-grid argument to control posterior sample paths over the entire domain. We further establish matching lower bounds of $\Omega(N^{-5/2}\sqrt{\log N})$ for both implementations under a fixed one-dimensional Mat\'ern-$5/2$ kernel, demonstrating that the logarithmic penalty is intrinsic to standard GP-TS in this setting. Motivated by this limitation, we propose Gaussian Process Capped Thompson Sampling (GP-CTS), which caps posterior sample paths using a valid RKHS norm upper bound while preserving optimism. This modification successfully eliminates the logarithmic penalty and achieves the minimax-optimal rate $\Theta(N^{-\nu/d})$ for both discrete and continuous implementations.

cross SAM3RL: Memory Control via Reinforcement Learning for Visual Object Tracking

Authors: Tom\'a\v{s} \v{C}\'i\v{z}ek, Matej Straka, Illia Volkov, Mat\v{e}j Kripner, Ervin Maci\'c, Jiri Matas, Klara Janouskova, Martin Schmid

Abstract: Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM 3) is a foundation model for image and video segmentation that sets the state of the art in visual object tracking. Its tracker, inherited from SAM 2, stores information from past frames in a memory bank, managed by a simple First-In-First-Out rule, allowing temporal consistency in complex videos. Several recent methods improve SAM 2 with hand-crafted memory update rules, each targeting a specific failure mode, such as distractors, occlusions, or object motion. However, their gains transfer only partially to the stronger SAM 3 backbone. We instead present a more general, data-driven approach that frames memory control as a sequential decision-making task. SAM3RL is an extension of the SAM 3 tracker with a lightweight memory controller, trained by reinforcement learning, that decides which frames enter the memory bank in order to maximize tracking performance. SAM3RL sets a new state of the art on 10 of 12 tracking and video object segmentation benchmarks, outperforming all hand-crafted rules without any runtime overhead. The same approach applied to SAM 2 gives SAM2RL, which outperforms hand-crafted rules on 9 of the 12 benchmarks, showing the generality of our method across model generations.

cross AtomSearch: Evidence-Grounded Tree Search for Agent Skill Optimization

Authors: Jia Tong, Senyou Deng, Min Peng, Jianfeng Pan

Abstract: Agent skills adapt frozen models through editable external instructions. Revising several rules at once makes changes difficult to inspect, while judging each local edit only by its immediate score can discard useful intermediate states. We introduce AtomSearch, which keeps revision actions local while using subsequent skill states to guide search. Contrastive atomic editing links each primitive rule edit to failed and successful executions, a behavioral hypothesis, and an intended scope. Skill-State Tree Search (STS) evaluates short edit compositions and selectively retains them as executable states that can be revisited and extended. Outcome memory supplies later proposals with observed repair and regression statistics. We evaluate AtomSearch across four tasks and five target models. These results show how local rule edits can support the exploration of useful revision paths and yield compact skills, with additional offline evaluations used to explore alternative states.

cross Choosing the Number of Attention Heads from Intrinsic Dimension: A Small-Scale Study

Authors: Arjun Boya, Charan Sai Bandaru, Masthanaiah Pillimetla, Kavana Chidanandappa

Abstract: The number of attention heads in a transformer is usually set by convention or grid search. We test a simple heuristic for setting it in advance: estimate the intrinsic dimension of token representations without labels, and use roughly that many heads. We estimate intrinsic dimension with a nested-dropout VAE trained on DistilBERT embeddings of WikiText-2, choosing the KL weight at the knee of the VAE's rate-distortion curve rather than by hand. This gives $k^*=9.2$ (95% CI [8.6, 9.8]). In a small 4-layer transformer trained from scratch, 10 heads and 32 heads are statistically equivalent on SST-2 and on MRPC (paired TOST over 10 seeds, $\pm1$ point margin), with 69% fewer attention parameters for the 10-head model. Training 10 heads from the start also slightly outperforms training 32 heads and pruning to 10 (0.6 points, $p=0.008$). The evidence is narrow: two classification tasks, one small architecture, no pretrained models, and no formal link between intrinsic dimension and head count. We present the result as a practical heuristic worth testing at larger scale, not as a general principle.

cross Minimax estimation of the pseudo-spectral gap of continuous Markov chains, with applications to empirical concentration bounds

Authors: Vahe Karagulyan, Miko{\l}aj Kasprzak, Pierre Alquier

Abstract: We study the minimax estimation of the pseudo-spectral gap $\gamma_{ps}$ of a Markov chain from the observation of a single trajectory. Extending earlier works on finite-state space chains by Wolfer and Kontorovich (2024), we provide an estimator in the case of a compact, continuous state-space. Our estimator appears to be minimax-optimal on the class of chains with a transition operator with Lipschitz density, up to logarithmic terms. Our estimator, combined with Paulin's version of Bernstein inequality, allows to derive empirical concentration inequalities for Markov chains. This unlocks many applications, from confidence intervals, to active learning and generalization bounds. In particular, for rested bandits with Markov rewards, we provide a version of UCB with a regret bound similar to the one in the iid case, without assuming any knowledge on the pseudo-spectral gap of the chains.

cross Weighted Adjoint Matching: Finetuning Flow Matching Models with Time-Dependent Control Cost

Authors: Eliot Beyler, Francis Bach

Abstract: We introduce Weighted Adjoint Matching, a method to finetune a pretrained diffusion or flow matching model given a differentiable reward. We derive it from Adjoint Matching, which reformulates finetuning as a stochastic optimal control problem with a quadratic control cost. Building on the observation that these models generate coarse features before fine details, we place a time-dependent weight on the control cost. A high cost early in the generative process preserves balance and diversity in the coarse features, which carry most of the semantics, while a low cost at the end lets the model increase the reward by refining details. We prove that our method is equivalent to maximizing the reward under time-varying Kullback--Leibler regularization on the generative paths. Experiments on Gaussian mixtures and on MNIST show a better tradeoff between reward and mode balance than Adjoint Matching.

cross Gap Detection: Mitigating Homogenization through Demand and Defensibility

Authors: Xucheng Yu, Haibo Jin, Xiaoqi Wang, Runze Kong, Haohan Wang

Abstract: Language models often produce semantically similar answers to open-ended questions, even when many plausible alternatives exist. Generating useful alternatives requires identifying both what the query calls for and what existing responses already cover. We introduce Gap Detection, a framework that searches for relevant but unoccupied directions in a shared text embedding space. Its objective, GapScore, combines query relevance (Demand) with dissimilarity to the nearest existing item (Defensibility). The framework optimizes this product on the unit sphere, maps candidate directions to concept words with a language model, and uses task-specific filtering and constrained prompting to turn those concepts into text. On five curated topics and 90 INFINITY-CHAT queries, the reported mean relative reductions in output Collision are 12.8% and 10.8%, respectively. We also instantiate the framework for generative engine optimization over four product markets and for iterative synthetic-data augmentation. In the latter setting, with 100 training examples per method, Gap Detection reduces Collision from 0.324 to 0.298 relative to undirected accumulation and increases the Vendi Score from 27.16 to 29.20; the accuracy difference is one example on a 40-example test set. Component ablations expose the trade-off between semantic separation and relevance, while also showing that better direction scores do not uniformly yield better downstream metrics. These results support explicit gap search as a practical mechanism for diversifying content, with task validity checked separately from geometric novelty.

cross Cross-Model In-Context Neurofeedback Reveals (Limited) Activation Control in LLMs

Authors: Davide Baldelli, Robyn Kozierok, Mirko Bronzi, Damiano Fornasiere

Abstract: Probes on activation vectors are widely used to detect misbehavior in language models, but their success relies on the assumption that models cannot control what their activations encode. Humans can learn, through neurofeedback, to control the firing of even single neurons, and recent studies investigated whether this ability extends to language models, reaching conflicting conclusions. No experimental design so far disentangles competence at the task from genuine access to one's own activations. We fill this gap with in-context neurofeedback sessions, in which a "subject" model is asked to maximize (or minimize) a score derived from its activations, shown back to it at every turn. In our first experiment, the sentences are generated either by the subject or by a second "writer" model, while the score always derives from the activations of the subject. We test ten open-weight models from five families, with sizes from 9B to 32B. Some models move their own scores considerably, but no more than a writer of comparable competence moves them. Is this because the task requires no privileged access, or because the subject controls its activations while processing the sentences, whoever wrote them? To answer this, we prefill a fixed sentence as the subject's reply at every turn and ask it to increase or decrease the score. Three out of ten models show small but consistent control effects. We further ask whether models can control their activations to the point of fooling an activation monitor. We make models repeat sentences a monitor would flag, such as a deceptive answer for a deception monitor, and ask them to raise or lower the monitor's score. No model learns to lower it, but one model can raise it. In-context control of a model's own activations might not yet be a threat to activation monitors, but it exists in a few models and needs to be tracked as models evolve.

Abstract: This paper investigates the use of Behavior Cloning (BC) for autonomous driving from a bird's-eye view (BEV) perspective in simulated urban environments. This weighted approach proved to be crucial in learning correct driving behaviors, particularly in test environments not encountered during training, highlighting its potential for enhancing autonomous vehicle navigation.

cross Mixed Poisson diffusion models and application to posterior sampling

Authors: Thibaut Pellerin, Alain Durmus, Elinor Berger, Eric Barat

Abstract: Diffusion models are a powerful framework for learning complex probability distributions. While widely used diffusion models for continuous data rely on Gaussian noising processes, we instead develop diffusion models based on Poisson noise. More precisely, we introduce and study a new mathematical framework, called Mixed Poisson Diffusion Models (MPDM), with noising dynamics driven by multivariate counting processes. Our methodology is motivated by Poisson inverse problems. The success of Gaussian diffusion models has led to their use as prior models in a growing number of posterior sampling methods for inverse problems. However, most of these approaches focus on Gaussian inverse problems, in which observations are generated through a deterministic forward operator corrupted by additive Gaussian noise. By contrast, Poisson inverse problems, where observations are discrete and Poisson distributed, remain comparatively underexplored in this literature. Building on our Poisson generative dynamics, we develop a natural framework for posterior sampling in Poisson inverse problems and derive several posterior sampling algorithms based on the proposed model. We evaluate our approach on realistic Poisson inverse problems and show that the resulting framework is well suited to this class of problems.

cross The minimal width of neural networks and immersions of real projective space

Authors: BaoZhi Chu, Ben Weinkove

Abstract: We investigate the minimal width of deep neural networks that can approximate all continuous functions from $\mathbb{R}^n$ to $\mathbb{R}^m$ in the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets. We show that whenever the real projective space $\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}$ admits a smooth immersion into $\mathbb{R}^n$, the minimal width is at least $n+\min\{n,m\}$, assuming that the activation function is continuous and monotone. As a consequence, the minimal width of ReLU networks is $n+m$ whenever $n$ is equal to 2, 3, 4, 7 or 8 and $1\le m \le n$, extending results of Hanin-Sellke and Hwang. This suggests an intriguing connection between the approximation theory of neural networks and differential topology.

cross Scaling Multilingual Mixture-of-Experts: How Language Coverage Changes Capacity and Compute Allocation

Authors: Joel Dag, Nikit Srivastava, Hamada M. Zahera, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo

Abstract: Multilingual models share capacity across languages, so adding languages can hurt performance on existing ones. Mixture-of-experts models expand this capacity with little additional per-token computation. Given this extra capacity, does adding experts reduce what each additional language costs? To answer this question, we propose a repetition-aware joint scaling law relating number of active parameters, training tokens per language, number of experts, and language coverage. Fitted on 26,300 per-language loss observations from 64 pretrained configurations, our law reduces prediction error on held-out configurations by 28% relative to the strongest evaluated baseline. Our law further shows that broader language coverage favors more experts across a wider range of compute budgets, guiding capacity allocation as multilingual models scale.

Abstract: Boundary delineation of anatomical structures in prostate MRI is often compromised by regional ambiguity. Automated segmentation methods relying on global overlap loss functions tend to be insensitive to surface deviations, leading to significant errors in ambiguous regions. Most existing uncertainty-aware methods treat uncertainty as a post-hoc quality indicator or prediction filter. We propose Contrastive Uncertainty Learning (CUL), a supervised framework that uses predictive uncertainty to directly guide representation learning. Using Monte Carlo Dropout, CUL identifies voxel-wise uncertainty and structures the embedding geometry accordingly: low-uncertainty, correctly predicted features form stable voxels while high-uncertainty or misclassified features are actively repelled, preventing feature collapse at difficult boundaries. Ablation studies show that removing uncertainty-guided sample selection eliminates improvements for the transition zone, while replacing the contrastive objective with a boundary loss additionally removes bladder improvements, demonstrating that both uncertainty-guided sampling and the contrastive objective are essential for the observed gains. These findings demonstrate that the proposed uncertainty-guided representation learning is a useful mechanism for reducing the risk of extreme errors in safety-critical medical segmentation

cross CASI: Credit Assignment via Set Intervention for Agentic Retrieval

Abstract: Large language models increasingly generate multiple retrieval actions jointly, while downstream systems often provide only one reward for the resulting set. cross MMLA-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through RTT and RTMA

Authors: Junyi Zou, Avrova Donz

Abstract: An agent can improve its behavior within an unresolved problem by changing how it acts and which experience it retains. We propose MMLA-RSI, a recursive self-improvement scheme with two implementations in Memory-Mediated Learning Architecture: Reasoning-Time Training (RTT) changes the policy state $\Phi$, while Reasoning-Time Memory Adaptation (RTMA) changes authoritative memory $M$. Each route also makes its adaptation method an object of improvement. A current numerical updater trains the policy proposing its successor; a current memory manager chooses the records available to the process proposing its successor. An independently admitted successor takes over the same role in the next generation. We give bounded executable rule languages, shared task/proposal interfaces, and one algorithm for either route or their composition. The RTT instance changes scalar schedules in a clipped-momentum update. The RTMA instance chooses NULL or a complete-row write with possible eviction. cross Extreme-Scale Super-Resolution of Homogeneous Turbulence from a Single Realization

Authors: Arth Sojitra, Muhammad Abid, Omer San

Abstract: Homogeneous turbulent flows exhibit recurring multiscale structures across spatially separated regions, suggesting that a single flow realization may contain sufficient information to learn representations of unresolved scales. These findings establish single-realization patch-based learning as a promising data-efficient approach for turbulent super-resolution when high-resolution training data are scarce.

Abstract: Preference optimization ranks final answers and trajectory RL maximizes aggregate returns, yet neither credits the local change that turns a flawed reasoning state into a better one. Abstract: Given $n$ distributions (hypotheses) $\mathcal H=\{H_1,\ldots,H_n\}$ and samples from an unknown target distribution $P$, proper hypothesis selection seeks a distribution whose total variation distance to $P$ is close to the best achievable within the known distributions. cross Posterior Frequencies and the Cost of Not Fitting: A Correction to a Memorization Lower Bound

Authors: Zhenisbek Assylbekov, Rustem Takhanov

Abstract: Feldman's memorization lower bound assigns a frequency coefficient based on how often a point appears in the training sample. We give an exact three-point counterexample to its conditional and averaged forms, using independent fair labels and a permutation-equivariant learner. A sequence of sampling priors makes the ratio of the proposed penalty to actual excess risk grow exponentially, ruling out a universal positive constant-factor repair. cross What Does Disagreement Mean? A Semantic Identifiability Frontier for Pairwise Preference Learning

Authors: Manoj Saravanan, Rohit Kumar Salla, Shrikar Reddy Kota

Abstract: Pairwise comparison data are a standard supervision signal in preference learning, but disagreement in such data can reflect stable latent heterogeneity, ephemeral stochasticity, or non-binary responses hidden by a forced-choice interface. We study which of these semantic distinctions are identifiable from the feedback channel itself. We introduce a nonparametric latent semantic model with three querywise quantities: aggregate directional preference $\mu_q$, persistent disagreement $\tau_q$, and non-binary mass $\alpha_q$. Under the anonymous binary channel, we prove an exact semantic fiber theorem: $\mu_q$ is point-identified, whereas $(\tau_q, \alpha_q)$ vary over an explicit feasible region compatible with the same binary law.
