cross The COUNT: Counting N-grams Unlocks Scaling on Host Memory and Training-Free Adaptation

Authors: Ian Magnusson, Noah A. Smith, Pang Wei Koh

Abstract: The scaling relationship of compute and performance for language models (LMs) has been extensively studied; more recently, models are being designed to scale memory use, as well. In particular, adding embeddings for n-grams scales memory separately from compute use, because each pass only processes individual embedding rows, which are much smaller than the storage used for the whole embedding table. However, existing methods train embeddings from scratch, which needs expensive GPU memory for fast reading and writing. Meanwhile, the ordinary memory of the host system is cheap and underutilized by these training methods. To scale on host memory, we introduce COUNT (Counting Offline Unlocks N-gram Transformers), which builds n-gram embeddings without adding learned parameters by storing precomputed n-gram counts on host. We use the corpus frequencies to weight together the LM's own unembeddings of the next tokens most often following the current n-gram history. At a fixed compute budget, COUNT can be added as an additional input embedding for pretraining 1B-parameter LMs to improve validation loss as much as an LM with 57% more compute. If GPU memory is limited, using COUNT to let backbone parameters fill GPU memory gives better performance than splitting memory with additional learned n-gram embeddings. Beyond efficiency, COUNT allows switching in a different table of n-gram counts from a domain-specific corpus at inference time to adapt to a new task without any training.

cross SafeAdapt-Poker: Counterfactual Risk Calibration for Budgeted Adaptation under Opponent Shift

Authors: Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Rui Chen, Daren Zha, Jun Xiao

Abstract: Opponent histories can mislead after behavioral shifts. SafeAdapt-Poker formulates budgeted adaptation under opponent shift as a dual-counterfactual sequential-control problem using only information available before each decision. At each decision the controller asks two distinct questions: what local downside a deviation from the frozen anchor would induce under matched legal continuations, and what future adaptation value would be lost by consuming part of the remaining deviation budget now. The first question is answered by a monotone strategic-risk model over deviation intensity; the second by a paired budget-state continuation model that prices current budget consumption. The controller selects among support-preserving candidate deviations by trading immediate adaptation value against these two quantities, a pathwise cumulative-KL ledger bounds total anchor-relative deviation, and a finite family of frozen controllers is screened on complete paired calibration sessions before held-out interaction. A finite-candidate perturbation bound shows that the allocation objective degrades continuously with continuation-value estimation error. Evaluation covers exact-game exploitation and safety, four opponent shifts, local-risk calibration, delayed-opportunity allocation, direct opportunity-value estimation, value-error robustness, factorial mechanism attribution, budget sensitivity, abstract-HUNL cross-play, and player/time-held-out public-history behavior. On exact Leduc the full controller attains a matched weak-pool gain of +0.0718 with a worst-opponent delta of -0.0126, raises delayed-opportunity capture from 0.6318 under myopic allocation to 0.7841 under the same session budget, and reaches +42.7 mbb/hand in abstract-HUNL cross-play; the retained public-history screen reports 0.5820 action accuracy and 0.9277 log loss versus 0.5039 and 0.9778 for a public prior.

cross Lost in Translation: Why Lower Bias Scores Don't Mean Fairer Models

Authors: Harsh Kumar

Abstract: Bias audits of multilingual language models usually compare a model's average stereotype score in English with its average in another language and read the difference as a difference in fairness. We ask how much of that difference is noise. Auditing ten open models in eight languages, we measure how far scores move when English test items are merely reworded, test every language shift item by item, and check that the test catches shifts we inject on purpose. In all 70 model-language pairs the stereotype score falls below its English level, and for Hindi, Korean and Swahili the drop clearly exceeds what rewording English produces; most of it comes from StereoSet items. Judging every translation, we find that imperfect ones drop more, but faithful translations alone drop nearly as much. Still, a lower average does not show that a model is fairer. An item's English score predicts its translated score far less well than its reworded score (correlation 0.23, 0.27 on faithful translations, vs. 0.48), while scores stay as far from neutral, models agree on translated items, and within Hindi or Spanish a translated item's score correlates with its own rewording at 0.84 and 0.82. The average cannot distinguish lower bias from a different pattern of item-level scores. We also document three common pipeline errors that silently erase bias signal, and release our code.

cross VascuSR: Iterative Anatomy-Aware Diffusion for CT Super-Resolution

Authors: Hairong Ni, Jiahe Huang, Minghao Guo, Wojciech Matusik, Rose Yu

Abstract: Generative super-resolution (SR) can recover sharp detail in computed tomography (CT) images from severely downsampled observations, but the missing high-frequency content is not uniquely determined by the low-resolution input. Consequently, visually plausible reconstructions may contain anatomically unsupported vascular detail, such as missing, spurious, or incorrectly connected vessels. To overcome this challenge, we introduce VascuSR, an iterative anatomy-aware diffusion framework that combines image reconstruction with explicit 3D vascular guidance. First, to provide richer anatomical guidance, we use an explicit 3D vascular representation that captures structural information beyond individual slices. This representation also simplifies vascular refinement by allowing a lightweight 3D generative refiner to focus on vascular structure rather than complex CT appearance. Second, to guide slice-wise SR, a lightweight anatomy controller injects the refined structure into the diffusion decoder, while iterative feedback updates this guidance from each new SR volume. On re-annotated MSD Task08 and HVM datasets, VascuSR outperforms interpolation and learning-based SR baselines in segmentation accuracy (Dice and clDice) and 3D vascular structure (connectivity and mean centerline distance), while maintaining image quality comparable to its SR backbone. Compared with learning-based baselines, VascuSR reduces class-averaged connected-component counts by over 20% on both datasets and mean centerline distance by over 10% on MSD.

cross VASPO: Self-Distillation with Privileged Value Estimation

Authors: Fei Wu, Zhenrong Zhang, Jianshu Zhang, Jun Du

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation provides dense supervision for reasoning models via a privileged self-teacher. However, this supervision reflects local token preferences rather than the expected return of an unguided student continuation. Recent methods combine this signal with GRPO. They use GRPO for update directions and the teacher for signal scaling. However, we demonstrate a more effective integration within PPO. Specifically, we identify complementary positional dynamics: privileged information enhances a critic's value-prediction accuracy toward the response's end, whereas self-teacher guidance is strong initially, drops mid-generation, and rebounds finally. These insights motivate Value-Augmented Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (VASPO), wherein a self-teacher and critic evaluate identical privileged information at student-generated prefixes. The critic computes token-level PPO advantages from predicted continuation returns, while the self-teacher modulates selected positive advantages via bounded probability ratios. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, VASPO improves mean accuracy over PPO by 2.59 and 1.81 percentage points on Qwen3-4B and OLMo3-7B variants, respectively, and achieves the highest mean accuracy among evaluated methods on Knights and Knaves. Our code is available at https://github.com/cii030/VASPO.

cross ActiveEvidence: Decision-Focused Evidence Graphs with Verifiable Acquisition Gaps

Authors: Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Peng Zhang, Daren Zha, Jun Xiao

Abstract: A multimodal agent must decide whether visual evidence is worth acquiring, which candidate or resolution to query, and when further interaction can no longer change its decision. We study this problem under a strict free-metadata/paid-observation boundary, stochastic vector costs, and a frozen execution trace, and treat it as decision-certifiable active evidence acquisition. A Counterfactual Evidence Interaction Graph (CEIG) represents unary evidence value, signed second-order interaction, and directed conditional harm while maintaining explicit score enclosures for unresolved interactions; a decision-weighted enclosure objective tightens the bounds that can change action identity. Gap-directed refinement evaluates only interactions that can displace the incumbent acquisition and stops once an $\epsilon$-decision certificate shows that unresolved terms cannot change the selected action beyond a declared tolerance. The certificate is model-relative: it verifies policy-consistent optimality under the pinned learned score, and semantic quality is measured separately through post-freeze labels. Paired continuation rollouts learn the downstream value of paid previews, an online operational cost filter is separated from independent finite-policy-family trajectory certification, and an acquisition monitor with an independently implemented verifier binds every certificate to the frozen trace. On 640 cases split into 384/128/128 training, validation, and test cases, the controller reaches task score 0.842, grounding F1 0.816, evidence recall 0.873, 1.74 accepted calls, 286.5K visual tokens, and 742ms p95 latency. Dense replay of issued certificates shows 0.992 action agreement and a 0.988 certificate rate while evaluating 19.6 instead of 191.4 expensive interaction terms per decision.

cross PrismaCoder: Pushing the Boundaries of Open Multimodal Code Intelligence through Decomposed Rubric Synthesis

Authors: Qiushi Sun, Jingyang Gong, Yang Liu, Qiaosheng Chen, Zhangyue Yin, Zixian Huang, Hanxu Hu, Jing Xiong, Fangzhi Xu, Haochen Tan, Haoli Bai, Lifeng Shang, Xiaoguang Li, Ben Kao, Qipeng Guo, Lingpeng Kong, Jiahui Gao

Abstract: Multimodal code intelligence connects the symbolic world of programs with the visual world their execution creates. Interest in this capability is burgeoning, as models are increasingly asked to produce charts, webpages, animations, and semi-structured documents. Recently, proprietary models have pushed far ahead, producing interactive artifacts even from a text description or a screenshot alone. Yet their data and training remain closed, and open progress is held back by the quantity and quality of available data and by the pipelines and methods for producing it. Quality is the harder of the two, since a sample must be correct as a program, well built, and above all visually right once rendered. No existing signal covers all three. Execution often returns just a pass or fail, code similarity does not map to the visual output, and task-specific verifiers do not generalize. To push open progress further, we start from the data side with PrismaForge, a hierarchical data pipeline centered on a synthesized rubric that decomposes these factors into checkable criteria. The same rubric serves as both filter and reward: it curates the supervised corpus, then drives reinforcement learning on visual-coding tasks. Leveraging PrismaForge for data synthesis and filtering, we build PrismaCoder-2M, the most diverse open dataset for multimodal code intelligence and the largest to date. On the model side, we train PrismaCoder-9B and PrismaCoder-35B on this data and release both. They lead the open state of the art by a wide margin and rival leading proprietary systems. Our analyses further show that the proposed rubric-based pipeline improves multimodal coding performance across model backbones. We release our code and data at https://qiushisun.github.io/PrismaCoder-Home/.

cross Latent Compaction with Test-Time Training

Authors: Yingfa Chen, Xingyu Shen, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han

Abstract: Long contexts enlarge key--value (KV) caches, increasing memory use and attention costs during generation. Compressing these caches must preserve information for unseen questions. We introduce **Latent Compaction**, which learns a compact document-specific cache through test-time training. We initialize cache entries using reconstruction importance, teacher attention, and key-space diversity. Training first fits attention outputs and log-normalizers to those of the full cache, with reconstruction-selected entries held fixed. It then jointly optimizes the entire cache through end-to-end distillation from the full-cache model. Model weights remain frozen throughout, and training uses only generic prompts about the document, without access to downstream questions or answers. Experiments on RULER, LongBench, and Phonebook Lookup demonstrate substantial gains over budget-matched compression baselines. On a RULER, for example, our method achieves 96.7% accuracy at 32$\times$ compression, compared with 40.0% for KVZip+. The method also transfers across model scales and families, with retrieval gains on both Qwen3 and MiniCPM5. These results show that treating the KV cache as a neural network and optimizing it with test-time training can preserve retrieval accuracy under aggressive compression. The code is released at https://www.github.com/THUNLP/latent-compaction

URLs: https://www.github.com/THUNLP/latent-compaction

cross Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets

Authors: Shubhendu Trivedi, Jacob Eisenstein, Alekh Agarwal, Ian Gemp, Michael Kaisers, Adam Fisch

Abstract: An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. An agent's bid is based on its expected value for the prompt, which is the expectation of the reward for its response minus its inference costs. First-price auctions (FPAs), in which the highest bidder pays its bid and receives the prompt, are a natural way to allocate prompts in such a market, since they are simple and transparent to run, and do not require central knowledge of private capabilities and costs. However, FPAs are not truthful: participants may bid less than their true value in the hope of a larger profit, and the prompt may then be allocated to someone other than the bidder who values it the most. This welfare loss is quantified by the $\textit{price-of-anarchy}$ (PoA). In this paper, we argue that agentic markets are likely to possess features that reduce the price-of-anarchy. The first is a $\textit{competitive floor}$, which occurs when multiple bidders usually value the prompt above some baseline share of the optimal welfare (e.g., because the best models are often distilled into cheaper alternatives, which perform similarly). We show that competition among them then limits the welfare loss, which can be driven to zero as the floor rises. The second feature is $\textit{near-independence}$ of values after conditioning on the prompt, which occurs when most of the predictable variation in the expected value is determined by the difficulty of the prompt. As the value distribution approaches conditional independence, we can derive an efficiency guarantee that approaches a factor of $1 - 1/e^2$ of the optimal welfare. Thus, both features give rise to welfare lower bounds that are significantly better than the well-known general case of $1 - 1/e$. We validate these results empirically using auctions with strategic bidders simulated on the EmbedLLM prompt routing dataset.

cross Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents

Authors: Haofei Yu, Yixing Chen, Yining Zhao, Xuyan Ye, Yuxiang Liu, Zhigang Hua, Yan Xie, Shuang Yang, Jiaxuan You

Abstract: Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $\tau^2$-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.

URLs: https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.

cross Modeling and Understanding Neural Weights of Humanoid Policies with Diffusion

Authors: Huixuan Cheng, Bike Zhang, Shankar Sastry, Koushil Sreenath

Abstract: Recent advances in robot learning have driven rapid progress in humanoid control, enabling increasingly agile behaviors in the real world. However, humanoid control networks are typically obtained through conventional training procedures, while comparatively little attention has been given to directly modeling the distribution of their learned weights and generating functional networks in weight space. In this work, we investigate whether high-performing humanoid control networks can be generatively modeled directly through their weights. We adopt a latent diffusion framework that couples a linear autoencoder with a Diffusion Transformer. The autoencoder compresses actor network parameters into a compact latent space, and the Diffusion Transformer models the distribution of these latents. Trained on networks collected from reinforcement learning for the Unitree G1 robot, our approach generates deployable networks that perform various motions in the real world, including walking, dancing, and spin kick. Beyond generation, we further study the learned weight space to better understand the structure captured by these humanoid control networks. We identify a structural principle in the first-layer weights that consistently reflects the robot's kinematic prior. Notably, this kinematic prior emerges under both Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Imitation Learning (IL) training, suggesting that it is not specific to a particular optimization process but may instead reflect common knowledge associated with humanoid embodiment. Together, these results demonstrate the feasibility of generative modeling in humanoid network weight space, while providing initial insights into the information encoded by their learned weights. Additional results are available on our project website: https://humanoid-weight-diffusion.github.io/

cross Hierarchical Residual Policy Learning for Real-World Mobile Manipulation with Sparse Human Guidance

Authors: Zhefei Gong, Hefeifei Jiang, Zehao Wang, Jiachen Li

Abstract: Mobile Manipulation (MoMa) is a cornerstone of autonomous robotic systems, yet real-world fine-tuning remains a formidable challenge because of high-dimensional action spaces and intricate coupling between heterogeneous modules. At its core, HiRe-MoMa adopts a Hierarchical Residual Architecture that fine-tunes a frozen base policy. A Policy-Agnostic Residual learns additive corrections atop the base policy, while Hierarchical Decoupling splits the residual into mobile-base and manipulator-arm heads with unidirectional conditioning, mitigating gradient interference and aligning corrections with the platform's physical hierarchy. To our knowledge, this is among the first frameworks to enable efficient on-robot fine-tuning for mobile manipulation in the real world, providing a practical paradigm for deploying complex robotic systems in dynamic, human-centric environments. replace Generation of data on discontinuous manifolds via continuous stochastic non-invertible networks

Authors: Mariia Drozdova, Vitaliy Kinakh, Guillaume Qu\'etant, Tobias Golling, Slava Voloshynovskiy

Abstract: The generation of discontinuous distributions is a difficult task for most known frameworks such as generative autoencoders and generative adversarial networks. Generative non-invertible models are unable to accurately generate such distributions, require long training and often are subject to mode collapse. Variational autoencoders (VAEs), which are based on the idea of keeping the latent space to be Gaussian for the sake of a simple sampling, allow an accurate reconstruction, while they experience significant limitations at generation task. In this work, instead of trying to keep the latent space to be Gaussian, we use a pre-trained contrastive encoder to obtain a clustered latent space. Then, for each cluster, representing a unimodal submanifold, we train a dedicated low complexity network to generate this submanifold from the Gaussian distribution. The proposed framework is based on the information-theoretic formulation of mutual information maximization between the input data and latent space representation. We derive a link between the cost functions and the information-theoretic formulation. We apply our approach to synthetic 2D distributions to demonstrate both reconstruction and generation of discontinuous distributions using continuous stochastic networks.

replace Bellman Conformal Inference: Calibrating Prediction Intervals For Time Series

Authors: Zitong Yang, Yu Ming Huang, Emmanuel Cand\`es, Lihua Lei

Abstract: We introduce Bellman Conformal Inference (BCI), a framework that wraps around any time series forecasting models and provides calibrated prediction intervals. Unlike the existing methods, BCI is able to leverage multi-step ahead forecasts and explicitly optimize the average interval lengths by solving a one-dimensional stochastic control problem (SCP) at each time step. In particular, we use the dynamic programming algorithm to find the optimal policy for the SCP. We prove that BCI achieves long-term coverage under arbitrary distribution shifts and temporal dependence, even with poor multi-step ahead forecasts. When the raw forecast intervals are approximately calibrated, we prove that BCI is near optimal for its surrogate model predictive control problem. We find empirically that BCI avoids uninformative intervals with infinite lengths under our main experimental configuration and generates substantially shorter prediction intervals when the raw forecast intervals are poorly calibrated.

replace GLL: A Differentiable Graph Learning Layer for Neural Networks

Authors: Jason Brown, Bohan Chen, Harris Hardiman-Mostow, Jeff Calder, Andrea L. Bertozzi

Abstract: Standard deep learning architectures used for classification generate label predictions with a projection head and softmax activation function. Although successful, these methods fail to leverage the relational information between samples for generating label predictions. In recent works, graph-based learning techniques, namely Laplace learning, have been heuristically combined with neural networks for both supervised and semi-supervised learning (SSL) tasks. However, prior works approximate the gradient of the loss function with respect to the graph learning algorithm or decouple the processes; end-to-end integration with neural networks is not achieved. In this work, we derive backpropagation equations, via the adjoint method, for inclusion of a general family of graph learning layers into a neural network. The resulting method, distinct from graph neural networks, allows us to precisely integrate similarity graph construction and graph Laplacian-based label propagation into a neural network layer, replacing a projection head and softmax activation function for general classification task. Our experimental results demonstrate smooth label transitions across data, improved generalization and robustness to adversarial attacks, and improved training dynamics compared to a standard softmax-based approach.

replace Forget About It: Mitigating Forgetting Without Replay or Auxiliary Objectives

Authors: Anat Kleiman, Gintare Karolina Dziugaite, Jonathan Frankle, Sham Kakade, Mansheej Paul

Abstract: Have we made mitigating catastrophic forgetting more complicated than it needs to be? Continual adaptation often degrades performance on earlier tasks, motivating rehearsal buffers, auxiliary objectives, and adaptive merging procedures. We investigate how far a simpler alternative-periodic parameter interpolation during fine-tuning-can go on its own. We introduce Sequential Fine-tuning with Averaging (SFA) and evaluate it alongside replay-free Lookahead, originally designed for optimization stability to find compelling results. Together, these findings suggest that simple in-training interpolation deserves consideration before introducing more elaborate forgetting-mitigation mechanisms.

replace Spectral structure learning for clinical time series

Authors: Ivan Lerner, Anita Burgun, Francis Bach

Abstract: We develop and evaluate a structure learning algorithm for clinical time series. Clinical time series are multivariate time series observed in multiple patients and irregularly sampled, challenging existing structure learning algorithms. We assume that our times series are realizations of StructGP, a k-dimensional multi-output or multi-task stationary Gaussian process (GP), with independent patients sharing the same covariance function. StructGP encodes ordered conditional relations between time series, represented in a directed acyclic graph. We implement an adapted NOTEARS algorithm, which based on a differentiable definition of acyclicity, recovers the graph by solving a series of continuous optimization problems. Simulation results show that up to mean degree 3 and 20 tasks, we reach a median recall of 0.93% [IQR, 0.86, 0.97] while keeping a median precision of 0.71% [0.57-0.84], for recovering directed edges. We further show that the regularization path is key to identifying the graph. With StructGP, we proposed a model of time series dependencies, that flexibly adapt to different time series regularity, while enabling us to learn these dependencies from observations.

replace Trustworthy AI Must Account for Interactions

Authors: Jesse C. Cresswell

Abstract: Trustworthy AI encompasses many aspirational aspects for aligning AI systems with human values, including fairness, privacy, robustness, explainability, and uncertainty quantification. The ultimate goal of Trustworthy AI research is to achieve all aspects simultaneously. However, efforts to enhance one aspect often introduce unintended trade-offs that negatively impact others. In this position paper, we review notable approaches to five aspects and systematically consider every pair, detailing the negative interactions that can arise. For example, applying differential privacy to model training can amplify biases, undermining fairness. Drawing on these findings, we take the position that current research practices of improving one or two aspects in isolation are insufficient. Instead, research on Trustworthy AI must account for interactions between aspects and adopt a holistic view across all relevant axes at once. We offer recommendations for how researchers can work towards integrated trust, and provide guidance for practitioners to manage interactions today.

replace Rule-based and reinforcement-learning policies for resource allocation in a pediatric echo lab: a simulation study

Authors: Bozhi Sun, Seda Tierney, Jeffrey A. Feinstein, Frederick Damen, Alison L. Marsden, Daniele E. Schiavazzi

Abstract: Scheduling echocardiographic examinations in a hospital setting presents significant challenges due to non-deterministic factors such as patient no-shows, variable arrival times, and heterogeneous exam durations, as well as asymmetric resource constraints between fetal and non-fetal patient streams. Fetal cases involve specialized ultrasound examinations performed during pregnancy, whereas non-fetal cases correspond to general diagnostic echocardiography services. Using one week (184 patients) of operational data from the Echo Laboratory at Stanford University's Lucile Packard Children's Hospital, we estimate patient no-show probabilities and derive empirical distributions of arrival times and exam durations, and use them to build a discrete-event stochastic simulation model using SimPy, exposed through an open-source Gymnasium interface (https://github.com/desResLab/echo_rl). As a baseline for policy optimization, we systematically compare two scheduling paradigms: on-the-fly allocation, in which patients are served according to their arrival time without prior reservations, and reservation-based allocation, in which varying proportions of resources are preassigned. We consider a hospital with seven ultrasound rooms (one equipped for both fetal and non-fetal exams, six for non-fetal exams only) and six sonographers (four qualified for both exam types, two for non-fetal exams only). In this setting, on-the-fly allocation markedly reduces the average patient waiting time compared with every reservation-based configuration tested. Building on this foundation, we train a Dueling Double Deep Q-Network (DQN) with prioritized experience replay to approximate an optimal dynamic on-the-fly allocation policy. The learned RL policy is comparable to, but does not outperform, the best on-the-fly rule-based policy.

replace The Invisible Leash: Why RLVR May or May Not Escape Its Origin

Authors: Fang Wu, Weihao Xuan, Danny Xing, Ximing Lu, Mingjie Liu, Yi Dong, Zaid Harchaoui, Yejin Choi

Abstract: Recent advances highlight Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) as a promising approach to enhancing LLM capabilities. However, it remains unclear whether the current practice of RLVR truly expands models' reasoning boundary or mainly amplifies high-reward outputs that base models already know. We introduce empirical support dynamics, a framework that quantifies how RLVR preserves, expands, and shrinks access to verified solutions. Applied across models, reasoning tasks, and training checkpoints, it matches verified successes question by question to reveal changes that aggregate accuracy can conceal. Across the evaluated model-level benchmark aggregates, \textit{the shrinkage of observed support outweighs its expansion}: more questions lose a verified success than gain one at the stated sampling budgets. These losses occur alongside gains in \texttt{pass@1}. Some RLVR models increase average token-level entropy while reducing final-answer entropy: their token choices become more uncertain on average even as their final answers become more concentrated. Together, these findings reveal an ``invisible leash'' in current RLVR practice: it can make existing successes more reliable while losing access to other verified solutions. Empirical support dynamics make this imbalance measurable and motivate exploration strategies that reach underrepresented solutions while retaining prior successes.

replace Sig-DEG for Distillation: Making Diffusion Models Faster and Lighter

Authors: Wen Ge, Lei Jiang, Lingyi Yang, Leonardo Roveri, Mingxuan Yi, Po-Yu Chen, Niels Cariou-Kotlarek, Francois Buet-Golfouse, Gaurav Mittal, Hao Ni

Abstract: Diffusion models achieve state-of-the-art generative performance but remain computationally expensive at inference, often requiring many discretization steps. We propose Sig-DEG (Signature-based Differential Equation Generator), a generator for distilling pre-trained diffusion models. Inspired by high-order stochastic Taylor expansions, Sig-DEG combines partial signatures that summarize Brownian motion over subintervals with a recurrent structure to approximate stochastic differential equation solutions to achieve universality. We formulate distillation as supervised learning, matching fine-resolution teacher trajectories on a coarse time grid. At inference, Sig-DEG enables fast generation, as the partial signatures can be simulated exactly from their known distributions without requiring fine-grained Brownian paths. Experiments on image and time-series generation demonstrate competitive sample quality with inference speedups of up to several hundred times over the teacher. A Schr\"odinger bridge example further demonstrates Sig-DEG's applicability beyond diffusion distillation.

replace Context Value Informed In-Context Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Wenhao Zhang, Shao Zhang, Xihuai Wang, Yang Li, Ying Wen

Abstract: In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) has emerged as an effective paradigm for test time adaptation to unseen tasks without parameter updates. However, existing ICRL methods can exhibit brittle and unstable adaptions, and the mechanisms underlying such adaptions remain poorly understood. We provide a policy-gradient view of ICRL and argue that relying on trajectory-level return feedback can lead to high-variance updates when test time rollout is limited. Motivated by the actor--critic principle, we propose Context Value Informed ICRL (CV-ICRL), which equips the in-context policy with an explicit context value: the expected discounted return conditioned on the current state and accumulated interaction context. CV-ICRL trains a value head and writes its prediction back into the context as a value token, enabling TD-style return targets for lower-variance policy updates. Experiments on the Dark Room, Minigrid, and Procgen testbeds show that CV-ICRL substantially improves the stability of test time adaptation and achieves higher returns across tasks and environments. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Bluixe/CV-ICRL .

replace A Byzantine-Robust Federated Learning with Nesterov Momentum

Authors: Lihan Xu, Xiaoyi Fan, Gang Wang, Runhao Zeng, Xiping Hu, Yanjie Dong

Abstract: We investigate robust federated learning, where a group of workers collaboratively train a shared model under the orchestration of a central server in the presence of Byzantine adversaries capable of arbitrary and potentially malicious behaviors. To improve communication efficiency while retaining resilience against such adversaries, we propose a Byzantine-resilient Nesterov-augmented federated learning (Byrd-NAFL) algorithm. Byrd-NAFL applies Nesterov's momentum at the server to the output of any soft $\gamma$-Byzantine resilient aggregation rule, while each worker only computes and uploads a stochastic gradient, which keeps the workers stateless, as desired for intermittently participating Internet of Things devices. For smooth non-convex losses, we establish a finite-time convergence guarantee for all momentum factors $\beta\in[0,1)$ without requiring the aggregated gradient to be unbiased or bounded; the bound reduces to that of Byzantine-resilient stochastic gradient descent at $\beta=0$ and is tighter than that of server-side heavy-ball momentum. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Sentiment140 show that Byrd-NAFL converges faster and attains higher accuracy than existing benchmarks in most i.i.d. and non-i.i.d. setups under diverse attacks, while its resilience is bounded by that of the underlying aggregation rule.

replace Ground-Truth Subgraphs for Better Training and Evaluation of Knowledge Graph Augmented LLMs

Authors: Alberto Cattaneo, Carlo Luschi, Daniel Justus

Abstract: Retrieval of information from graph-structured knowledge bases represents a promising direction for improving the factuality of LLMs. While various solutions have been proposed, a comparison of methods is difficult due to the lack of challenging QA datasets with ground-truth targets for graph retrieval. We present SynthKGQA, an LLM-powered framework for generating high-quality Knowledge Graph Question Answering datasets from any Knowledge Graph, providing the full set of ground-truth facts in the KG to reason over questions. To demonstrate its utility, we apply SynthKGQA to ogbl-wikikg2, a Wikidata-derived KG, and validate generated items against an up-to-date dump of Wikidata to create GTSQA. This new dataset is specifically designed to test zero-shot generalization with respect to unseen graph structures and relation types, enabling us to analyze the abilities and limitations of SOTA graph retrieval approaches at an unprecedented level of granularity. We also show that KG retrievers trained on GTSQA can transfer to a human-curated benchmark, and that the ground-truth subgraphs produced by SynthKGQA provide a better training supervision signal than previously-used heuristics.

replace A Versatile Variational Quantum Kernel Framework for Non-Trivial Classification

Authors: Jiang Yuhan, Matthew Otten

Abstract: Quantum kernel methods are a promising branch of quantum machine learning, but their per- formance across data types and encoding choices remains incompletely understood. To address this gap, we developed an algorithmic framework for variational quantum kernels utilizing resource- efficient ans\"atze for complex classification tasks and introduced a parameter scaling technique to accelerate convergence. We conducted a comprehensive benchmark of this framework on eight chal- lenging, real-world and high-dimensional datasets covering tabular, image, time series, and graph data. Our results show that the proposed quantum kernel demonstrated competitive classification accuracy compared to standard classical kernels in classical simulation, such as the radial basis function (RBF) kernel. This work demonstrates that properly designed quantum kernels can func- tion as versatile, high-performance tools, laying a foundation for quantum-enhanced applications in machine learning. Further research is needed to fully assess the practical performance of quantum methods.

replace The Instability of Safety: How Random Seeds and Temperature Expose Inconsistent LLM Refusal Behavior

Authors: Erik Larsen

Abstract: Current safety evaluations of large language models rely on single-shot testing, implicitly assuming that model responses are deterministic and representative of the model's safety alignment. We challenge this assumption by investigating the stability of safety refusal decisions across random seeds and temperature settings. Testing four instruction-tuned models from three families (Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen 2.5 7B, Qwen 3 8B, Gemma 3 12B) on 876 harmful prompts across 20 sampling configurations (4 temperatures x 5 seeds), we find that 18-28% of prompts exhibit decision flips (the REFUSE/PARTIAL/COMPLY label changes across configurations), depending on the model. Our Safety Stability Index (SSI) reveals that higher temperatures significantly reduce decision stability (Friedman chi-squared = 396.81, p < 0.001), with mean within-temperature SSI dropping from 0.977 at temperature 0.0 to 0.942 at temperature 1.0. We validate findings across all model families using Claude 3.5 Haiku as a unified external judge, achieving 89.1% inter-judge agreement with the Llama 70B judge on the two models both judges labeled (Cohen's kappa = 0.62). Within each model, prompts with higher compliance rates exhibit lower stability (Spearman rho = -0.47 to -0.70, all p < 0.001), indicating that models "waver" more on borderline requests. These findings demonstrate that single-shot safety evaluations are insufficient for reliable safety assessment and that evaluation protocols must account for stochastic variation in model behavior. For Llama 3.1 8B, single-shot evaluation agrees with multi-sample ground truth only 92.5% of the time when pooling across temperatures (98.7% at greedy to 90.3% at temperature 1.0), and we recommend scaling samples with temperature--one at greedy, three at low temperature, more at higher temperatures (where three reach only ~95%), and ten when pooling--rather than a single flat threshold.

replace Long-Range Distillation: Distilling 10,000 Years of Simulated Climate into Long Timestep AI Weather Models

Authors: Scott A. Martin, Noah Brenowitz, Dale Durran, Michael Pritchard

Abstract: Accurate long-range weather forecasting remains a major challenge for AI models, both because errors accumulate over autoregressive rollouts and because reanalysis training datasets provide limited sampling of the slow modes of variability underpinning predictability. Most AI and all numerical weather prediction models are autoregressive, repeatedly applying short-lead forecasts to reach subseasonal-to-seasonal (S2S) or seasonal lead times, often causing instability and miscalibration. Long-timestep probabilistic models that generate long-range forecasts in a single step offer an alternative, but training on the 40-year reanalysis record leads to overfitting, suggesting orders of magnitude more data are required. We introduce long-range distillation, whereby a long-timestep probabilistic ``student'' model is trained to forecast directly at long-range using a huge synthetic dataset generated by an autoregressive ``teacher'' model. Using the Deep Learning Earth System Model (DLESyM) as teacher, we generate over 10,000 years of simulated climate to train student models across a range of timescales. In perfect-model experiments, the distilled models outperform climatology and approach their teacher's skill while replacing hundreds of autoregressive steps with a single timestep. In the real world, both a diffusion model student fine-tuned on ERA5 and a linear inverse model student with mixed DLESyM-ERA5 training achieve S2S forecast skill competitive with the ECMWF IFS ensemble, with synthetic pre-training providing improvements beyond ERA5-only training. This demonstrates the benefit of synthetic pre-training for both deep learning and classical data-driven methods, and represents the first demonstration that real-world skill can be improved by training on AI-generated synthetic data in volumes far exceeding the observational record.

replace Tree-Structured Synergy of Large Language Models and Bayesian Optimization for Efficient CASH

Authors: Beicheng Xu, Weitong Qian, Lingching Tung, Yupeng Lu, Bin Cui

Abstract: To lower the expertise barrier in machine learning, the AutoML community has focused on the CASH problem, which jointly automates algorithm selection and hyperparameter tuning. While traditional methods like Bayesian Optimization (BO) struggle with cold-start issues, Large Language Models (LLMs) can mitigate these through semantic priors. However, existing LLM-based optimizers generalize poorly to high-dimensional, structured CASH spaces. CASH requires coordinated exploration and exploitation both across candidate algorithms and within their algorithm-specific hyperparameter spaces. Yet existing approaches often rely on flat optimization histories that mix observations across algorithms and obscure configuration-refinement trajectories. In this paper, we propose LB-MCTS, a trajectory-structured optimization framework that uses a Monte Carlo Tree Search tree as a shared state for algorithm selection, hyperparameter refinement, and BO-LLM proposer synergy. Within this shared state, BO provides algorithm-specific surrogate modeling for quantitative search, while the LLM exploits path-aware selective memory to generate semantic proposals and reflections. As the surrogate model improves, a reliability-aware proposer policy adaptively shifts from LLM-driven to BO-driven proposals within a unified search trajectory. Experiments on 104 AMLB datasets demonstrate that LB-MCTS consistently outperforms BO-based, LLM-based, and hybrid baselines. Code: https://github.com/PKU-DAIR/open-box.

URLs: https://github.com/PKU-DAIR/open-box.

replace Interpolation of GEDI Biomass Estimates with Calibrated Uncertainty Quantification

Authors: Robin Young, Srinivasan Keshav

Abstract: Wall-to-wall biomass density estimation from NASA's GEDI mission requires interpolating sparse LIDAR observations across heterogeneous landscapes. While Random Forest and XGBoost are widely used, they predict each location independently from satellite features, without adapting to the varying difficulty of heterogeneous landscapes. We introduce Attentive Neural Processes (ANPs), a probabilistic meta-learning architecture that explicitly conditions predictions on local observation sets as well as geospatial foundation model embeddings. ANPs learn a flexible spatial covariance approximator that is more uncertain in complex landscapes and less in homogeneous areas. replace SWE-Spot: Building Small Repo-Experts with Repository-Centric Learning

Authors: Jinjun Peng, Magnus Saebo, Tianjun Zhong, Junfeng Yang, Shi-Xiong Zhang, Baishakhi Ray, Simin Chen, Yangruibo Ding

Abstract: The deployment of coding agents in privacy-sensitive and resource-constrained environments drives the demand for capable open-weight Small Language Models (SLMs). However, they suffer from a fundamental capability gap: unlike frontier large models, they lack the inference-time strong generalization to handle complicated, unfamiliar codebases. We identify that the prevailing Task-Centric Learning (TCL) approach, which scales exposure across disparate repositories, fails to address this limitation. In response, we propose Repository-Centric Learning (RCL), a shift that prioritizes vertical, single-repository depth over horizontal, single-task breadth for SLM training. To accomplish this depth, we design a four-unit Repository-Centric Experience, transforming static codebases into interactive learning signals, to train SWE-Spot-4B, a family of highly compact models built as repo-specialized experts. Under controlled comparisons with the same teacher and data budget, RCL consistently outperforms TCL across tasks, teachers, model scales, and programming languages. On seven target repositories, SWE-Spot-4B experts match or exceed existing open-weight models up to $8\times$ larger (e.g., CWM by Meta, Qwen3-Coder-30B) and surpass or match efficiency-focused commercial models (e.g., GPT-4.1-mini, GPT-5-nano) across multiple SWE tasks. Further analysis reveals that RCL yields higher training and inference efficiency, deep knowledge acquisition, and can scale to repository-cluster experts. Our work emphasizes that for building efficient intelligence, repository mastery is a distinct, necessary, and practical dimension that complements general coding capability.

replace Deep Time-series Forecasting Needs Kernelized Moment Balancing

Authors: Licheng Pan, Haocheng Yang, Shuting He, Han Zheng, Yuzhou Chen, Guangyi Liu, Kangning Zhang, Zhixuan Chu, Yuan Lu, Hao Wang

Abstract: Deep time-series forecasting can be formulated as a distribution balancing problem aimed at aligning the distribution of the forecasts and ground truths. According to Imbens' criterion, balancing two distributions requires that their first moments be identical with respect to any balancing function. We demonstrate that existing objectives enforce moment matching only for one or two predefined balancing functions, thus failing to achieve full distribution balance. To address this limitation, we propose direct forecasting with kernelized moment balancing (KMB-DF). Unlike existing objectives, KMB-DF adaptively selects the most informative balancing functions from a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) to enforce sufficient distribution balancing. A tractable and differentiable formulation is derived to enable estimation from empirical samples and seamless integration into gradient-based training pipelines. Extensive experiments across multiple models and datasets show that KMB-DF consistently improves forecasting accuracy and achieves state-of-the-art performance. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KMB-DF-403C.

URLs: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KMB-DF-403C.

replace Rational Transductors

Authors: Mehryar Mohri

Abstract: Standard Transformers excel at semantic modeling but struggle with rigid sequential logic and state tracking. Theoretical work establishes that self-attention is limited to $\AC^0$ (under hard attention) or $\TC^0$ (under soft attention), complexity classes that often fail to support robust length generalization on sequential problems without intermediate chain-of-thought \citep{hahn2020theoretical, hao2022formal, merrill2022saturated}. In this work, we introduce \emph{Rational Transductors}, a dual-stream architecture that augments the Transformer with a matrix-valued recurrence derived from Weighted Finite Automata (WFA). By injecting rational state information into the attention mechanism via a \emph{Deep Rational Injection} scheme, our framework strictly generalizes the expressive power of Transformers to capture all Regular Languages, $\NC^1$-complete languages (such as the word problem of $S_5$), and fundamental separations like Parity and Modular Counting, while preserving $O(\log T)$ parallel training efficiency. We ground the architecture in a rigorous learning theory: we prove that \emph{Random Rational Features} act as a universal basis for sequential dependencies, justifying our initialization strategy, while establishing that the \emph{Differentiable Rational Feature} regime is necessary to close the representational compactness gap. Theoretical analysis and empirical results demonstrate that Rational Transductors solve the ``Regular Gap,'' enabling robust length generalization on algorithmic tasks where standard Transformers fail, without the sequential computational bottlenecks of traditional RNNs.

replace On the Dynamics of Delayed Generalization in Linear Models

Authors: Atreya Vedantam, Nataraj Das, Chandrashekar Lakshminarayanan

Abstract: Delayed generalization, where training accuracy becomes nearly perfect substantially before test accuracy, has often been studied in the context of the depth and compositional structure of deep networks. In this paper, we ask whether delayed generalization can occur in simpler settings. We show that it can arise in a linear model trained with gradient descent and an exponential-tail loss on linearly separable data. We characterize the finite-time hyperplane dynamics into three phases: a population phase, a support vector dominated unlearning phase and a generalization phase. The convergence of the residual beyond the maximum-margin direction is sufficient for the unlearning phase to begin. In the support vector dominated phase, we characterize the hyperplane dynamics. The time scale in these dynamics causes delayed generalization. We validate our theory through controlled experiments varying population and support-vector distributions, and further verify the predicted bias behavior in a deep model with a single output neuron on MNIST. Our results provide a unified characterization of the asymptotic and finite-time dynamics underlying delayed generalization in linear models.

Authors: Yunhui Liu, Pengyu Qiu, Yu Xing, Yongchao Liu, Peng Du, Chuntao Hong, Jiajun Zheng, Tao Zheng, Tieke He

Abstract: Attributed Graph Clustering (AGC) is a fundamental unsupervised task that integrates structural topology and node attributes to uncover latent patterns in graph-structured data. Despite its significance in industrial applications such as fraud detection and user segmentation, a significant chasm persists between academic research and real-world deployment. Current evaluation protocols suffer from the small-scale, high-homophily citation datasets, non-scalable full-batch training paradigms, and a reliance on supervised metrics that fail to reflect performance in label-scarce environments. To bridge these gaps, we present PyAGC, a comprehensive, production-ready benchmark and library designed to stress-test AGC methods across diverse scales and structural properties. We unify existing methodologies into a modular Encode-Cluster-Optimize framework and, for the first time, provide memory-efficient, mini-batch implementations for a wide array of state-of-the-art AGC algorithms. Our benchmark curates 12 diverse datasets, ranging from 2.7K to 111M nodes, specifically incorporating industrial graphs with complex tabular features and low homophily. Furthermore, we advocate for a holistic evaluation protocol that mandates unsupervised structural metrics and efficiency profiling alongside traditional supervised metrics. Battle-tested in high-stakes industrial workflows at Ant Group, this benchmark offers the community a robust, reproducible, and scalable platform to advance AGC research towards realistic deployment. The code and resources are publicly available via GitHub (https://github.com/Cloudy1225/PyAGC), PyPI (https://pypi.org/project/pyagc), and Documentation (https://pyagc.readthedocs.io). replace ANCRe: Adaptive Neural Connection Reassignment for Efficient Depth Scaling

Authors: Yilang Zhang, Bingcong Li, Niao He, Georgios B. Giannakis

Abstract: Scaling network depth has been a central driver behind the success of modern foundation models, yet recent investigations suggest that deep layers are often underutilized. This paper revisits the default mechanism for deepening neural networks, namely residual connections, from an optimization perspective. Rigorous analysis proves that the layout of residual connections can fundamentally shape convergence behavior, and even induces an exponential gap in convergence rates. Prompted by this insight, we introduce adaptive neural connection reassignment (ANCRe), a principled and lightweight framework that parameterizes and learns residual connectivities from the data.
